Low-Frequency Theta Waves Organize Frontal Cortex Cognition

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A neurophysiology investigation from The Picower Institute for Learning and Memory at MIT reveals that slow-moving theta rhythms (4–10 Hz) act as the overarching organizational framework for cognition across the frontal cortex. In a study published in Cell Reports, researchers led by Earl K. Miller and Matthew Broschard recorded multi-site neural spiking alongside local field potentials across three frontal regions during a complex visual categorization game.

The team demonstrated that theta oscillations dynamically lock task-relevant neural spikes to specific wave phases, direct long-range communication between cortical areas, and coordinate a cross-frequency hierarchy: theta phase modulates beta amplitude, which in turn gates local gamma bursts.

Source: Picower Institute for Learning and Memory / MIT / Cell Reports

To solve problems, hold working memories, and adapt to changing environments, the primate brain must coordinate the activity of billions of neurons distributed across diverse cortical areas. Yet, how the central nervous system establishes a flexible, coherent organizational infrastructure, ensuring that information flows to the right region at the exact millisecond it is needed, has remained one of systems neuroscience’s most profound questions.

While local circuits process sensory inputs, motor plans, and abstract rules, cognition requires top-down coordination that can be reconfigured dynamically as task demands change.

In a study published in Cell Reports, neuroscientists at The Picower Institute for Learning and Memory at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT) deliver direct evidence that low-frequency theta waves (4–10 Hz) serve as the master organizers of the frontal cortex.

Led by postdoctoral fellow Matthew Broschard and senior author Earl K. Miller, Picower Professor of Neuroscience, the research team discovered that theta rhythms act as a long-range operational scaffold, timing individual neural firing, routing feedback loops across distant cortical regions, and orchestrating faster beta and gamma rhythms.

To observe this dynamic coordination, the researchers recorded electrical activity across three interconnected cortical regions: two distinct subregions of the lateral prefrontal cortex (PFC)—the executive center of the brain—and the frontal eye fields (FEF), which govern visual attention and saccadic eye movements.

Animals engaged in an abstract visual categorization game, viewing varying dot patterns on a monitor and classifying them into distinct conceptual categories while maintaining gaze fixation. The researchers simultaneously tracked the electrical action potentials of hundreds of individual neurons alongside the collective, synchronized local field potential oscillations across multiple frequency bands.

The data revealed that slow theta waves act as a master infrastructure, coordinating activity across the frontal lobes much like a regional radar grid.

“Theta seems to be this master organizer,” explained senior author Earl K. Miller. “Imagine that local brain activity supporting cognition is like individual airport control towers. Theta is the whole air traffic control zone covering hundreds of miles including many airports. It’s the overall infrastructure that ties the system together and organizes it.”

Neural spiking in all three frontal areas encoded specific features of the task, including stimulus properties, category identity, and motor selection. However, in the frontal eye fields, this information was tightly modulated by the precise phase of the ongoing theta cycle.

At the exact moment when a category decision was made, spiking activity carrying information about the chosen category peaked sharply during the falling phase of the theta wave and reached its trough during the rising phase. When the animal received feedback indicating whether the choice was correct or incorrect, that outcome signal was similarly bound to a specific theta phase window.

“At different times during the task, different information is important,” Miller noted. “The information that’s being synchronized to the theta waves is whatever is relevant at the moment. It’s pretty compelling evidence that theta is playing a real functional role.”

To determine how brain areas influence one another, the team applied Granger Causality—a mathematical framework that evaluates whether electrical activity in one cortical node can predict subsequent activity in another.

The directionality of theta-band signaling shifted dynamically based on task phase and behavioral success:

“An important result was that theta’s role in organizing brain activity changed rapidly and depended on what the animal was immediately doing,” said lead author Matthew Broschard. “This sort of flexibility is essential for cognition. We constantly need to update what we are attending to and what we are thinking about. These theta waves may be important for rapidly reconfiguring brain activity so that we jump from one thing to the next.”

The investigation also mapped the nested relationship connecting slow and fast neural rhythms. Previous landmark research from the Miller laboratory established that mid-frequency beta waves (15–30 Hz) regulate high-frequency gamma bursts (40–90 Hz), effectively acting as an inhibitory gatekeeper that determines when and where local neural circuits process information.

The new findings extend this model into a comprehensive cross-frequency hierarchy: while beta controls gamma locally, theta orchestrates beta across long cortical distances.

Cross-frequency phase-amplitude coupling analysis demonstrated that the phase of slow theta waves directly modulated the amplitude of beta oscillations, which subsequently governed the timing and amplitude of gamma bursts.

Because theta rhythms oscillate at a relatively slow 4–10 cycles per second, their long wavelengths can travel over centimeters of cortical tissue, creating an expansive temporal window within which faster, localized cognitive computations can be coordinated. By tying phase-locked spiking, directional feedback loops, and nested cross-frequency coupling into a single operational architecture, the study shows that low-frequency theta waves provide the dynamic infrastructure required for flexible, goal-directed cognition.

What role do theta waves play in cognitive processing in the frontal cortex?

Theta waves (4–10 Hz) act as a master organizer across the frontal cortex. They coordinate when individual neurons fire, govern long-range communication between distant brain regions, and direct higher-frequency beta and gamma waves that process local information.

How does theta phase coupling affect decision-making?

In the frontal eye fields (FEF), task-relevant information synchronizes to specific phases of the theta oscillation. Spiking that encodes category choices peaks during the falling phase of the theta wave and drops during the rising phase, ensuring that critical information is processed during optimal excitability windows.

What happens to theta communication when the brain makes an error?

During decision-making and after correct choices, theta influence flows from prefrontal executive regions back toward the frontal eye fields. However, when an error occurs, this directional flow flips, traveling forward from the FEF to the prefrontal cortex to trigger rapid cognitive and behavioral updating.

Funding: The Freedom Together Foundation, the Army Research Office, the National Institutes of Health, the Office of Naval Research, and the Simons Center for the Social Brain provided support for the research.

Theta-based organization of information in the frontal cortex

Theta (4–10 Hz) oscillations seem to coordinate activity across long-range distances. It is less clear how theta coordinates activity more locally within frontal subareas. We examined activity in the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (dlPFC), ventrolateral prefrontal cortex (vlPFC), and frontal eye fields (FEFs) as non-human primates categorized dot patterns.

We characterized how theta coordinates activity within frontal cortex. First, theta phase selectively couples to behaviorally relevant information in spiking. Second, theta influences move in a posterior direction during choices (i.e., dlPFC/vlPFC to FEF) and after correct outcomes (i.e., dlPFC to FEF), but not after errors. Instead, anterior-direction (i.e., FEF to vlPFC) theta influences are stronger after errors. Third, theta participates in behaviorally associated nested cross-frequency interactions.

Theta couples to beta (15–30 Hz) oscillations during choices and after errors, whereas beta couples to gamma (40–90 Hz) oscillations after correct outcomes. Our results position theta as a critical mechanism that flexibly and dynamically coordinates activity in frontal cortex.

Students Want More Than Degrees. Community Colleges Now Have to Prove Economic Value

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Community colleges are rethinking student success by focusing on living-wage jobs and higher earnings.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Students are increasingly looking beyond earning a college degree or credential. They want to know whether their education will lead to meaningful employment, better earnings, and long-term career opportunities. As expectations shift toward tangible career outcomes, community colleges are facing growing pressure to demonstrate the value of the programs they offer.

A July 2026 report from the Aspen Institute and Columbia University’s Community College Research Center highlights the challenge. According to the research, more than one-third of community college credentials are unlikely to lead directly to living-wage jobs or bachelor’s degrees that provide pathways to such employment. This raises an important question: Are colleges preparing students simply to complete academic programs, or to succeed in the workforce?

Some institutions are already rethinking their approach. Through the Unlocking Opportunity initiative, launched in 2022, 10 community colleges across the United States have been working to align academic programs more closely with employment opportunities and pathways to further education. Between 2022 and 2025, these institutions moved more than 20,000 additional students into programs associated with stronger career and transfer outcomes, representing a 27% increase in enrollment in high-value pathways.

These developments point to a shift in how community colleges are defining student success, placing greater emphasis on the career opportunities and economic mobility that education can create.

As students increasingly prioritize career outcomes, community colleges are reassessing whether their academic programs reflect labor market needs. Rather than relying solely on enrollment demand or existing course offerings, institutions are using employment data, regional workforce trends, and employer partnerships to identify programs that can lead to stronger career opportunities.

This approach is particularly important in sectors facing workforce shortages, where colleges can play a direct role in preparing students for in-demand occupations. By collaborating with employers, institutions can better understand the skills required for specific roles, adapt their curricula, and expand access to practical training opportunities.

At San Antonio College in Texas, this strategy has translated into measurable enrollment growth. To address a regional nursing shortage, the college partnered with healthcare employers to expand clinical training placements, a major constraint on nursing program capacity. According to the Aspen Institute’s July 2026 report, these efforts helped increase enrollment in its selective nursing programs by 71% over three years.

Similarly, Southwest Wisconsin Technical College identified a shortage of radiologic technologists in its region and introduced a radiography program to address the gap. Local hospitals supported the initiative by donating equipment, while the college converted existing space into a training laboratory. The program filled its available places upon launch, demonstrating student demand for training connected to regional employment opportunities.

These examples show how community colleges are taking a more active role in workforce development. Instead of simply offering programs and leaving students to navigate the job market after graduation, institutions are collaborating with employers to create clearer pathways into in-demand occupations. While the long-term employment outcomes of these initiatives are still being evaluated, their early results demonstrate how aligning academic offerings with workforce needs can expand access to career-oriented education.

As the value of higher education comes under greater scrutiny, community colleges are facing a difficult question: Should they continue offering programs that attract students but provide limited opportunities after graduation? Enrollment and completion rates remain important indicators of institutional performance, but they do not necessarily reflect the economic value of a credential.

This is prompting a closer examination of how academic programs align with employment opportunities, graduate earnings, and pathways to further education. Rather than evaluating programs solely on student demand, colleges can use labor market information and graduate outcomes to determine which offerings need to be updated, expanded, or reconsidered.

South Puget Sound Community College in Washington offers a recent example. In 2026, the college’s board voted to close its Paralegal, Culinary Arts, and Baking & Pastry Arts programs following a review of regional employment demand, career outcomes, and financial sustainability. College officials found that many local culinary and baking positions did not require formal qualifications or offer significant wage advantages to credential holders. The decision was also driven by budgetary pressures, with the two food-related programs costing the institution approximately $618,000 annually.

However, measuring program value is not always straightforward. Some credentials prepare students for immediate employment, while others provide foundational education for transfer to four-year institutions. A program with modest short-term earnings may still offer significant long-term value if it helps students pursue advanced qualifications or enter occupations with stronger career progression.

For community colleges, the challenge is not simply to eliminate programs with weaker employment outcomes. It is to ensure that students understand where different educational pathways can lead and that institutions regularly assess whether their offerings deliver on those expectations.

Helping students enter programs with strong career prospects requires more than offering the right courses. Community colleges are increasingly integrating career planning into academic advising to help students understand employment opportunities, potential earnings, and the qualifications needed for their chosen careers.

A 2024 report from the Center for Community College Student Engagement, based on responses from 83,189 students across 199 community colleges, found that more than 90% had chosen a career path, yet many lacked essential information about how to pursue it.

To address this gap, Lane Community College in Oregon uses a Guided Pathways framework to help students explore career options, select programs, and develop academic plans. Technology, including AI-supported advising tools, can further improve access to personalized guidance and help advisors identify students who need additional support.

By connecting academic decisions with career goals earlier, community colleges can help students make more informed choices about their education and future employment.

The path forward requires community colleges to turn these strategies into measurable results. Stronger employer partnerships, regular program evaluations, and proactive career advising must become integral to how institutions operate, rather than isolated initiatives.

The challenge now is to ensure that these changes translate into better employment prospects, higher earnings, and smoother transitions to further education. Ultimately, the institutions that succeed will be those that can demonstrate the economic value of their programs and consistently deliver on the career opportunities they promise students.

Students are increasingly looking beyond earning a college degree or credential. They want to know whether their education will lead to meaningful employment, better earnings, and long-term career opportunities. As expectations shift toward tangible career outcomes, community colleges are facing growing pressure to demonstrate the value of the programs they offer.

A July 2026 report from the Aspen Institute and Columbia University’s Community College Research Center highlights the challenge. According to the research, more than one-third of community college credentials are unlikely to lead directly to living-wage jobs or bachelor’s degrees that provide pathways to such employment. This raises an important question: Are colleges preparing students simply to complete academic programs, or to succeed in the workforce?

Some institutions are already rethinking their approach. Through the Unlocking Opportunity initiative, launched in 2022, 10 community colleges across the United States have been working to align academic programs more closely with employment opportunities and pathways to further education. Between 2022 and 2025, these institutions moved more than 20,000 additional students into programs associated with stronger career and transfer outcomes, representing a 27% increase in enrollment in high-value pathways.

AI Is Making Expertise Cheaper. Here’s What Will Make You More Valuable.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

When customers can access sophisticated knowledge through AI, entrepreneurs need to rethink what makes their expertise valuable.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

For decades, being an expert was a competitive advantage. You spent years developing knowledge, understanding an industry and accumulating experience that other people didn’t have. Customers paid for access to that expertise because acquiring it themselves was difficult, expensive or time-consuming.

Today, a business owner can ask AI to analyze a market, explain a financial concept or develop a marketing strategy, receiving information that previously might have required a specialist.

Knowing all the answers used to make you the smartest person in the room. Today, it might just make you the most expensive search engine. The uncomfortable question for entrepreneurs who sell expertise is this: If customers can access much of what you know, what exactly are they paying you for?

This isn’t simply a prediction about where AI might take us. We’re already seeing how knowledge-based work is changing. Microsoft’s 2025 Work Trend Index found that 81% of leaders expected AI agents to be moderately or extensively integrated into their companies’ AI strategies within 12–18 months. Meanwhile, the World Economic Forum’s Future of Jobs Report 2025 identified analytical thinking as the most important core skill for employers, with creative thinking, resilience and technological literacy also featuring prominently.

These findings point toward an important distinction. Technology is becoming increasingly capable of producing information and completing tasks, but businesses still need people who can evaluate that information, connect it to commercial realities and decide what should happen next.

Consider a consultant who has spent 20 years becoming an expert in a particular industry. That knowledge hasn’t suddenly become worthless, but if much of their value proposition involves explaining information that AI can now provide in seconds, customers may reasonably question what they’re paying for. The premium is shifting from possessing knowledge to applying it.

I founded my international public relations agency, NettResults, in 1999. At the time, one of our advantages was having specialist knowledge of the Middle East technology market. International companies entering the region needed to understand the media landscape, local business practices, cultural differences and how to communicate effectively with unfamiliar audiences. We had that knowledge, along with relationships that took years to develop. Clients couldn’t simply search for much of the information or replicate those relationships overnight.

Today, AI can explain the regional media landscape, identify publications, research competitors and help develop communications strategies in minutes. Information that once took considerable time and experience to assemble is increasingly accessible, but that doesn’t mean our expertise has become less valuable. Knowing which publications cover technology is useful. Knowing which journalist is likely to care about a particular story, how regional sensitivities might affect its reception and whether the story should be pitched at all requires something different. It requires experience, relationships, context and judgment — and those are much harder to automate.

One limitation of traditional expertise is that we tend to organize it into disciplines. Marketing people understand marketing, financial people understand finance, and technology specialists understand technology. But business problems rarely arrive neatly labeled, and solving them often requires looking beyond the area where the symptoms first appear.

I recently worked with a business owner who believed he needed to hire more people because his company was struggling to keep up with demand. On the surface, it was a staffing problem. But as we examined the business, we discovered that inefficient processes, unclear responsibilities and the owner’s involvement in too many everyday decisions were creating much of the pressure. Hiring more employees might have increased capacity, but it wouldn’t necessarily have solved the underlying problem. The better approach was to examine workflows, clarify accountability and identify where technology could remove unnecessary work before adding more people.

What initially looked like a hiring decision was actually a combination of operational design, leadership and technology. AI can help analyze each of those areas, but the entrepreneur’s opportunity is to connect them, recognize which information matters and make decisions that account for the whole business. The business owner who understands enough about technology, psychology, finance, operations and customer behavior to connect those disciplines may create more value than someone who knows everything about just one of them.

That doesn’t mean specialists are becoming unnecessary. Deep expertise remains essential, particularly when decisions carry significant financial, legal or operational consequences. But expertise without the ability to apply it in context becomes easier to commoditize.

If your business sells knowledge, advice or specialist services, I recommend examining how your value proposition is changing. This doesn’t require an expensive consultant or a six-month strategic review. Start by asking four questions about what your customers actually buy from you and where you create value that they cannot easily reproduce themselves.

1. What do customers currently pay us to know? Identify the specialist knowledge, information and capabilities that underpin your products or services.

2. What could AI now provide them directly? Be realistic about which parts of your offering are becoming easier, faster or cheaper for customers to access independently.

3. Where does our judgment create value? Look for the decisions, relationships, interpretation and experience that produce better outcomes than information alone.

4. How should we reposition what we sell? Consider whether you should be charging for access to knowledge, time spent delivering it or the commercial outcomes your expertise helps customers achieve.

This exercise isn’t about abandoning your expertise. It’s about making sure you’re not building tomorrow’s business around yesterday’s competitive advantage.

For entrepreneurs, the opportunity isn’t to compete with AI on how much information we can produce. That’s a competition we’re unlikely to win. Instead, we need to become better at asking the right questions, connecting information across disciplines and making decisions that create measurable value for customers.

AI can increasingly tell people what is possible. Experienced entrepreneurs and professionals help determine what is sensible, commercially viable and worth doing. The future doesn’t belong to people who simply know the most. It belongs to those who know what matters, what to do about it and are prepared to take responsibility for the result.

For decades, being an expert was a competitive advantage. You spent years developing knowledge, understanding an industry and accumulating experience that other people didn’t have. Customers paid for access to that expertise because acquiring it themselves was difficult, expensive or time-consuming.

Today, a business owner can ask AI to analyze a market, explain a financial concept or develop a marketing strategy, receiving information that previously might have required a specialist.

Knowing all the answers used to make you the smartest person in the room. Today, it might just make you the most expensive search engine. The uncomfortable question for entrepreneurs who sell expertise is this: If customers can access much of what you know, what exactly are they paying you for?

Guide: Cyberpunk 2077: Best Mods on PS5

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

What are the best Cyberpunk 2077 mods on PS5? In this guide, I'm going to highlight which mods I think are well worth installing to make your dark future experience more enticing.

On this page: Cyberpunk 2077: Best Mods on PS5

Mod support for Cyberpunk 2077 was added with Patch 2.5 on PS5, allowing for user-created content on Sony's console.

Choosing and installing mods is easy: just click the 'Mods' option on the game's main menu and you'll be taken to the current list of available mods. You can scroll through pages manually or you can use the search bar.

If you find a mod you like, select it from the list and then click the 'Install' button, or just press the Options button on your controller to install it right there and then.

You can also hit the 'Like' button to add the mod to your 'Liked' list. Doing this lets you keep track of your favourite mods via the 'Liked' filter button new the top of the screen.

You can have a maximum of 150 mods installed at any one time, and said mods need to have a combined file size of 15.76GB or under (although this isn't really an issue as of yet, as standard mod sizes tend to be very, very small).

Unfortunately, yes, using any mods disables Trophies when playing Cyberpunk 2077 on PS5.

This counts for any save file that has ever been used in conjunction with mods.

As such, you may want to beat the game at least once, or go all the way and get the Platinum, before diving into the world of mods.

Below, you'll find a list of what I think are currently the best Cyberpunk 2077 mods available on PS5.

Admittedly, the selection is far from vast at this point in time — but new mods are being added on what seems to be a weekly basis, and so this list should only grow over the coming months.

A mod that's bound to be at or near the top of Cyberpunk 2077's modding menu for years to come, Deceptious Quest Core is a necessary framework mod for a number of Deceptious' other mods.

Basically, you need to have this mod installed if you want a few of the other mods featured on this list — it's just the way that the game's quest-based modding works.

Any mod that has [DQC] in its title requires Quest Core to function.

Note that you need to enter V's H10 apartment (and then possibly exit it) for this mod to activate, and subsequently activate all of the [DQC] mods.

This mod aims to fix a surprisingly long list of bugs that are still found throughout Cyberpunk 2077.

The vast majority of these bugs are minor and only affect very small aspects of the overall game. We're talking things like slight animation glitches, specific audio mixing (those annoyingly quiet, and sometimes annoyingly loud TVs), and quest-related oddities.

Still, it's a mod worth installing when you start adding all of these fixes up — especially if you plan on starting a new playthrough.

This mod allows Johnny to appear in all of V's apartments, not just their starting home in the H10 megabuilding.

If you enjoy Johnny's company, this mod's good for immersion purposes. He'll lounge around the apartments, and add to the ambience by playing his guitar when V asks him to.

One of my favourite mods on PS5 so far, Weather Probability Rebalance does exactly what its name suggests, adjusting the chances of different weather patterns appearing as you carve out a life in Night City (and its surrounding wastelands).

For those who have already spent tens or even hundreds of hours playing Cyberpunk 2077, you may have noticed that the weather can be a bit... boring. The whole atmosphere of the city can change when it's raining, or when there's a sandstorm — but it can feel like it just doesn't happen often enough.

According to modder RMK_, the game's weather system is actually a little funky. In short, sunny weather can become 'stuck' under certain circumstances, and so this mod attempts to fix the problem.

As a result, adverse weather is much more common and lasts longer, allowing you to soak up the vibes.

A great mod for immersion purposes, Night City Interactions lets you go out drinking at various locations across the map — but the best part is that you can invite other characters, like Panam, Judy, River, and Kerry, to come along with you.

As a bonus, this mod also includes a Cyberpunk Edgerunners tour quest. As you may know, the anime features a number of backdrops that are based on actual locations from the game, and you can check these specific places out at your leisure.

Note that you need to enter V's H10 apartment (and then possibly exit) before this mod activates.

Three small mods that add just an extra touch of immersion to ripperdoc visits.

Instead of jumping straight to the cyberware menu upon talking to a ripper, V will actually sit down in the clinic's chair before undergoing surgery.

The Dogtown mod does the same for rippers in the Dogtown region of the map, while the Vic-specific mod uses Vic's existing animations from early in the game.

These are mods that, when used in conjunction with other immersion-focused mods, add up to a more detailed experience.

Another two mods that bring just a little more immersion to your time in Night City.

They let you sit down at the game's various bars and actually take a fully-animated drink. You can even select the kind of drink that you want from a dialogue menu.

What's more, the drinks give the same kind of temporary stat buffs that you get from consuming drinks in your inventory. A quick pre-gig bar crawl never hurt anyone!

Just like the Immersive Bartenders mods, this one lets you grab a fully-animated snack or drink from the numerous food vendors found across Night City.

Stat buffs are applied as if you were using items from your inventory, so it actually makes food stalls worth visiting if you're gearing up for some action.

This mod improves the readability of shards, which can be difficult to properly parse — especially if you're in a rush.

Basically, Readable Shards adjusts the text's formatting, making it slightly bigger and adding coloured names to the people who actually wrote the conversations in the first place.

This is a small mod but it can be a surprisingly big difference, particularly if you're a sucker for reading every bit of lore that you find.

A great little mod that adds a security camera system to NCARTS (metros).

By pressing left and right on the d-pad, you can switch to the cameras while you're riding the train, allowing you to view four new perspectives: two cameras shows the length of the carriage that you're on from either end, while the other two shows the outside of the metro from the front and back.

If you want to see more of Night City during your NCART travels, then this mod's an easy recommendation.

If you're absolutely sick of El Capitan giving you unreasonable time limits during his dynamic car-jacking gigs, then this mod's for you.

It simply increases said time limits by 25%, which usually equates to much easier missions. It also means that you can better enjoy the more combat-focused gigs without having to race ahead.

What are your favourite Cyberpunk 2077 mods on PS5? Hand out some recommendations in the comments section below.

A little too much, some might say.

7 Ways to Stretch Your Days Between Showers Without Becoming a Walking Biohazard

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The pages of Backpacker are a safe space for those of us whose on-trail routines fall outside the normal boundaries of hygiene. But I’ll be the first to admit I’ve pushed those limits, even by the backpacking community’s loose standards. I’ve taken my hair out of a 10-day ponytail and had it stay in the shape of a ponytail, gotten a comb so stuck I had to break the teeth to get it out, and watched in wonder as my socks, coated in several days of sweat and grime, stood up unsupported after I took them off.

We might not follow the standard showering protocol when we’re living in the woods, but there are a few ways to mitigate the horror that those around you endure during your next extended stretch between town days without packing a four-pound hygiene kit.

Your hair isn’t greasy, but your scalp sure is. If you’re planning on going a long time between hair washes, do everything in your power to keep scalp oil from dragging through your hair. This is where a Buff, headband, or hat comes in handy. The less your hair is falling in your face, the less you’re going to keep swiping at it, moving the oils and grime from your hands and scalp through the strands.

It seems counterintuitive, but if your goal is being Less Gross, you should brush your hair less frequently, not more. You can do this by braiding long hair, or pulling it back into a combination ponytail/braid. This pulls double duty, preventing (some) tangles while keeping your hands away from it. I opt for a tight French braid to tame flyaways, then throw a headband or Buff over the top to keep it in place. I’ve kept this braid in place for more than a week at a time, and while it wasn’t pretty when I finally brushed it, I was less greasy than I would have been if I’d been messing with it every day.

Wet wipes are a luxury item for discerning ultralighters, but for the rest of us, they’re the next best thing to a shower. Choose your variety based on your needs. Standard wet wipes will do the trick for most hikers thanks to their combination of cleanser, moisturizer, and antibacterial agents.

You can also choose a backcountry-specific option like Pact—each wipe is the size of a bottle cap and hydrates with a few drops of water. Sea to Summit’s outdoor wipes are also gentler on your face than standard Wet Wipes. (Caveat: If you have sensitive skin, disregard everything I just said and ask your dermatologist for the best option for avoiding backcountry breakouts.)

For most people, the strongest odor buildup is under the arms and around the groin area. Both places have a high concentration of sweat glands, folds of skin that stay warm and trap moisture, and natural bacteria (healthy ones!) that help break down oils. Keeping these body parts wiped down and freshened up at the end of each day will help prevent dirty skin from chafing and keep those stronger odors at bay.

Just be careful not to upset your body’s natural pH, and if you have concerns about scented wipes or their ingredients, err on the side of caution and keep them away from truly sensitive body parts. I’m a huge fan of the antimicrobial Kula Cloth instead of the drip-dry method, and I probably don’t have to explain why that’s a good hygiene choice.

Slapping a frozen burrito onto the gas station counter with dirt-blackened fingernails is pretty gross, but the space under those unkempt nails doesn’t just look yucky: It’s a prime spot for dead skin cells, dirt, and oil to stay trapped. None of us are washing our hands in the woods as frequently as we do at home, and limiting the chance for bacteria by keeping your nails short makes a huge difference. Toes are the same story, but add in an increased risk of fungal infections and ingrown nails.

There are plenty of valid points on both sides of the synthetic-versus-natural-fibers argument, but when it comes to odor resistance, natural fibers are the runaway winner. Synthetic materials like nylon or polyester bind to skin oils, creating a breeding-and-feeding ground for bacteria, which turn trapped sweat into clinging, stinky odors. Natural fibers, on the other hand, regulate your microclimate (the space between your skin and clothes) better and release moisture rather than trapping it in the fibers.

Both materials are wicking, but natural fibers have antimicrobial properties that greatly reduce the growth of those stinky microorganisms. If odor prevention is your goal, opt for a lightweight merino/bamboo blend for your hiking top, natural fibers for your undergarments, and merino or alpaca for hiking midlayers. Avoid clothes made primarily from polyester or nylon, though almost everything will have some percentage of synthetic fibers for durability.

On a shorter trip or cold-weather outing where I’m trying to save pack weight, I’ll occasionally wear my camp pants for chilly early-morning miles, or wear my hiking midlayer to sleep in. But for longer trips where your hiking clothes (and yourself) are going to be fighting the biohazard warning sign, keep your camp clothes for camp and your hiking clothes for your daily miles.

You can keep those camp clothes even cleaner by using your wet wipe ration to wipe your body and feet thoroughly before putting them on, which will also help keep oils and grime out of your sleeping bag liner.

How to Avoid Sports Injuries in Your 30s, 40s, and Beyond

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

At 38 years old, I’ve been fortunate enough to go most of my life without being bothered by sports injuries, despite being consistently and competitively active for the better part of three decades. On the soccer field, I’ve played on and off at club level since primary school. (I’m currently holding it down as a winger on my local club’s 30+ “seniors” team.)

Meanwhile, as a health journalist and all-around enthusiastic exerciser, I’ve thrown myself at countless physical challenges—mostly for fun, sometimes to help pay the rent. Marathons, trail races, triathlons, 24-hour relays, mud runs, obstacle course races, Hyrox—you name it, I was probably obsessed with it for a time. And through it all, the only injury I ever incurred was a torn meniscus (the undignified result of attempting a wildly above-my-paygrade basketball dunk on a charitably lowered rim, at a promotional event). If only the surgeon could have extracted the memory of that day along with the frayed connective tissue he removed from my knee. I’m pretty sure I was wearing jeans, too.

All of this to say, everything was smooth sailing for a very long time, until it suddenly wasn’t. Somewhere around the age of 35, for reasons beyond my comprehension at the time, the wheels started to come off the bus. First, a stress fracture to my foot. I noticed it one night in bed and couldn’t tell you when it happened. Regardless, it put me in a boot for three months.

Next, a torn hamstring during a routine game of pick-up football, making the same run I’d made so many times before. I thought I’d just pulled it, so I kept playing, which of course only made it worse. That decision cost me six months. Then a strained calf muscle at a gentle session of run club. And, finally, shin splints. I don’t even remember how many months that one took me out for, but it felt like forever.

This barrage of sports injuries—all sustained within a roughly two-year period—was unrelenting, each overlapping pang seemingly passing the baton to the next. Needless to say, I was miserable. And the worst part was that I couldn’t understand why this was happening to me. I had been maintaining the same activity level, same diet, same supplements, and the same sleeping habits.

You always hear people talking half-jokingly about how getting older takes its toll on the body. But nothing really prepares you for that moment when you start to actually experience it for yourself. It’s existential. Brutal. Depressing, honestly. Sometimes the best way to deal with these spiral-inducing moments is to simply talk to other people dealing with the same thing. So I recently asked my, er, seasoned soccer team’s group chat: What do you do to avoid and manage sports injuries as you get older?

Kevin, 40, chimes in first: “There’s no avoiding, mate. Just managing.”

“Funnily enough, I’ve actually started doing yoga, which has been great for mobility and stretching out my lower back,” writes Chris, 35, who battles with a recurring quadratus lumborum (QL) issue. “I was always a bit hesitant to give it a go—not because I didn't think it would help, but because I couldn't get past the fact that I'd essentially be following a similar exercise plan to my mum! I think that’s been one of the bigger adjustments as I’ve gotten older: accepting that looking after your body sometimes means doing things you might have previously associated with an entirely different generation.”

“For me, I think the most important thing is to keep going in general,” adds Brian, who recently turned 42. “I remember when I was around 20 years old, my manager at the time, who was in his late 30s and retired from playing, was doing some running on his own before the team training session started. The lads were joking and teasing him about making a comeback, but I remember him saying, ‘If you stop moving at my age, you won't be able to start again.’ I think that’s good advice.”

In some way, I suppose it’s comforting to know that this is something we all have to deal with, whether you’re a Sunday league bench warmer or in contention for the Ballon d’Or. "With age, there’s just a higher incidence of soft-tissue injuries as your muscles lose strength and elasticity,” says Dr. Brian Forsythe, a sports medicine surgeon, associate professor at Rush University Medical Center, and recreational soccer player himself. According to Forsythe, who also serves as head orthopedic officer for MLS team Chicago Fire FC and president of the Professional Soccer Physicians Society, over time athletes across the skill spectrum will ultimately begin running into the same injuries.

You may not be able to halt the inevitable march of time, but there are certainly ways to minimize and slow its toll on your body. This requires some work but could ultimately be the difference between hanging up your boots and kicking on for another 10, 20, or even 30 years.

“It's up to you to take some responsibility,” Forsythe says. “Think about the lifestyle you had when you were 17 or 18—how you took care of your body and what you looked like. Then look in the mirror and ask yourself, ‘How much of my life now resembles what it was then?’ If you’re going out and having a few pints, understand that that inhibits your recovery and decreases protein synthesis. If you’re overweight, that has effects beyond just not looking great in your clothes. It’s increased joint load, increased rotational torque. You’re more likely to get injured. So you have to set realistic expectations of what your body is capable of doing. But you can definitely still do a lot.”

If you’re not ready to call time just yet, here are a couple of strategies you can start implementing today, courtesy of Forsythe and a handful of other experts.

“Number one, and it should be obvious, is just general conditioning,” says Forsythe. Being a slightly less sedentary human being during the work week means you won’t send your body into total shock when you decide to get going over the weekend.

“A lot of injuries come from that mismatch between what we do all week and the intensity we demand from our bodies on the weekend,” says sports medicine physician Dr. Alex McDonald. As Dr. James B. Chen, orthopedic surgeon at DISC Sports and Spine Center, puts it: “The biggest risk isn't necessarily your age, but your schedule.”

“When you think about playing in your teens and twenties, you were probably close to your ideal body weight,” says Forsythe. Ideal body weight, or IBW, refers to the weight at which your body is able to function most efficiently and without a heightened risk of injury. “All of the forces across your joints are multiplied almost exponentially with additional pounds on your frame,” says Forsythe. For example, “the force across your knee can be up to six times your body weight.”

“If you've ever been overweight and you climb up and down stairs and notice that the front of your knee hurts, that's because of those increased pressures and forces,” adds Forsythe.

Jogging a couple of miles a week may help you get through your Sunday workout our pick-up game, but it’s not doing anything to prevent in-the-moment injuries. To do that, you have to train the way you play—whatever game that is. “It's changes of direction," says Forsythe. It's rapid acceleration and deceleration. It's pivoting, twisting, turning.” Basically, bring back whatever drills you used to do on your sports team when you were younger, and adjust them for the demands of the sport you currently play.

No matter how frequently and comprehensively you train, a complete warm-up before any big athletic event is a non-negotiable. "It helps dramatically, which is why you'll always see professionals warm up for 30-45 minutes before a match,” says Forsythe. When it comes to soccer, FIFA actually has developed a warm-up program, called FIFA ll+, specifically for injury prevention. “It’s a free routine that only takes about 20 minutes, and includes running drills plus strength, balance, and landing exercises such as Nordic hamstring curls and single-leg balance work,” says McDonald. “It’s been shown to cut injuries by about a third, but it needs to be done at least twice a week—ideally three or four times a week—to really reduce the risk of injury.”

Stretching after your warm-up is important, too, but just be careful not to overdo it. “If you stretch too aggressively, you can actually predispose yourself to muscle tendon injuries,” says Forsythe. “First warm up, and then stretch out within your range of comfort. But right before a game is not the time to all of a sudden correct tight hamstrings.”

Often, being sidelined by an injury can be more painful than the injury itself, causing guys to get back out there earlier than they probably should. “A common issue that I see is when someone sustains a minor sprain or strain, and they return to sport-specific activity too early,” says Chen, “which can result in either worsening the injury or even picking up a secondary injury.” As annoying as it might be to sit out for 2 or 3 weeks with a strained hamstring, turning that into a tear will bench you for far longer. Believe me.

Playing sports in your 30s, 40s, and beyond also means coming to grips with your body’s increasing need for routine recovery between training sessions and games. "You have to recognize that your body needs more time to recover,” says Forsythe. You may have great cardiovascular fitness and feel ready to play on consecutive days, but your muscles and connective tissues need time to bounce back. “It’s important to respect fatigue, and ensure proper recovery after games or training,” says McDonald.

To be fair, this is universally sound guidance regardless of age. But it's advice that tends to take decades to sink in. “I think that one of the key advantages of playing as you get older is that you know your body better in your 30s and 40s,” Brian’s response concluded. “You understand what it's saying to you now. You might not have even listened when you were 21!”

OL : Carlos Vicens officiellement nommé pour succéder à Paulo Fonseca !

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

L'Olympique Lyonnais a officialisé ce lundi 9 novembre 2026 à 18h la nomination de Carlos Vicens au poste d'entraîneur principal. Libre depuis son licenciement du SC Braga, le technicien espagnol de 43 ans s'est engagé pour un an et demi, soit jusqu'au 30 juin 2028, avec pour objectif principal de décrocher une qualification européenne en mai prochain.

Pour relancer l'équipe, la direction de l'OL a choisi de miser sur un profil au bagage formateur reconnu. En effet, avant son expérience au Portugal, Carlos Vicens a passé huit saisons au sein de Manchester City. Vainqueur de la FA Youth Cup en 2020 avec les U18 du club anglais, l'Espagnol est ensuite devenu l'adjoint de Pep Guardiola entre 2021 et 2025. Un passage formateur au plus haut niveau.

Fort de cette riche expérience, le nouvel entraîneur lyonnais s'était ensuite lancé comme numéro un au SC Braga lors de la saison 2025-2026. Une aventure réussie avec une quatrième place en championnat portugais et une demi-finale de Ligue Europa, avant un licenciement survenu le 20 octobre 2026 après trois défaites consécutives.

Dans son communiqué, le club rhodanien explique son choix : « La direction a ainsi retenu le profil de Carlos Vicens, dont l'approche du jeu, l'exigence et l'importance accordée au développement des joueurs correspondent pleinement aux orientations définies par le club ». Cette arrivée met un terme au feuilleton de la succession sur le banc rhodanien. L'OL devait en effet trouver un remplaçant à Paulo Fonseca, récemment parti pour le Sporting CP après 83 matches dirigés à Lyon, contre une indemnité estimée à 5 millions d'euros. Durant cette période délicate, Jorge Maciel avait assuré l'intérim. Toutefois, les difficultés sportives ont rapidement ressurgi en championnat.

Après un match nul contre la Real Sociedad (1-1) en Ligue Europa, les Lyonnais se sont inclinés à Brest (0-2) dimanche 8 novembre 2026 lors de la 10e journée de Ligue 1. Une rencontre disputée en Bretagne pendant laquelle Carlos Vicens a visité les installations du club. L'Espagnol a ainsi découvert son futur cadre de travail avant de parapher son contrat de dix-huit mois.

Pour mener à bien sa mission, Carlos Vicens est accompagné par quatre adjoints, dont son assistant Mario Enguidanos (34 ans) et Lander Iza Garcia (30 ans), chargé de la performance. Dans un souci de continuité, le club a également maintenu des cadres techniques déjà en place : « Jorge Maciel conservera ses fonctions d'entraîneur adjoint, dans une volonté de continuité et avec la pleine confiance de la direction du club. Adrien Tarascon poursuivra également sa mission de responsable du développement individuel des joueurs, tandis que Sébastien Hamel, jusqu'ici entraîneur des gardiens de la Pro 2 depuis janvier 2025, rejoindra définitivement le staff. »

Le technicien espagnol n'aura que peu de temps pour prendre ses marques. Carlos Vicens dirigera sa première séance d'entraînement mardi 10 novembre au matin avant d'être présenté aux médias à 15 heures. Au-delà des présentations, le travail tactique débutera sans attendre. Le premier match officiel de Carlos Vicens avec l'OL aura lieu le samedi 21 novembre 2026 face au LOSC au stade Pierre-Mauroy lors de la 11e journée de Ligue 1.

A massive trade in Nvidia just went off – here are the details

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The biggest options trade on Nvidia stock Monday seems to think the controversy swirling around the chip leader and its embattled CEO is creating a buying opportunity.In a trade about an hour after the opening bell that involved more than $100 million in options premium, someone exchanged an existing call spread for a more bullish position that captures the next five months of trading in Nvidia.The core position was the purchase of 52,000 195-strike calls expiring Feb. 19 worth about $57 million – contracts the trader opened for a new long position while simultaneously selling the same number of 245-strike calls for the same expiry, lowering the cost of the trade by $10 million. The net position, a bullish call spread, needs Nvidia to trade above around $206 by expiry, or about 15% higher, to be profitable. If Nvidia stock reaches the short, 245-strike call, it would represent a nearly 40% rally and return the stock to its all-time high of $243, that was reached on October 6th.The trade was likely part of a "roll" down from an existing 210/255-strike call spread worth about $42 million that was closed out, according to SpotGamma analysis.

"The person got more aggressive rolling this spread down to lower strikes to get more upside exposure by getting higher delta," SpotGamma founder Brent Kochuba said by phone. "They're trading the call spread here because the vol is expensive going into earnings. It's a significant increase in upside delta exposure."Delta is options parlance for describing how quickly an options price will react to a change in the underlying stock price. The trader's updated position, taken at lower strikes, will make money faster if Nvidia rallies. In other words, it's a higher conviction trade that the chipmaker's stock will soon rebound. The 195-strike calls they bought for $57 million was the single biggest options trade on the tape Monday, according to SpotGamma. Of the top 20 options trades by dollar-amount, 12 were Nvidia, the data show.Flows around the stock generally leaned bullish by midday, with 57% of options volume tied to calls and more than $660 million of call premium likely initiated by call-buyers, SpotGamma data show. The most popular single contract by volume was the 180-strike call expiring Monday, that needs Nvidia to recover 1.2% by the bell to break even.

An Artificial CORTEX to Guide Satellite Rescue Missions

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The growing problem of satellites crowding one another is generating concern in the space community, for good reason. Almost 20,000 satellites orbit Earth now, and with more satellite constellations and other satellites being launched every year, Low-Earth Orbit (LEO) is starting to look like it's reaching its limit. Of particular concern are the 3,500+ plus satellites that are basically zombies. They are either completely non-functional or not sending any signals alerting other satellites to their presence.

A collision between two satellites could be catastrophic. Satellite constellations have some built-in redundancy which would help deal with the loss, but there's an additional risk. A collision could also create a debris cloud, multiplying the ongoing risk. The Kessler syndrome describes a cascade of collisions generated from debris created in a single, initial collision, that goes on to create even more collisions. It's like a feedback loop that keeps getting worse.

Somehow, this problem must be dealt with. The existing debris is one thing, but intact zombie satellites are another. These have lost the ability to communicate and warn other satellites of their presence. These can be repaired, and that's the subject of new research in Acta Astronautica. It's titled "CORTEX: An autonomous, end-to-end, convex-based framework for close-range rendezvous trajectory design and guidance with hardware testbed validation," and the lead author is Minduli Wijayatunga, a new aerospace engineering faculty member in The Grainger College of Engineering, at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign.

This work is all about the growing idea of Rendezvous and Proximity Operations (RPO). Spacecraft docking operations have been custom-designed solutions for individual situations, like docking with the ISS. But with thousands of zombie satellites, custom docking solutions aren't realistic.

"Rendezvous and Proximity Operations (RPO) are shifting from bespoke demonstration missions such as Northrop Grummanâs Mission Extension Vehicles, Chinaâs Tianyuanâ1, and Astroscaleâs ADRAS-J, to routine missions due to the growth of in-orbit assets and the resulting demand for inspection, servicing, assembly, and debris mitigation," the authors write. They point out that while there were only about 13,700 tracked objects in LEO in 2019, there were more than 24,000 in 2025. That's a shocking increase, and there's no signs of it slowing down.

That's forcing scientists to develop autonomous ways to deal with the overpopulation. The growing population of satellites isn't the only thing dictating an autonomous solutions. Autonomous repair is also critical because of the time delay in communications between the ground and LEO, and because satellites are best observed in and maneuvered with in windows when they're illuminated by sunlight.

âThese spacecraft must be able to track the trajectories of uncooperative objects autonomously but also make go/no-go decisions without waiting for the ground to decide,â Wijayatunga said. âSuppose your thruster fails or you encounter high-order orbital perturbations that push you away from your nominal trajectory. In those instances, how do you make those decisions?â she added.

That's what CORTEX is being designed to do.

"This paper presents CORTEX (Convex Optimization for Rendezvous Trajectory EXecution), an autonomous framework for close-range rendezvous trajectory design and guidance under sensing, actuation, and dynamics uncertainties," the authors write.

"Among RPO phases, the close-range rendezvous, where the servicer locates the docking port axes and approaches the client, is particularly safety-critical. It must satisfy strict operational constraints (e.g., keep-out zones, approach corridors, plume/line-of-sight constraints, illumination/eclipses) under limited actuation authority and imperfect relative navigation," the authors write.

Wijayatunga's PhD involved trajectory and guidance using something called convex optimization. Convex optimization is a method that lets a spacecraft approach a target using math and trajectories, not input commands. It basically solves a math problem of trajectories and thruster commands until the lone, best answer is arrived at. The answer should mean a successful docking. The idea is to reach that docking state with minimal fuel use while also following constraints like the targets no-go zones or keep-out zones. Those are places on the target satellite that might house sensitive equipment like sensors or antennae.

This work expanded to include the idea of sunlight windows. Cortex also includes options other than aborting if things went sideways. Instead of calling the docking off, CORTEX can reconsider its options. It even takes into account situations that arise where thrusters can't be fired.

âI wanted to bring that same idea into developing CORTEX and expand it, taking into consideration sunlight windows so we don't have to operate in darkness. That, and the replanning versus abort architecture, are novel in CORTEX,â Wijayatunga said.

âCORTEX has trajectory design and guidance, so if you deviate significantly from your nominal mission trajectory, it replans the trajectory and re-approaches unless you are in severe violation of your keep-out zone, in which case it returns to a passively safe orbit. It's an algorithm to recover from the unexpected. We tried instances where you're supposed to use your thrusters, but you can't for whatever reason. And CORTEX was very robust to that.â

CORTEX is being tested in a particular modelled docking scenario. It's what's called an ISAM, an In-orbit Servicing Sssembly and Manufacturing. It's designed for what will most likely be fairly typical in the future. It involves a Client Spacecraft Object (CSO) in LEO and is designed to test autonomous approach, servicing, and inspection of the CSO by a servicing spacecraft with a Guidance Navigation and Control (GNC) system for proximity operations.

Hold positions are an important part of CORTEX. In the modelled scenario, there are two of them that let the servicing spacecraft pause and re-evaluate in a fixed relative position. One is at the beginning of the fly around, and the other is at the start of the final approach. They are there to let the servicer pause during eclipses, a new idea in CORTEX.

This figure shows some aspects of CORTEX controlling a servicing spacecraft while it approaches and rendezvous with a client satellite. Blue shows the Rendezvous Sphere, orange shows the Approach Sphere, and red is the Keep Out Sphere. The approach axes is identified as CORTEX's successful solution to the docking problem. Image Credit: Wijayatunga et al. 2026. Acta Astronautica.

One way of looking at CORTEX is as a flow chart governed by four things. "The goals of CORTEX are: (i) minimize fuel and time consumption, (ii) ensure that both the fly-around and final approach occur entirely in sunlight, (iii) avoid the KOS during the fly-around phase, and (iv) satisfy the approach corridor constraint and avoid plume impingement during the final approach," the authors write.

This schematic flow-chart shows how CORTEX works. CORTEX has the ability to abort its approach if necessary, and instead of giving up, it can determine how to re-approach its client. Image Credit: Wijayatunga et al. 2026. Acta Astronautica.

In the tests, the servicing spacecraft was forced into an odd, unexpected situation and CORTEX had to deal with it. Its orbit was unexpectedly perturbed, and thruster operation was impaired, not firing when they were supposed to. Other errors were also introduced, corresponding to unexpected factors like solar storms and thrust errors.

CORTEX fared well in the tests and was able to deal with the unexpected situations in the models. "In all cases, the framework either recovered successfully while respecting safety constraints or executed a safe retreat before entering unsafe regions," the authors write.

âIn some of the hardware tests, if the servicer violates the approach corridor, it leaves the keep-out zone and retreats to a safe position and in others, the servicer is able to recover and carry on with its mission,â Wijayatunga said.

Satellite close approach and recovery isn't purely hypothetical. The Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAX) launched Astroscale's ADRAS-J in 2024. It successfully maneuvered within 50 meters of a typical piece of space debris, a spent rocket stage, considered to be an 'uncooperative target.' It flew around the object, catching multiple images from different angles. It used sensors and autonomous navigation to accomplish this.

Astroscale's ADRAS-J autonomous spacecraft approached a spent rocket booster, a typical piece of space debris, and captured images of it from multiple angles. It's a step toward eventual servicing of zombie satellites. Image Credit: Astroscale/JAXA.

The hallmark of the past few decades is rapidly-advancing technology. Among other things, it's led to a crowded LEO and a growing population of zombie satellites.

Maybe we'll end up using technological tools like CORTEX, enabled by AI and machine learning, to manage the problems technology has helped generate in the first place.

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

Massie moves to force Hegseth impeachment vote

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., took to the House floor Monday to unveil a resolution that would impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, and a vote on the issue could occur by Wednesday.

A draft resolution that Massie read aloud on the House floor on Monday accused Hegseth of eight violations of law or the Constitution, including that he violated the 1973 War Powers Resolution in his prosecution of the Iran war and acted illegally in military campaigns in Venezuela, Yemen, the Caribbean Sea and the eastern Pacific Ocean.

Massie’s resolution also charges Hegseth with disregarding protection of civilians in wartime and suppressing the free speech of a U.S. senator.

Massie announced his intention to file the resolution as a matter of the privileges of the House.

Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., will now have to rule as to whether the resolution constitutes a proper question of the privileges of the House. There is precedent from prior instances when impeachment was found to be valid as it relates to the House’s constitutionally granted powers.

He must schedule a vote to occur within two legislative days. Assuming that the speaker rules that Massie’s resolution is privileged, the House could vote on the resolution itself, or on a motion to table it or refer it to committee.

If the House were to impeach Hegseth under this process, it would mark a historical anomaly. According to the Congressional Research Service, recent attempts to impeach an official as a matter of the privileges of the House have not resulted in approval of the resolution. In cases in which an official has been impeached, the House has nearly always chosen to conduct an investigation first.

In an interview on Oct. 26, Massie put the chances of his resolution being adopted at 50-50.

In comments to reporters outside the Capitol after his floor remarks, Massie said he suspected some Republicans may back his resolution but he indicated he has neither asked for nor received any such assurances from any of them.

He predicted there could be some GOP support in the Senate if Hegseth is impeached and it goes to trial. He noted that if the trial does not start until 2027, any Republicans who might vote to convict Hegseth would presumably be joined by most if not all of a more sizeable Senate Democratic caucus.

Massie’s odds of success for his resolution may now be greater than his previous 50-50 prediction given the results of the Nov. 3 midterm elections, which gave Democrats control over both chambers in the next Congress — a vote motivated largely by opposition to the Iran war and its inflationary impact.

The questions now are, first, how many House Republicans will defy President Donald Trump and vote to impeach Hegseth; and, secondly, how many incumbents who lost their reelection bids on Nov. 3, a cohort that is disproportionately Republican, might be absent for the impeachment vote?

Massie said some Republicans who lost their reelection bids may feel freer to vote to impeach Hegseth. And he also said Republicans who were reelected and will have to face voters in 2028 may also think about how their vote on an impeachment resolution would reverberate politically.

“It’s going to be everybody’s conscience, and then they’ll have to live with that vote,” Massie said. “And they’re going to have to live with that vote two years from now if they’re not part of the retirement caucus. And if they were part of the retirement caucus, what do they have to lose?”

Asked why he did not bring articles of impeachment against President Donald Trump, Massie said: “I think the president needs to see one of — this is a metaphor — one of his horses shot out from under him, and then maybe he’ll he will see things differently.”

If the House does vote on Massie’s resolution, Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who is expected to become the House speaker in the next Congress, said on ABC’s “This Week” on Sunday that he would be a “hard yes” on it.

Hegseth has “got to go,” Jeffries said. “The so-called secretary of Defense has abused his power, undermined our national security and engineered this reckless and costly war of choice in Iran, which has skyrocketed prices on the American people and made the country less safe.”

Most if not all House Democrats are expected to agree.

Last week, Rep. Adam Smith, D-Wash., the ranking member of the Armed Services Committee and its likely chairman in the next Congress, indicated in an interview with The New Republic that he would support Massie’s impeachment resolution, and he predicted it would be adopted.

Referring to Massie’s measure, Smith said, “By every estimation, it will pass and he will be impeached. And off that goes to the Senate. Then the Senate is legally required to spend all of their time dealing with that until it’s done with.”

“Every second Pete Hegseth remains at the top of the Pentagon, our servicemembers are at risk,” Rep. Jason Crow, D-Colo., a senior member of the Armed Services Committee, said in a post on X on Sunday.

Also on Monday, Everett B. Kelley, the national president of the American Federation of Government Employees, a union that represents some 820,000 federal and D.C. government employees, sent a letter to House members urging a “yes” vote on Massie’s Hegseth impeachment resolution.

The letter cited the risks Kelley said the Iran war has posed to the lives of the Defense Department’s uniformed and civilian personnel, and the missive noted Hegseth’s stripping of workplace rights and protections for the department’s civilians.

“By adopting Rep. Massie’s resolution, the House can begin the long overdue bipartisan effort to reclaim its constitutional powers and authority, restore checks and balances, and take responsibility for the lives of military personnel and civilian employees that Mr. Hegseth’s capricious leadership has jeopardized,” Kelley wrote.

Massie announced in mid-September his intention to file an impeachment resolution against Hegseth. But Johnson adjourned the House before the vote would have occurred.

Massie’s new resolution is substantially the same as his earlier one, with the same eight articles of impeachment.

The first three of these alleges that Hegseth’s conduct of the Iran war violated the War Powers Resolution.

Massie’s measure says Hegseth prosecuted the Iran war without congressional authorization and in the absence of an attack on the United States or an imminent threat.

Further, the measure alleges that Hegseth continued the war despite the House and Senate adopting in June a concurrent war powers resolution calling for the withdrawal of U.S. forces from the fight.

And, the resolution says, Hegseth continued the Iran war after the expiration of the War Powers Resolution’s 60-day termination period and any potential 30-day withdrawal period.

Massie’s measure further asserts that Hegseth:

The House has only voted twice to impeach a member of the Cabinet. In 1876, Secretary of War William Belknap was impeached over allegations of corruption, and his abrupt resignation likely contributed to his acquittal in the Senate.

In 2024, the House voted 214-213 to impeach Alejandro Mayorkas, then-secretary of Homeland Security, for “willful and systemic refusal to comply with the law” and “breach of public trust.” The Senate dispensed with the charges without a trial.

California Wine Country Saw Its Earliest Harvest in Decades. Here’s How a 67-Year-Old Business Navigated It — And What Every Entrepreneur Can Learn.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

From supply chain to sustainability, Rodney Strong Vineyards knows what it takes to succeed.

Napa Valley and Sonoma, two of California’s top-growing wine regions, recently underwent their earliest grape harvest in decades. In response to a record-breaking March heat wave and early bud break, grape-picking started in July. The process typically starts in early to mid-August.

Fortunately, wine-growers and -makers across the region turn to harvest playbooks that have withstood the test of time.

One of them is 67-year-old Rodney Strong Vineyards in Healdsburg, California.

Rodney Strong, who had a brief stint in the Navy before dancing on Broadway in New York City, retired from performing life and established his namesake winery in 1959. He brought on Rick Sayre as winemaker in 1979.

In 1989, the Klein family purchased Rodney Strong Vineyards. A graduate of Stanford’s MBA program, Tom Klein met Strong and Sayre a decade prior when his team at McKinsey & Company was hired to evaluate the winery.

To this day, more than six decades on, a successful harvest at Rodney Strong Vineyards strikes the balance between art and science. (Just one example of many: The preferred light pink shade of Rodney Strong’s rosé requires bringing those grapes in under the cover of night, before any heat can degrade them).

During the final days of 2026’s harvest, Entrepreneur visited Rodney Strong Vineyards and its estate vineyard Rockaway Vineyards to learn more about the business’s approach.

The takeaways from chief winemaking and operations officer Justin Seidenfeld and head of winegrowing Allison Dericco can ripen any business for success, whether or not they’re focused on the vine.

Businesses should never underestimate the value of knowing their supply chain — and its nuances — to ensure their product delivers on quality every time.

On the topic of Rodney Strong’s very intentional rosé: Heat is the most immediate logistical risk to grape quality between a morning harvest at Rockaway Vineyards and the crush pad.

“As the fruit sits in the sun, it will begin to dessicate,” Seidenfeld says. “That will change the balance of the fruit.”

That’s why the team starts picking grapes at dawn and fast tracks them to the winery. The fruit is kept in an air-conditioned space until it’s sorted and put into a tank for fermentation, Seidenfeld notes.

These days, businesses across industries can drill down on their supply chain with advanced technologies that gather data at every touchpoint, putting an innovative twist on operational fundamentals that comes with a major competitive advantage.

In a similar vein, businesses have to understand their assets and how to prioritize them for long-term success.

Rodney Strong boasts a robust wine portfolio. A premium, small-lot Rockaway grape experiences a different crush and fermentation than a larger-lot grape destined for the broader Sonoma County or Estate tiers.

Rodney Strong’s Sonoma and Estate wines are larger, so they require a less precise “sweet spotting” technique compared to Rockaway and other reserve wines, Seidenfeld says.

Still, those grapes destined for the broader tier are managed closely to ensure quality.

“[We rely on] careful site development to keep the block as uniform as possible and the implantation of deficit irrigation to help find as much balance across the whole block as possible,” Seidenfeld says.

What’s more, any business can put this into practice by zeroing in on an often overlooked strategy: dynamic resource allocation, which shifts money, talent and management attention to areas where they can generate the most value.

Data analysis plays an important role in business operations and risk mitigation, but well-informed intuition can be just as valuable.

At the vineyard, whether a block of grapes hangs on the vine for one more day or gets picked as soon as possible comes down to chemistry and gut feeling.

Seidenfeld says the decision on when to harvest is the most important one the winery makes every year.

“While we use the data from the samples we pull to guide us, the final call on when to harvest is made through our eyes and ears and taste and not with what we get from the lab,” Seidenfeld explains.

Some degree of risk is inevitable in business, but entrepreneurs who pay attention to the pressures related to growth, culture and information management can sharpen their decision-making ability when it counts — and minimize potential losses.

Businesses that embrace sustainability not only foster positive consumer sentiment, but also protect their long-term product viability and bottom line.

Rodney Strong became the first carbon neutral winery in Sonoma County in 2009. The team works to create a balanced growing environment with practices like water conservation, soil management and biodiversity encouragement, maintaining healthy vines and managing stress as they develop, Dericco notes.

Additionally, using tools like soil moisture monitoring, weather stations and plant-based measurements allows the team to make more precise farming decisions, particularly when it comes to irrigation.

“While our carbon neutrality and waste reduction efforts have broader environmental benefits, it’s these everyday practices in the vineyard that directly influence vine health, fruit development, and ultimately, wine quality,” Dericco says.

The lesson for business owners? Consider sustainability a building block, not an afterthought, to ensure genuine messaging and meaningful collaboration.

Napa Valley and Sonoma, two of California’s top-growing wine regions, recently underwent their earliest grape harvest in decades. In response to a record-breaking March heat wave and early bud break, grape-picking started in July. The process typically starts in early to mid-August.

Fortunately, wine-growers and -makers across the region turn to harvest playbooks that have withstood the test of time.

One of them is 67-year-old Rodney Strong Vineyards in Healdsburg, California.
