Ford May Have a Replacement for the Shelby GT350 Mustang in the Works

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A report citing dealer documents claim a new naturally aspirated ‘Stang will pick up where the GT350 left off.

The sixth-generation Ford Mustang Shelby GT350 was an enthusiast’s favorite. It offered a track-ready middle ground between the standard V-8-powered GT and the supercharged Shelby GT500, with a 5.2-liter naturally aspirated “Voodoo” V-8 pumping out 526 horsepower and revving to 8250 rpm, and a 6-speed manual transmission. The GT350 left production in 2020; Ford has not replaced it with a like-for-like variant for in the current seventh-generation Mustang lineup. But that could change as soon as next year.

Ford Authority is reporting that Ford sent out dealer documents confirming a new high-performance Mustang. The new Mustang would not be called the Shelby GT350—Ford has not used Shelby branding for the S650-generation Mustang, and Shelby American is currently building its own GT350. But the new model would, it seems, fulfill that role in the lineup. (Road & Track reached out to Ford for comment on the potential new Mustang, but a spokesperson only noted the brand “can’t comment on speculation” about future vehicles.)

The report claims the new Mustang would slot between the current Mustang Dark Horse and the Dark Horse SC, and would feature either a 5.2-liter or 5.4-liter naturally aspirated V-8. A six-speed manual transmission would be available, if not the only option, and the new Mustang—unlike the Dark Horse SC—would only be available as a coupe. Per the report, it would arrive in late 2027 as a 2028 model-year vehicle and have a limited production run through the 2029 model year.

Firm performance specs were not detailed for the potential new Mustang, but Ford Authority expects the power output to be around 600 hp, slotting between the 500-hp Mustang Dark Horse and the 795-hp Mustang Dark Horse SC. No word yet on pricing, either; however, the current Mustang lineup has a large gap between the $72,760 Dark Horse Premium and the $108,085 Dark Horse SC where this vehicle would fit in nicely.

Ford has been looking at options to “expand on the Mustang family,” with reported ideas including non-traditional variants such as a four-door coupe-style sedan and even an off-road variant. Reviving the manual GT350 Mustang, at least in spirit, would counter those sacrilegious notions with some welcome red meat for enthusiasts.

As Measles Cases Surge, U.S. Risks Losing Its Elimination Status

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Last week alone, three states reported measles-related deaths, including an infant in Wisconsin.

Measles has made headlines time and time again over the past two years, as cases have hit record highs and vaccination rates reach record lows. Now, the looming threat may soon come true: if measles continues to spread at its current rate, the U.S. may lose its measles elimination status as soon as this week. The nation reached 5,182 confirmed measles cases in 2026 last week, the most to be reported in the United States in 35 years. Last week alone, three states reported measles-related deaths, including an infant in Wisconsin.

The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) will meet this week to decide whether ​the U.S. still meets the criteria ​for measles elimination, according to Reuters. Measles was considered officially eliminated in the U.S. in 2000, meaning there had been no large outbreaks or uncontrolled domestic transmission of the virus for at least 12 months prior.

America’s elimination status is in jeopardy now after the current measles outbreaks first began in January 2025 in West Texas. By January 2026, the spread continued unchecked. A meeting to discuss the country’s measles elimination status was originally scheduled for April 2026, KFF reports, but the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) asked for more time to determine whether measles cases were being transmitted domestically or “imported” (for example, if someone contracted measles abroad and then brought it to the U.S.).

Many issues have contributed to the country’s ongoing measles outbreaks, from sweeping funding and staffing cuts to public health agencies to a lack of leadership — there was no Senate-confirmed director of the CDC for an entire year during this measles outbreak. But the main driver is declining vaccination rates, which experts blame on the Trump administration, specifically HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr, as Scary Mommy previously reported.

The CDC and American Academy of Pediatrics both recommend children get their first measles vaccine dose between 12 and 15 months of age, and the second dose between 4 and 6 years of age before starting school. According to CDC data, 96% of measles cases in 2026 have occurred in unvaccinated individuals. A KFF poll found that roughly one in five adults believe getting the measles vaccine is more dangerous than becoming infected with the measles. Parents are commonly exposed to vaccine misinformation, and data shows it is affecting their health decisions: the number of kindergarteners with vaccine exemptions rose to 4.2% in the 2025-2026 school year, the highest in the country’s history, with 39 states falling below the 95% vaccinated threshold needed to prevent outbreaks.

“There’s no question. The falling vaccination rates have led to these outbreaks. Since before the pandemic, more than half the U.S. counties that track measles coverage have fallen below that magic threshold of 95% needed to prevent outbreaks. Now we’re on the cusp of losing measles elimination, which would be a huge embarrassment to public health in this country,” medical reporter Hallie Jackson said on-air for NBC.

The largest outbreak in the U.S. since it earned measles elimination status, prior to the current one, was in 2018-2019. It was curtailed through a strong federal and state-level public health response, including closing schools where transmission occurred, prohibiting unvaccinated children from attending school, and fining parents for not vaccinating their children, KFF reports. The CDC also declared a public health emergency and made “clear statements about the importance of measles vaccinations.”

With the Trump administration pushing parental choice and shared clinical decision-making for childhood vaccines, only time will tell if this sort of public health response is possible in 2026.

→ Baca artikel asli

Millions of dollars poured into prediction markets on election night, trading on Democratic control of Congress and Texas governor's race

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

More than 87% of all election markets across Kalshi and Polymarket gave the right candidate at least a 90% chance of winning their race 24 hours before election night, a CNBC analysis found.

Election night showed how traders reacted to real-time midterm results, as trading activity surged and market prices shifted to Democrats after results from Indiana began rolling in before 7 p.m. on Nov. 3. Many midterm races were uncompetitive, which boosted the overall hit rate on prediction markets.

"I'd say prediction markets did pretty well," said Koleman Strumpf, a professor of economics at Wake Forest University. "But they didn't outperform the best," he added, referring to traditional forecast polls that tracked results that night.

Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

Prediction markets saw big trades on election night.

After 8 p.m. on Nov. 3, at least $1 million was traded on a Kalshi market that incumbent Gov. Greg Abbott would be reelected. Abbott eventually lost to Democrat Gina Hinojosa by more than 700,000 votes, according to the Associated Press. A little after 11 p.m. on election night, more than $1.4 million was traded on Kalshi that Republicans would not secure control of the House of Representatives.

Following the midterms, both Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan and Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour were on the social media platform X crediting prediction markets for correctly predicting races. Nate Silver, a political forecaster and founder of the Silver Bulletin newsletter, disputed the claim, arguing forecast models influenced prediction market movement. Silver joined Polymarket as an advisor in 2024.

"Prediction market probabilities strongly converged toward [our models] in individual Senate and gubernatorial races," Silver wrote on Silver Bulletin. "I was tracking this stuff closely, precisely because I envisioned that we might wind up in this sort of debate where prediction markets are low-key stealing valor from the models."

Prediction market volume on midterm election-related contracts across Kalshi and Polymarket more than tripled from the last week of October into the midterms week, according to a CNBC analysis of data published on Predictions.com. The data did not include combo markets, which bundle multiple contracts and pay out only if every leg resolves in the trader's favor.

Still, volume on midterm contracts pales in comparison with sports volume, which remains larger than all other categories.

Kalshi exceeded $3 billion in total midterm contract volume, according to the platform's website, which also includes volume for contracts on mayoral races. Last week, Polymarket's two exchanges reported nearly $700 million in volume for midterm contracts.

While prediction markets broadly anticipated Democratic control of Congress, the blue tsunami is expected to bite the industry in other ways.

Three days after the midterms, Sen. Adam Schiff, D-Calif, criticized the federal regulator responsible for the platforms, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, for allowing sports contracts.

"Sports prediction markets are sports bets. By effectively requiring prediction markets to offer contracts in all 50 states, even on Tribal lands, the CFTC is once again violating state law and Tribal sovereignty," Schiff wrote on X.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

Higher Deductibles Cut Auto & Home Premiums – If Your Emergency Fund Can Cover Them

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Raising your auto and homeowners deductibles cuts your premiums every year, but it only works if you could pay the higher amount tomorrow without borrowing. Get a quote at a $1,000 deductible on each policy, and make the switch only if your emergency fund could cover both deductibles at once.

The Insurance Information Institute estimates that raising a car’s collision and comprehensive deductible from $200 to $500 can cut the cost of that coverage by 15% to 30%. Going from $200 to $1,000 can cut that cost by 40% or more. On a homeowners policy, raising the deductible from $500 to $1,000 can cut the premium by as much as 25%.

The catch is timing. You collect the savings a little at a time, every year you don’t file a claim, and you pay the higher deductible all at once on every claim you do file. Say moving from $500 to $1,000 saves $125 a year. It takes four claim-free years for the savings to cover the extra $500 out of pocket.

That’s why your emergency fund decides it. One hailstorm can dent your car and your roof on the same afternoon, and that’s two deductibles due at once. Some homeowners policies also carry a separate wind or hail deductible, usually 1% to 5% of the home’s insured value, that stays in place whatever you pick for the standard one.

If you couldn’t pay the higher deductible this week without a credit card, keep the lower one until your savings catch up.

Air France Takes Its 60th A220 as A318s Fly On

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Air France took delivery of its 60th Airbus A220-300 on Friday, completing the order it placed in 2019 to replace its A318s, A319s and some A320s. The airline’s remaining A318s are still scheduled to fly through March 27, 2027.

The new A220, registered F-HOZJ and named Mimizan, arrived just over five years after Air France put the type into commercial service in October 2021. The airline says its 148-seat A220s now serve 50 destinations and have carried nearly 24 million passengers.

Air France had initially expected to have all 60 A220s in its fleet by 2025. The carrier has also pushed back the end of A318 flying. An August schedule update moved the last planned flights from Nov. 8 to March 27, according to AeroRoutes. The winter schedule includes A318 service from Paris Charles de Gaulle to destinations such as Barcelona, Dublin, and Rome.

Air France took delivery of its first A318 − the smallest A320 family variant − in October 2003 and went on to operate 18, the largest fleet of the type. Three remain active, according to fleet data from Planespotters.net.

The 60th delivery closes out the initial firm order, which also included 30 options and 30 purchase rights. Air France says its 61st A220 and subsequent deliveries will have Wi-Fi installed before entering commercial service. About 85% of the A220s already in service have the equipment, with the remaining installations due by February 2027.

New Details Revealed About The Physical Edition Of GTA 6 For Xbox Series X|S

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Double-sided poster, shipping dates & more

Everyone already knows about the code-in-a-box situation for GTA 6's physical edition on Xbox Series X|S and PS5, but we've also come to learn a few extra details in recent days about what to expect from the boxed copy of the game.

So, when you open up GTA 6 for Xbox Series X|S in physical form, you'll be presented with a code that will allow you to redeem the game on the Microsoft Store - essentially converting your physical purchase into a digital one.

Rockstar Games mentioned a shipping date of November 12th for the physical version a few days ago, but we've spotted some retailers saying they'll start shipping on the 11th instead, while a very small number of people even seem to have their copy already.

Once you've got the physical version in your hands, you'll be able to preload GTA 6 in full, although you can technically also do that from tonight by installing the game via the Xbox mobile app.

As you can also see from the tweet above, the physical edition is now confirmed to come with a double-sided A3 poster (at least if you buy from certain retailers). This poster appears to include the game's main artwork along with some character art on the back, so that's at least a nice little bonus to ease the pain of a disc-less release.

But what about a map? Well, Rockstar Games is handling the map a bit differently this time around, making a digital version available to download for free at launch. Alternatively, physical maps will be available to buy from the Rockstar Store "shortly after launch" for $0.99, but we reckon those will be in extreme demand and quickly sell out.

Of course, none of this really changes the fact that the physical edition of GTA 6 is kinda pointless for any platform as things stand, but it does at least make for a good display piece, and it'll be popular for gifting purposes this Christmas.

What's even weirder about this is that Rockstar is supporting GTA 6's launch with various other physical releases, such as an official soundtrack album and all kinds of merch, but it seems there's little hope for a disc version right now.

Carl Sagan on How to Live with the Unknown

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

In our recent On Being conversation, NASA astrophysicist and exoplanet researcher Natalie Batalha said something that stopped me up short: as sentient beings endowed with awareness, we are “the universe itself becoming aware.” Echoing poet Diane Ackerman’s lovely notion of “the plain everythingness of everything, in cahoots with the everythingness of everything else,” Dr. Batalha added: “It took 13.7 billion years for the atoms to come together to create the portal to the universe which is my physical self. So in that statement is this idea, or the fluidity of time and space. And I kind of see it all at once. And I don’t know what ‘me’ is. I just feel part of everything. And I feel such deep gratitude for being able to take this conscious look at the universe — at myself as being part of the universe.”

The sentiment reminded me of a beautiful interview Carl Sagan (November 9, 1934–December 20, 1996) gave shortly after the premiere of his epoch-making documentary Cosmos, later included in Conversations with Carl Sagan (public library).

In late August of 1980 — two years after he conducted Susan Sontag’s most dimensional interview and nine years before his magnificent conversation with Leonard Bernstein — interlocutor extraordinaire Jonathan Cott visited Sagan’s home in Los Angeles to interview him for Rolling Stone. In the soaring the conversation that followed, Sagan stepped into his native nexus of the scientific and the poetic to contemplate our understanding of the universe and of ourselves, the nature of reality and of human knowledge, and how to live with the unknown.

It’s a critical moment in the history of the world… We are the representatives of the cosmos; we are an example of what hydrogen atoms can do, given fifteen billion years of cosmic evolution. And we resonate to these questions. We start with the origin of every human being, and then the origin of our community, our nation, the human species, who our ancestors were and then the riddle of the origin of life. And the questions: where did the Earth and Solar System come from? Where did the galaxies come from?

Every one of those questions is deep and significant. They are the subject of folklore, myth, superstition, and religion in every human culture. But for the first time we are on the verge of answering many of them. I don’t mean to suggest that we have the final answers; we are bathing in mystery and confusion on many subjects, and I think that will always be our destiny. The universe will always be much richer than our ability to understand it.

To be sure, understanding the whole of the universe seems like too grandiose an aspiration when we are continually struggling to understand the tiny subset of the universe that is ourselves. Three summers before this interview, Sagan had spearheaded The Golden Record — a poetic attempt at such self-comprehension, mirroring humanity back to itself. Now, with an eye to another landmark triumph of self-reflection made possible by scientific progress — the iconic Earthrise photograph taken by the astronauts of Apollo 8 in 1968 — Sagan considers the immense and paradoxical gift of cosmic perspective:

You saw [Earth] for the first time as a tiny blue ball floating in space. You realized that there were other, similar worlds far away, of different size, different color and constitution. You got the idea that our planet was just one in a multitude. I think there are two apparently contradictory and still very powerful benefits of that cosmic perspective — the sense of our planet as one in a vast number and the sense of our planet as a place whose destiny depends upon us.

In this awareness resides a humbling and disquieting reminder of our creaturely limitations. We navigate the world by our common-sense perception, but that perception has blinded us to reality again and again. We have mistaken our sensorial intuitions for facts of the universe — for millennia, we held wrong beliefs about Earth’s shape, motion, and position, because it feels flat and static beneath our feet, and central to the order of the cosmos. We have mistrusted processes and phenomena beyond the boundaries of what we can touch and feel with our limited senses — from evolution, which unfolds on scales of time too vast to be visible within a human lifetime, to quantum mechanics, which operates on subatomic scales imperceptible and almost inconceivable to the human observer. Long before Sagan equipped us with an antidote to the “common pitfalls of common sense” in his timeless Baloney Detection Kit for critical thinking, he tells Cott:

Common sense works fine for the universe we’re used to, for time scales of decades, for a space between a tenth of a millimeter and a few thousand kilometers, and for speeds much less than the speed of light. Once we leave those domains of human experience, there’s no reason to expect the laws of nature to continue to obey our expectations, since our expectations are dependent on a limited set of experiences.

We have to be very careful not to impose our hopes and desires on the cosmos, but instead, in the scientific tradition and with the most open mind possible, see what the cosmos is saying to us.

Sagan points to one particularly blatant obfuscation of reality driven by our self-centered hopes, desires, and delusions — astrology:

[Astrology is] like racism or sexism: you have twelve little pigeonholes, and as soon as you type someone as a member of that particular group, as long as someone is an Aquarius, Virgo or Scorpio, you know his characteristics. It saves you the effort of getting to know him individually.

Sagan ends by considering the nature of human knowledge itself. Drawing on its past, he projects its future:

Human knowledge is a set of successive approximations… There are all sorts of things that we’ve gotten wrong, and all sorts of mind-boggling things that we can’t even glimpse that will be the established fact in a century or two.

There are two extremes to worry about. One is the extreme in which everything is known and there’s nothing left to do. The other is where everything is so complicated you can never begin to do anything. We are lucky to live in a universe were there are laws of nature and things to discover, but they’re not impossibly difficult, so we can understand them to some extent. But they’re also difficult enough so that we’re nowhere near understanding them all. There are exhilarating discoveries yet to be made. It’s the best possible world.

Complement with Diane Ackerman — a favorite poet of Sagan’s, who was her doctoral advisor — on our longing to know the universe beyond ourselves and Primo Levi on the spiritual value of space exploration, then revisit Sagan on the value of uncertainty, the enchantment of chemistry, and the most important perspective in the human world.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

Redistricting battle could spill into 2028

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The redistricting fights that underscored this year’s battle for the House could continue into the 2028 election cycle, although lessons from last Tuesday will likely shape those efforts.

House Republicans had hoped that new congressional maps in seven states would help them limit losses in the face of an unfavorable political environment. But nearly a week after the election, the GOP appeared on track to flip just three redrawn seats while losing a host of seats in Texas, Florida, North Carolina, Ohio and Alabama that were reconfigured in their favor.

“The question is whether the results have let some air out of the [redistricting] balloon or not,” said Erik Nisbet, a professor at Northwestern University’s School of Communication.

Republicans may question the utility of redrawing more state maps in the 2028 cycle, while Democrats will need to weigh how to spend their political capital. But the new lines approved over the past year could still prove more favorable for Republicans in future elections.

“This was a wave election, and there’s no guarantee that the Democratic generic ballot would be D+13 — which is the reason why redistricting didn’t work for Republicans — in the next cycle,” Nisbet added.

New maps appear possible, if not likely, in at least a few states. Maryland voters last week approved an amendment that would clear the way for new boundaries ahead of the 2028 elections. Democratic Gov. Wes Moore, who easily won a second term last week, said he hopes to see a new map, citing the threat of President Donald Trump continuing to push for friendlier lines in GOP-led states. Maryland Democrats could draw a map that would favor them in all eight of the state’s House seats, including the Eastern Shore district of Rep. Andy Harris, the state’s lone Republican in Congress.

Advocates for a new map in Wisconsin got a boost last week when Democrats flipped both the state House and state Senate, setting up a Democratic trifecta. Gov.-elect David Crowley said he would sign a bill redrawing the state’s congressional map if legislators sent one to his desk.

Republicans in recent years have had a lopsided edge in the congressional delegation for Wisconsin, a perennial battleground state. But Democrats flipped two House seats last week, with Rebecca Cooke and Mitchell Berman unseating GOP Reps. Derrick Van Orden and Bryan Steil, respectively, bringing the state delegation to an even 4-4.

Democrats could also look to redraw the lines in New York and Virginia. Earlier this year, voters in the commonwealth approved a new map that would have given Democrats up to four pickup opportunities, but the Virginia Supreme Court later struck it down on procedural grounds. Even without the new map, Democrats flipped two House seats in Virginia last week and came within 2 points of flipping a third.

Democrats in New York, meanwhile, took initial steps toward mid-decade redistricting earlier this year, but 2028 is the earliest they could hold elections under a new map.

On the Republican side, several states have been mentioned as candidates for new maps in 2028.

Officials in GOP-led Mississippi have held hearings this year on redistricting, which could target Democratic Rep. Bennie Thompson. Republicans are also aiming to redraw the state’s legislative lines ahead of next year’s off-year elections.

It’s not clear whether states that were unsuccessful in redrawing their district lines for 2026 will try again when their legislatures convene next year. Republicans in both South Carolina and Indiana fell short in their push to pass new maps and could decide to revisit the issue. But Democrats flipped a House seat in each state last week, and some Republican incumbents won reelection by narrower margins than usual. Still, Republicans maintained their legislative majorities in both states, though Democrats broke their supermajorities in the South Carolina and Indiana state houses.

An analysis by DecisionDeskHQ offers a potential warning sign for Republicans in the Palmetto and Hoosier states: Under the GOP-drawn maps that were not adopted for 2026, Democrats could have flipped even more seats last week.

Redistricting appears off the table in a handful of states where new maps for 2028 had once been considered possible.

Georgia’s outgoing Republican governor, Brian Kemp, said last week that he would not call a special session to address redistricting before the end of the year, after Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms was elected to succeed him.

There were no House flips in the Peach State in the midterm elections, though several Republicans won by closer-than-anticipated margins.

In New Jersey, Democratic Gov. Mikie Sherrill told the New Jersey Globe that she also did not “see a path forward for redistricting right now.”

Democrats flipped two House seats in the Garden State last week. Navy veteran Rebecca Bennett defeated Republican Rep. Thomas H. Kean Jr., while Cape May Mayor Zack Mullock scored a surprise win over GOP Rep. Jeff Van Drew.

With their new majorities in Washington, Democrats could propose legislation to ban partisan gerrymandering.

That’s something John Bisognano, the president of the National Democratic Redistricting Committee, anticipates happening, but he said Democrat-led states should be prepared to respond to new maps drawn in Republican-controlled ones.

“If they are going to continue to take those actions, Democrats in states across the country need to be prepared to respond, and we, of course, now have significantly more opportunities than we did before Tuesday to respond to those states,” he said.

Rejected Twice by Sundance, ‘Mouse’ Is One of the Year’s Best Movies. Why Aren’t More People Talking About It?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Don’t be surprised if you emerge from Alex Thompson and Kelly O’Sullivan’s piercingly moving coming-of-age grief dramedy “Mouse” in a puddle of tears. Graceful in technique, compassionate in its writing, this movie, one of the year’s best, is a crying machine. But that’s not to say this story of a high school girl’s (Katherine Mallen Kupferer, astonishing) formative first encounter with major loss is any bit manipulative.

As heartbreak ripples through a small town, “Mouse” has the texture and deep feeling of Kenneth Lonergan’s post-9/11 New York City drama “Margaret,” starring Anna Paquin as a precocious teenager in freefall after witnessing a pedestrian’s brutal death. Unlike “Margaret,” “Mouse” didn’t take over half a decade to reach screens after production wrapped, but the road was still paved with rejection (baffling in retrospect) for Chicago directing duo Thompson and O’Sullivan. Related Stories King Ghidorah Is Ready for Its Close-Up: How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Cracked the Legendary Monster’s Next Evolution Best Actress Frontrunner Julianne Moore Nailed ‘The Debut’ by Singing, Styling Her Hair Like Liza Minnelli, and Avoiding Playing ‘Some Suffering Mom’

With “Mouse,” the filmmakers found themselves at the Berlin Film Festival for the first time back in February — even as an American indie set in Arkansas about the challenges of coming of age feels like catnip for a festival like Sundance, where such topics are historically de rigueur. And Thompson and O’Sullivan had been to Sundance before, with their critically championed 2024 comedy-drama “Ghostlight,” about an emotionally ramshackle, rookie community theater production of “Romeo and Juliet.” While placement at the legacy indie festival, headed to Boulder in January, doesn’t always mean you’re grandfathered into the acceptance pipeline for your next movie, it was still a surprise to the directors (who are also life partners with two children) when Sundance turned down “Mouse” not once, but twice.

“To be totally fair, we submitted before we probably should have, with a long cut,” O’Sullivan told IndieWire over Zoom. “Apparently, it didn’t matter,” Thompson added.

“We submitted it again the next year, when it was tighter,” added O’Sullivan, though the filmmakers were dismissed again. “The feedback that we got — and again, this is a game of telephone, so we don’t know — is that it had real champions, but that it also had people who didn’t get it. Yes, it was crushing. It was confusing. More than anything, I was like, ‘Wait, but people did such great work on this movie. The work is incredible.’ And not ours even, but Sophie [Okonedo] and Katherine [Mallen Kupferer], and the crew, I was like, ‘I feel like they deserve a really big stage.’”

Luckily, the Berlin Film Festival stepped in to program “Mouse” in the Panorama section, marking Thompson and O’Sullivan’s first bow at a European fest, where it grabbed strong reviews. And, as the filmmakers point out, that festival turned out to be the proper home. (Berlin, after all, just had its strongest lineup in years, under artistic director Tricia Tuttle’s second festival.)

“It really was hard for a moment, and then we heard back from Berlin, and it kind of felt like a career opportunity that went way beyond Sundance,” Thompson said. “Tapping into the narrative outside of the American indie felt like something we’d always aspired to. And those are the films that I identify with the most, the sort of work that comes out of those great European festivals. The most jealous and childish I am is every year when the Venice reviews start coming out, and it’s like, ‘Ah, well, those all sound great.’ And Cannes and Berlin, and our favorite festivals, we have a relationship with Thessaloniki and American Film Festival in Wrocław, and to have that stage at Berlin, it’s like this sort of historic stage. It’s absolutely mind-blowing. I’ve never been in a theater that big with a movie.”

A Gotham nominee for Best Feature, “Mouse” is now quietly in theaters from Independent Film Company. (A sparsely attended Academy screening also took place in Los Angeles over the weekend.) Film chatterers may deem this a “small” movie in a year of “You Can See Everything” and “La Bola Negra,” it is huge in every way.

The film follows Minnie Dunn (Kupferer), who, just as she’s about to enter her senior year, is dealt a life-altering loss. It’s 2002, Michelle Branch is on the radio, and September 11 is not even a year out of view. Faced with a hole in her life in the shape of her lifelong best friend after a tragic accident, Minnie forms a tight bond with the girl’s British stay-at-home mother, Helen (Sophie Okonedo, luminescent here), mourning on her own. Meanwhile, Minnie’s classmates all but literally compete to see who can grieve the loudest in the form of a talent show. “Mouse” is about the centrifugal force of grief and how life goes on around you, but you’re still stuck in the same place.

Big on emotional generosity and ace turns from the filmmakers’ stable of theater-based Chicago actors — including Kupferer and her real-life mother Tara Mallen, who plays Minnie’s struggling, lower-middle-class mom onscreen — “Mouse” deserves as much attention as any of the year’s best films. The grand ambition of “Mouse” and its emotional dividends and wonderful performances suggest it could’ve cleaned up at Telluride or even Toronto, where, in the latter case, a number of indie films flopped, and even the most acclaimed are still searching for distribution.

“Rejected, rejected,” O’Sullivan laughed, later adding, “We understand how complicated programming a festival is, and we don’t feel entitled to be programmed anywhere. We’re grateful to every festival that’s given us a slot.”

She wrote the script for “Mouse” in 2019, around the time their debut feature as collaborators, “Saint Frances,” opened at SXSW. That microbudget indie, directed by Thompson and starring O’Sullivan as a drifting 34-year-old nanny in Chicago, won the Austin festival’s audience award and later earned a John Cassavetes Spirit Award nomination. “Mouse” was actually meant to be their next feature, with one financier, a cushier budget, and a slightly different cast — until the strikes changed their plans, leading to “Ghostlight” as their Sundance debut on a smaller scale.

Based on O’Sullivan’s own experience with loss as a teen, “Mouse” picked back up in fall 2024, shooting on location in her home state of Arkansas. “We were amazingly able to retain the vision” of “Mouse” from before the strikes, she said. “Nothing got cut for budget.”

Thompson, who produced the film, said, “Knowing the budget so intimately, I guess that’s my superpower on the producing side that most directors don’t have is I just get really intimate with the budget. And it seems really boring, but it’s kind of like a script where if you don’t have the means, if it’s not there in the budget, it’s not going to happen… A lot of directors I talked to have been like, ‘Oh, well, my producer said we couldn’t do a rain tower, or my producers said we couldn’t do this shot because we can’t afford it.’ And it’s like, well, you can pretty much afford anything. You just have to give somewhere else. We tend to make really ambitious one-liners like schedules. We had 30 days on this, but that ended up being a two-hour and 15-minute movie, and we cut 20 minutes.”

When asked about the “Mouse” budget, he said, “I think we can say. We’re already underdogs. We might as well say. It was $3 million.”

And it’s $3 million, well-budgeted and in true indie spirit — like a scene where Minnie and her friends watch “Titanic” in a parent’s basement during one of those classic sleepovers where private confidences spill out, insulated from the adult world upstairs and outside.

“We didn’t even try to get the rights to ‘Titanic,’ but we’re mixing at Skywalker,” Thompson said. “They have the entire effects library for ‘Titanic’ … so we rebuilt the dance scene in ‘Titanic’ and used public-domain Irish music, and then Kelly and [producer] Pierce Cravens do the voices of Leo and Kate off in a room being like, ‘I feel so young,’ or something, whatever that is. If you watch the movie, you think they’re watching ‘Titanic’ in the basement, but it’s a totally constructed thing because we’re like, ‘We can’t get the rights to this.'”

On top of “Mouse” already dwelling on a very specific point of loss from O’Sullivan’s own childhood, the filmmaker’s mother also died in Arkansas toward the end of production. That meant she was actively grieving while also leading a set, alongside her partner.

“It was so bizarre,” O’Sullivan said. “She died when we had a week and a half left to shoot… It was incredibly complicated. I just couldn’t believe the timing of it for so many different reasons. I mean, one of which is just that it took forever to make this movie, but the movie allowed me to be there literally when she died. I didn’t have to get on a plane. But then when I was back on set, I just kept hearing these lines that I had written, and they took on a whole new meaning… lines that I had sort of written in a very theoretical way all of a sudden were hitting me in a, ‘God, I can’t believe how true this was, and I didn’t even know.'”

The autobiography that drove “Mouse” extended behind the scenes in even more literal ways. Christen Pitts, the choreographer behind the movie’s climactic ballet elegy sequence set to a cover of Cyndi Lauper’s “Time After Time,” was O’Sullivan’s high school dance teacher. “It feels like so many different chapters of my life are just compressing into the same space, and it feels like a culmination of my entire adult life and teenage years and echoes of the past existing,” O’Sullivan said.

Thompson and O’Sullivan have maintained their filmmaking footprint in and around Chicago — even as “Mouse” brought them to Arkansas — and they don’t feel especially tempted to make the leap to Hollywood, though there have been offers.

“I just write from a place of experience most of the time, and I don’t know, I’ve seen so many movies set in L.A. and New York, and I actually don’t identify with those towns,” O’Sullivan said. “I feel like enough people are doing that, and I like writing from a place of specificity … I’ve spent maybe a year in both places cumulatively. It doesn’t feel like a character that I personally need to explore. But having said that, if I spent more time there, it felt like a story was necessarily set there. I’d be open to it. I just don’t feel compelled to.”

“We really want to have a body of work,” Thompson said. “The people I admire the most are Steven Soderbergh and Sidney Lumet, directors who have dozens of films by the time they stop, and Soderbergh has not stopped, obviously.” He doesn’t know that a move to Los Angeles is possible given their artistic goals. “We’ve gone out on meetings, we’ve done pitches, we’ve considered projects from studios, and the timeline always felt kind of like it just hasn’t really worked out. ‘Mouse’ was so close to being that thing, and then the writers’ strike, and the SAG strike, we lost a cast member, and it just all fell away. So we just thought, ‘What if we just do what we did on ‘Ghostlight,’ but on steroids? If I can raise half a million dollars, I wonder if we can raise three.”

With the filmmakers now set up at Gersh talent agency, he added, “If we want to make a movie every 15 months, we probably can’t do that in the studio system. But I think we both like the idea of playing in a bigger sandbox and taking the intimacy, taking all the things we’ve learned, and working in a bigger sandbox. I don’t think that’s unattractive, but it’s just, for the way we work, this has made the most sense so far.”

Next up, Thompson directed a high-concept body-swap comedy starring the “Ghostlight” father-daughter (and real-life father-daughter) team of “Mouse” star Katherine Mallen Kupferer with Keith Kupferer. Plus, they’ve co-directed “The Steel Harp,” starring Lois Smith and O’Sullivan in a return to acting. Both are expected in 2027.

“We shoot on the same lenses as ‘Saint Frances,’ same aspect ratio as ‘Mouse,’ in a world that winks at a bigger sandbox,” Thompson said of the body swap movie. “It’s a total gamble. I’ve got no idea if it’s going to work… It seems to be quite funny according to my three-year-old.”

“Mouse” is now playing in New York and Los Angeles, with a wider expansion to follow in November.

Court declines to hear dispute tied to George Floyd protests and cases involving former baseball player Roberto Clemente and actor Randy Quaid

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court on Monday denied review in over 200 cases, including a dispute that arose in Minneapolis after the murder of George Floyd in police custody in 2020 and clashes involving actor Randy Quaid and the sons of a baseball legend. These denials were part of a list of orders from the court’s Nov. 6 conference that was released on Monday morning.

The court on Friday granted one petition for review from that conference, in a case involving whether and when a bankruptcy court can refuse to honor an agreement to arbitrate disputes.

The Minneapolis case was brought by a group of businesses located at the intersection where Floyd was killed. Following Floyd’s death, the city blocked the intersection “with concrete barricades,” which “prevented vehicles and pedestrians from accessing” the area, and withdrew police protection, according to the businesses’ petition for review. Those barricades remained in place “for over one year,” and led to significant economic losses for the businesses and a surge in violent crime, the petition said.

The businesses challenged the city’s actions in state court, alleging that it had violated the Constitution’s takings clause and similar protections in Minnesota’s constitution by interfering with their operations and failing to compensate them accordingly. However, a district court and the Minnesota Court of Appeals disagreed, and the Minnesota Supreme Court denied the businesses’ request for it to review the case.

In August, the businesses came to the Supreme Court, asking the justices to revive their case. On Monday, the Supreme Court declined to do so without comment.

The case involving family members of baseball legend Roberto Clemente also involved the takings clause. Clemente’s adult sons and companies associated with preserving Clemente’s legacy challenged Puerto Rico’s use of the baseball legend’s image on “license plates and tags,” an initiative that “generat[ed] 15 million dollars for Puerto Rico” but also fueled ill will against the family, “who Puerto Ricans thought were to blame for a fee levied on them during dire economic times,” according to the petition for review. Through their lawsuit, the Clementes aimed to block Puerto Rico from using their father’s image in the future and recover financial damages.

Earlier this year, the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 1st Circuit prevented the Clemente family’s takings clause claim from proceeding, emphasizing that it (along with the federal statute concerning trademark law) is “designed not to protect legacies but instead to remedy ... the taking of property for public use without just compensation.” In June, the Clementes asked the Supreme Court to take up the case and, among other things, clarify that the takings clause can apply in cases involving intangible property.

After considering the petition at three consecutive conferences, the justices on Monday denied review.

The petition for review involving Quaid addressed access to “restricted law-enforcement database material—including criminal history, warrant-status, mug-shot, and jail-record information” – but was perhaps most notable because it was written by Quaid and his wife, Evgenia Quaid. The Quaids contended that “an employee of the Santa Barbara Sheriff’s Department” sent such restricted material concerning the couple to an assistant district attorney, who then sent it to a private attorney, “without subpoena, discovery request, court order, or law-enforcement purpose.” This “unauthorized access” and information sharing violated privacy laws, according to the Quaids.

They came to the Supreme Court after losing before a federal district court and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 9th Circuit, which held that the Quaids’ privacy claims “are barred by the litigation privilege,” a legal doctrine that prevents lawsuits over communication that occurs as part of a judicial proceeding. On Monday, the Supreme Court declined to take up the case.

In Monday’s order list, the justices also asked the United States solicitor general to share the government’s views on AstraZeneca UK Ltd. v. Atchley, a case on whether U.S. terrorism victims may sue 21 “of the world’s largest pharmaceutical and medical-device companies” under the Antiterrorism Act for allegedly aiding and abetting terrorists in Iraq.

Additionally, the justices sent Roybal v. Griffith, a transgender female inmate’s challenge to jail policies in El Paso County, Colorado, regarding the treatment of transgender inmates back to the lower court for reconsideration in light of West Virginia v. B.P.J. and United States v. Skrmetti.

In B.P.J., the court ruled in June that state laws barring transgender female athletes from participating on women’s and girls’ sports teams do not violate a federal civil rights law or the equal protection clause of the 14th Amendment. In Skrmetti, the court last year upheld a Tennessee law prohibiting some medical treatments for transgender minors, holding that it was not subject to heightened scrutiny under the equal protection clause.

Also on Monday, the justices granted the solicitor general’s request to participate in three oral arguments during the December argument session:

New tax incentives give Washington’s sustainable aviation fuel industry a boost

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Challenges, breakthroughs and big ideas in the race to protect our planet. SEE MORE

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 9, 2026 at 9:38 amNovember 9, 2026 at 9:38 am

Washington has cleared a milestone in its effort to become a hub for lower-carbon jet fuel, unlocking tax incentives aimed at attracting production to the state.

Gov. Bob Ferguson announced Friday that Washington has reached the capacity to produce at least 20 million gallons of sustainable aviation fuel annually. That triggers incentives taking effect Jan. 1, including a tax credit starting at $1 per gallon for qualifying fuel. The credit rises to as much as $2 for fuels with greater emissions reductions.

The Department of Ecology recently surveyed potential producers and confirmed that Phillips 66’s Ferndale refinery alone has enough capacity to meet the threshold. The benchmark measures production capacity, not actual output.

Sustainable aviation fuel, or SAF, is widely seen as one of the most promising near-term options for cutting aviation’s carbon emissions because it can be blended with conventional jet fuel and used in existing aircraft. But it remains more expensive than petroleum-based fuel, and production is orders of magnitude below the industry’s needs.

“We have so many unique assets in Washington that position us to be global leaders in the SAF transition, and this tax incentive is the final element needed to really unleash the tidal wave of opportunity,” said Jake Gentry, executive director of the Cascadia Sustainable Aviation Accelerator, in a GeekWire interview.

The accelerator launched in January, bringing together airlines, SAF producers, public officials, researchers and others to promote the industry in the Pacific Northwest. Its work includes helping producers secure financing, develop feedstock supplies and connect with customers.

SAF can be produced from used cooking oil, grease and fats, plants and agricultural waste, and captured carbon. Its climate benefits depend on the raw materials and energy used to make it.

The fuel still produces carbon dioxide when burned. But unlike petroleum-based jet fuel, which releases carbon that would otherwise stay trapped underground, SAF recycles carbon already in circulation in plants, animals and the atmosphere. Washington’s per-gallon tax credit requires emissions at least 50% below those of conventional jet fuel.

Washington’s production capacity is below that of some larger facilities elsewhere. A Diamond Green Diesel plant in Texas can produce up to 235 million gallons of SAF annually, according to its partner Valero. That figure also represents capacity, not actual production.

With the minimum threshold being met, all Washington SAF producers will be eligible for the tax credit regardless of their volumes.

A startup called Twelve is already making the fuel in the central Washington city of Moses Lake. The company opened its AirPlant One facility last year and began commercially producing SAF this summer from captured carbon dioxide and water. The technology has the potential to create the most climate-friendly SAF available, potentially unlocking the largest state tax credits.

Nicholas Flanders, Twelve’s CEO and co-founder, welcomed the announcement.

“This is an extraordinary signal of support for our industry,” Flanders said via email. “Washington is building the leading foundation in the country for next-generation domestic fuel production, and Twelve is ready to build upon it.”

Twelve’s first-of-its-kind facility is designed to make up to 50,000 gallons of SAF per year. AirPlant Two, a much bigger plant that would make tens of millions of gallons a year, is in development. The company hasn’t said where it will be located.

Dutch-owned SkyNRG is building a SAF facility in Walla Walla, Wash., and plans to use renewable natural gas that’s captured at landfills and from dairy waste as a feedstock. Earlier this year, leaders said the company had secured key environmental approvals and plans to start commercial operations in 2030.

Washington’s relatively affordable hydroelectric power has historically helped attract climate tech companies. But growing electricity demand, including from data centers, is putting pressure on the region’s power supply.

Washington also faces competition from other states offering SAF tax incentives. Illinois and Minnesota already offer credits, and Nebraska’s program starts next year. Meanwhile, a federal credit was reduced from $1.75 per gallon to $1 after President Donald Trump returned to office.

Washington leaders tout other perks that could attract SAF production. It’s the only state that offers both a SAF tax credit and a clean fuel standard. Together, the policies help make cleaner jet fuel more cost-competitive by providing tax benefits and allowing SAF producers to earn credits they can sell to other fuel suppliers that need to meet the state’s emissions requirements.

The accelerator also supports efforts with Washington State University to develop regional feedstock supplies, including waste from logging and agriculture.

“Between our long history of aviation innovation, our intentional investments into the sector and our climate-forward policies, we’re making sure Washington is the best state in the country to produce sustainable aviation fuels,” Ferguson said in a statement.

The Climate Pledge is a commitment to reach net-zero carbon emissions by 2040, co-founded by Amazon and Global Optimism in 2019.

Seven years later, the community has grown to over 700 signatories across 62 industries and 49 countries and territories, reducing emissions, scaling emerging climate solutions, and addressing challenges across operations and value chains.

How to make friends at freshman orientation

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

What happens when more than 100 members-elect descend on Capitol Hill a week after Election Day?

As Rep.-elect Dan Koh, D-Mass., put it, “It really is like high school — but it’s fun high school.”

Trainings and guest speakers aren’t everyone’s idea of a fun time, but Democrats are feeling good about this year’s new member orientation. Giddy, even.

The incoming freshmen will pose for a class photo on the House steps. They’ll test their luck in a lottery to see who lands the best office space. And everywhere they look, they see signs of the blue wave that swept over the country.

“I think — well, I hope — that we will be doing a lot of exceeding expectations in this caucus. The American people deserve it and expect it,” said Rep.-elect Sam Forstag, D-Mont., as he headed to lunch after an orientation session on Monday.

Forstag is the first Democratic congressman from Montana in 30 years, succeeding retiring GOP Rep. Ryan Zinke. A former smokejumper who parachuted into remote forests to put out wildfires, Forstag won by more than 3 points in a district President Donald Trump carried by double digits in 2024.

“I think there’s a lot of folks who didn’t expect us to,” he said of his win. “Nobody knew who we were this past December when I was still working at the smoke jumper base in Missoula.”

Democrats are still digesting their larger-than-expected wins, which saw them flip more than 40 House seats and oust a number of high-profile incumbents, including National Republican Congressional Committee Chairman Richard Hudson, R-N.C.

“It’s like drinking out of a firehose,” said Rep.-elect Rebecca Cooke, D-Wis., who unseated incumbent GOP Rep. Derrick Van Orden in Wisconsin’s 3rd District.

Cooke and her opponent clashed during the campaign over his use of AI-generated ads that she described as deepfakes. Now, they have to work together to make sure the transition goes smoothly.

“The congressman said that he’s willing to be helpful in that transition. I think it’s important that we make sure that the casework is transferred over, that constituent service is a priority, and that nothing falls through the cracks for people,” she said.

The 120th Congress will be full of history-makers, and a record-breaking number of women are set to serve in the House.

“This is a Congress with the most women ever to be a part of this chamber,” Cooke said. “And a lot of [Democrats], frankly, from the Midwest, and so I’m excited to build that sort of power from the Midwest to make sure that our interests are represented.”

The math isn’t lost on Republicans navigating a freshman class where they have fewer peers than they hoped.

“I thought that it would probably go a little bit different. I think a lot of people did, but it is what it is. You work with what we have,” said Rep.-elect Mark Lamb, R-Ariz.

But just like high school, everyone’s trying to make friends. As incoming members filed into the Capitol Visitor Center on Monday morning, Rep.-elect Cait Conley, D-N.Y., introduced herself to Rep.-elect Sydney Gruters, R-Fla.

Meanwhile, Rep.-elect Claire Valdez, D-N.Y., who describes herself as a “proud democratic socialist,” walked by chatting with Del.-elect Robert White, who will become only the third person in the modern era to hold the title of D.C.’s nonvoting delegate.

“I’m sitting next to a teacher right now; I’m sitting next to the mayor of Scranton … good, nice people,” said Rep.-elect Zack Mullock, D-N.J., who said he was surprised by how many “regular people” he was meeting among the incoming class.

Rep.-elect Brad Lander, D-N.Y., who ousted fellow Democrat Dan Goldman in a Brooklyn-based primary before cruising to victory in the general election, was also making friends.

“I was hanging out with Donavan McKinney yesterday in orientation, and I’m just really excited to meet all the amazing people. Micah Lasher, we were hanging out last night. Jacqui Irwin, so we’re just trying to get to know each other,” he said.

After arriving on Sunday with suitcases in hand, the future freshmen got a chance to set foot inside the House chamber later that day. Lander said he’d been there before to see Rep. Greg Casar, D-Texas, a friend of his, get sworn in four years ago. But Sunday was his first time on the floor itself.

“It’s obviously a lot different when it’s you,” he said.

Koh agreed, saying “everyone in that room, Republican or Democrat, had a lot of reverence for where they were.”

“I think it was just a good reminder about how despite all the division, that’s what we’re going to focus on — trying to figure out how to get things done for the American people,” he said.

Members-elect were trying their best to navigate their new workplace on Monday, many of them donning backpacks as they came to terms with the realities of their new job.

“We just have to get back to class,” said Rep.-elect Ed Gallrein, R-Ky., to a crowd of reporters trying to flag him down as he left orientation to use the restroom.

They’d have Italian food for lunch, after a session in the CVC auditorium where many of the future members typed notes on their laptops.

“They’ve got a packed agenda for us all week, really helping us figure out how to get started. Build our team, set course, understand where the bathrooms are — large and small. We got a lot to learn,” Lander said.

13 Potted Plants You Can—and Should—Winter Indoors

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 9, 2026 5:31 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

Plenty of gardeners place their houseplants outdoors for the summer and enjoy them indoors the rest of the year. So why not try a similar strategy, and save a little money while you’re at it, by bringing your favorite cold-tender plants inside when the temperature drops so they’ll be ready to grow again when spring and summer rolls around?

Before you bring your outdoor plants in for the winter, you’ll need to do a bit of preparation. Start by inspecting them for pests and treating, if necessary. Repot if a plant needs it, and cut down on (or eliminate) fertilizing. Some plants can simply be brought indoors as houseplants, others should be forced into dormancy for a few months of cool storage, and still others are best kept going by making rooted cuttings. The following are a few cold-tender plants that you can bring inside each winter and then continue to enjoy outside for many summers to come.

While some palm trees are hardy in the winters of the Deep South, most palms need help to survive winter temperatures, even only to 55 to 60 degrees Fahrenheit . It’s possible to cover and winterize some palms planted in the ground, but potted palms should come indoors (preferably to a bright, humid spot). Move palms to an indoor location that gets lots of light. Water them regularly to keep the soil moist until spring, and carry them back outdoors after nights warm back up to at least 50 or 60 degrees.

Leave cacti outdoors as late into the season as possible so that the combination of shorter days and cooler nights will stimulate a dormancy response. You’ll need to keep an eye on the thermometer and know the low-temperature threshold for your particular type of cactus. Some species may be able to withstand 20-degree temperatures, but others cannot. If unsure, move potted cacti indoors as soon as cold drops to about 45 to 50 degrees Fahrenheit.

Stop watering cacti 3 to 4 weeks before the move so the plants are dry, and check them carefully for pets like mealy bugs or mites. In general, cacti do best in a cool location with minimal water, as they go dormant. Transition them back outside in early spring, gradually introducing sunlight if the plants show any sign of burning.

Citrus trees can actively grow and flower in winter, and may also ripen fruit if they’re pollinated. Even without winter growth, it’s a good idea protect these plants during the chilly months. As the days get shorter, move potted citrus trees (typically, dwarf varieties of lime and lemon trees) to an interim space outdoors that gets some protection from the elements, like a porch with radiant heat, and cut back on watering.

Before the first frost, move the citrus tree into a brightly lit indoor room, ideally between 55 and 70 degrees Fahrenheit. Citrus plants are sensitive to the drying effects of air, so it’s best to position them away from heat vents and cold drafts. Provide supplemental humidity with a pebble tray or regular misting.

Move tropical hibiscus plants indoors when overnight temperatures reach the low 50s. Place them in a brightly lit room, and treat them like regular houseplants. Cut back on watering and fertilizing during these cooler months. Try to avoid heat vents and cold drafts, and put the plants on a pebble tray to boost the ambient humidity, if necessary. Prune tropical hibiscus three times during late fall and winter (October, December, and February) to maintain the plant’s shape.

Prune jasmine vines by at least 25 percent before bringing them in for winter, letting them adjust gradually by bringing them inside at night and slowly lengthening the time spent indoors. Some or all of the plant’s leaves will fall off, but new ones will grow again in time. Bring jasmine into a sunny room before frosty weather arrives, and keep it away from drying air vents and drafts. Use a pebble tray to boost humidity to about 30 percent.

Coleus plants are grown for their stunning foliage and adaptability to shade. The plants are annuals in most regions, though, since they do not like temperatures below 50 degrees Fahrenheit and cannot survive temperatures below freezing. Bring the potted plant indoors once nights hover near 50 degrees. Repot the plant if it is outgrowing its container.

Another option is to propagate coleus stems to use next spring. Cut a 6-inch healthy stem, remove low leaves, and dip the stem in a rooting hormone before planting it in a container with moist potting mix. Cover the container with a plastic bag for a few weeks and keep it in a warm and sunny spot; water regularly.

Aromatic rosemary is a favorite cooking herb for many fall and winter dishes. However, the plant does not always survive winters in USDA Zones 6 or lower. Start by planting a rosemary in a well-draining container. Bring potted rosemary indoors before the first freeze. Keep it in a cool location with fairly high humidity, but avoid overwatering; if its roots stay damp and cool, it can develop root rot.

Feel free to harvest from the plant, but never cut off more than about 20 percent of the foliage and avoid cutting into woody main stems. You also can propagate rosemary indoors at the end of the season with 4- to 6-inch cuttings from a stem with no flowers.

Elephant ears, or taro, is a tropical plant that can survive a light frost only; a true freeze will kill the tuber. Simply move potted elephant ears indoors to a cool bright location. For those grown in the ground, carefully dig up the elephant ear tuber when cool weather approaches and the foliage dies back (they won’t survive winter outdoors in USDA Zone 6 and lower). Cut stems down to about 4 to 6 inches, and gently wipe off excess dirt.

Place the tuber in a warm, dark location with good air circulation to dry out for a few days. Wrap the bulbs in paper or drop them in a paper bag and store them in a cool, dry, dark place. Check them periodically for rot (throw rotten ones away) and insect damage (treat with an insecticide). Beware that nearly all its parts are toxic to pets and people if ingested—we don’t recommend bringing it indoors if you have young children.

It is technically possible to bring Boston ferns indoors and overwinter them in their active state, but it gets very messy very quickly. Instead, let them rest. Put them in a cool, moderately light to dark location that will not freeze, like an unheated basement or garage.

Before bringing your fern inside, hose it down on a warm (60-degree Fahrenheit) day to spray away insects and loose leaves. Give it a light trim, and shake and ruffle the fronds to drop debris before carrying the plant to its indoor spot. Moisten dry soil as needed until spring. Don’t be alarmed if some leaves pale or drop.

Geranium is another plant that likes to sleep the winter away. Dig the plants out of the ground before the first freeze, and gently knock the soil off the roots. Allow the plants to dry in a cool, shady location for a couple of days. Place the plants upside down in a paper bag and close the top. Store them in a cool, dark location that stays between 55 and 60 degrees.

About 2 months before the last frost date, trim the stored geraniums and plant them in moist potting soil with two leaf nodes below the soil line. You also can propagate cuttings or bring potted geraniums indoors to a sunny window.

Thyme is a flavorful and aromatic perennial herb, but a tender one in regions 5 or lower. Plant culinary thyme in a container in spring, keeping it in a sunny, bright location with well-draining soil. Bring it indoors for winter before the first frost. If the herb’s roots are peeking though the bottom of the pot or it looks crowded, repot the thyme when you bring it in. It also can be divided easily.

Be sure to place the container near a sunny window with a western or southern exposure in a room that maintains a 60- to 70-degrees Fahrenheit temperature for most of the day. Prune or pinch off flowers to preserve the herb’s flavor. Water as needed, allowing the container dry out between watering. You can fertilize every week or two with a diluted solution of fish emulsion or liquid seaweed. Harvest sprigs as needed for winter dishes.

Although Musa basjoo, Musella lasiocarpa, and a few other banana trees are quite cold hardy, most will not tolerate extended freezing temperatures. Potted banana plants can be brought indoors to a bright, sunny room to spend the winter in a slowed active state. They can take up some space indoors, so you also can help them sleep through winter. As the weather cools, gradually reduce watering. Before the first frost, cut the plant to 6 inches in height and move it to a dark, cool location. Keep the soil just moist until the weather warms in the spring.

The stunning evergreen cordyline is hardy only in the warmest growing zones, but potted cordylines can be moved indoors when temperatures dip into the 60s. Place them in a spot with good ventilation and consistent indoor temperatures; avoid placing them near air vents or in drafty areas. Increase humidity around cordyline by situating it in a humid room or on a saucer filled with pebbles and water, and avoid fluoride-treated tap water. Although the plant does not require dormancy in winter, it does like a rest, so cut water some during fall and winter.

We’ll help you set up your first garden—whether that’s a few pots on your patio, a raised bed, or an in-ground plot out back—and select the right plants for your soil and region.

Mark Wolfe is a BobVila.com multimedia reporter with an extensive background in the horticulture industry.

Teresa Odle is a writer and editor who has lived and gardened throughout the mountains of arid New Mexico. She joined the BobVila.com team as a freelancer in March 2020, editing and writing helpful articles mostly on gardening and outdoor living.

A Risky Summit Push on Everest

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

It’s now or never for the Russian team on the West Ridge of Everest. Andrey Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov have set off for the summit without oxygen, hoping the weather will hold. They are completely alone on the mountain, and the push is full of risk.

Everest is still loaded with fresh snow after two cyclones, and there are only three of them to break trail and find the best route above 7,500m. That was their highest point during their earlier venture in October.  In addition, the wind may rise dramatically on Thursday, Vitor Baia, a Portuguese high-altitude meteorologist, told ExplorersWeb.

“It looks like we have a weather window of about a week, followed by a significant intensification of the wind,” leader Andrei Vasiliev told Anna Piunova of Mountain Club.

The question is exactly when the wind will increase, and whether the climbers will have enough time to summit and descend to a safer altitude.

“They have three days of weak wind, until Wednesday [November 11], but then the wind will increase sharply,” said Baia.

He says the best summit day will be Tuesday, with clear skies and weak winds. “Then they could use Wednesday to descend,” he added. “[That day will have] some clouds but still acceptable wind speeds.”

The situation will worsen remarkably on Thursday, when the wind will shift from the west to the northwest and increase dramatically to 50 kph at 8,000m and 60kph at the summit.

In addition, avalanche hazards are still high due to the large amounts of snow after the latest storm two weeks ago. Finally, as winter approaches, it’s getting colder every day, the climbers reported.

After their first trip up ended in a tactical retreat as the cyclone hit, they retreated to Kathmandu to recover from some health issues. Now they are fit again and ready for a second try. They returned to Base Camp last Thursday. Not surprisingly, they found it covered in a thick new layer of snow. Breaking trail, they sank at some points up to their waists, Vasiliev reported.

“Our high-altitude boots were in a cache two hours ahead of us, so we used duct tape to seal our pant cuffs to our trekking boots, trying to keep the snow out,” Kondrashkin reported. They eventually reached the cache and continued up in an exhausting 14-hour-long effort to a second cache (their Camp 1), where they had left their tent and sleeping bags.

They were near an avalanche-prone area and several snow slides swept nearby, prompting them to wait a day before continuing. Meanwhile, Shipilov showed signs of food poisoning. Kodrashkin recalls that Saturday morning started with the following conversation:

“Vitalya, how are you feeling? Are we heading up?”

That answer needs some clarification. A professional like Vitaly Viktorovich [Shipilov] really only has two modes: “We’re going” and “I’m dying.”

In fact, Shipilov had a fever and acute pain in the lower back, so the team headed down to their base in Lobuche. As this was going on, the snow had time to sluff off from the previously avalanche-prone area.

After just one night of rest, Vasiliev confirmed they were going up again. If all went according to plan, they will spend tonight back in their tent above the Lho La. Unfortunately, the climbers have not shared their tracker showing their current location and progress. On their previous trip up, they reached some 7,500m on Everest’s West Shoulder, near the beginning of the West Ridge and the potential detour to the Hornbein Couloir.

Everest’s North Face from the base of the West Ridge. Photo: Sergey Kondrashkin

Quant (QNT) is consolidating after a spectacular run, yet the foundations for an extraordinary re-rating may already be in place. Elon Musk’s Grok AI predicts that while the broader crypto market has faced pressure and QNT has pulled back from its late-September peak, a highly unlikely but transformative catalyst could still propel it into a new valuation paradigm.

Imagine a scenario in which The Clearing House’s On-Chain Money Initiative, having already selected Quant’s Overledger technology, expands to become a de facto global standard.

(SOURCE: Grok AI Predicts QNT Crypto to Explode)

In this hypothetical situation, major central banks, the Bank for International Settlements, and a consortium of the world’s largest commercial banks formally designate Quant’s interoperability layer as the preferred, or even required, technology for cross-border tokenized deposits, wholesale central bank digital currency (CBDC) bridges, and regulated real-world asset (RWA) settlements.

New capital and liquidity rules could incentivize or mandate institutions to hold QNT as a necessary asset for network access and collateral, creating structural, multi-year demand that far exceeds current circulating supply dynamics.

QNT currently trades in the $195–$205 zone after a powerful rally that took it from the $60–$70 area to highs above $300–$350 in weeks.

The subsequent pullback has found support in the $185–$195 region on multiple tests, with higher lows beginning to form relative to the deeper summer base.

Volume expanded dramatically during the initial breakout and remains elevated on up days, suggesting accumulation rather than pure distribution.

A sustained reclaim and hold above the $220–$240 resistance cluster would confirm the next leg higher and open the path toward a retest of the prior peak.

In an extreme institutional-adoption scenario, measured-move extensions and multi-cycle Fibonacci projections from the multi-year base could theoretically support far higher targets once the market fully prices in structural demand for the token.

Even as price has cooled from its parabolic spike, the underlying institutional progress offers a constructive counter-narrative. Real bank infrastructure deals of this scale are rare in crypto.

If the broader market stabilizes and the 2027 launch timeline for The Clearing House initiative approaches with positive updates, Quant is well-placed to reassert itself as one of the purest plays on regulated, interoperable tokenized finance.

BYD Offices Raided in Legal Probe Over Land Rover Defender Similarities

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The BYD Ti7 bears more than a passing resemblance to Land Rover’s current off-road champ, but is that enough to make a case in court?

American buyers may not be familiar with the BYD Ti 7, a Chinese-made three-row hybrid SUV with a boxy shape and an unusual square of painted surface floating above the beltline. They will, however, be familiar with the Land Rover Defender sold in the U.S., a three-row SUV with a boxy shape and an unusual square of painted surface floating above the beltline. And according to a report from British newspaper The Times, Land Rover may have noticed some similarities between the two SUVs.

Last August, according to The Times, a court-ordered raid of BYD’s Dutch offices occurred on behalf of Land Rover company JLR. This was, reportedly, to find documents surrounding how a Land Rover Defender was used in the Ti 7’s development—evidence that could be key in a potential legal case over similarities between the models.

The BYD Ti 7 starts at the equivalent of $27,000 below the MSRP of the most affordable three-row Defenders on the U.K market. It also gets a plug-in hybrid powertrain alongside its seven-seat layout, while Land Rover’s plug-in Defender is only available in England with six seats.

Notably, Land Rover has already been involved in one lawsuit over another automaker attempting to build a product that looks astonishingly similar to a Defender. That model, the Ineos Grenadier, was cleared for sale in 2020 despite looking, well, much like an original Defender.

In the case of the BYD Ti 7, the visual similarities with Land Rover’s larger Defenders are obvious. The question is how much the two SUVs actually have in common, and whether or not it is enough to build a winning legal argument. Top Gear reports that a Dutch court will decide whether a case will proceed later this week.
