Rubies on Mars?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Written by Roger C. Wiens, SuperCam Principal Investigator for NASA’s Perseverance rover at Purdue University

Exploration on Mars turns up the unexpected. Perseverance is equipped with a fantastic set of instruments for studying the rock record of this ancient planet. Both PIXL and SuperCam have great capabilities for detecting all kinds of elements. SHERLOC, SuperCam, and PIXL can detect minerals using various techniques including x-ray fluorescence, Raman, and infrared spectroscopies. As a side benefit of the way SuperCam performs its Raman spectroscopy, it can also study time-resolved luminescence. Normally luminescence occurs over a broad wavelength range (most of Earth’s commercial lighting uses luminescence in some way or another). Two years ago, the SuperCam team was startled to find strong, narrow luminescence peaks at two wavelengths of 692.7 and 694.1 nanometers when they shot a light-colored rock with the laser. This luminescence comes from delayed atomic transitions in chromium atoms present in a corundum mineral structure. SuperCam can time the delay of the atomic transitions via the resulting photons, and so we could verify that this emission came from chromium in corundum. The results were published in Geophysical Research Letters by a team led by Ann Ollila earlier this year, so you can read all about it. The gemstone version of chromium-containing corundum is ruby. Prior to installing SuperCam on Perseverance, we placed a ruby in the laser beam and watched the entire room pulse a brilliant red as SuperCam’s laser beam hit the gemstone; it was extremely bright.

While we haven’t found collectable rubies on Mars yet, the little corundum crystals are helping us learn more about Mars’ past. Corundum is a metamorphic and pegmatitic mineral which we would not expect to find on Mars, a planet that lacks plate tectonics. However, large meteor and asteroid impacts can create local metamorphism due to long-term heating from the impact. Because we are in a very ancient terrain near the rim of Jezero crater, we can expect some evidence of localized metamorphism. As the first mission in such terrain, we are learning new things. Earlier in the mission we tried this technique only rarely; lately we have carried out the time-resolved luminescence observations frequently on light-toned rocks to look for more evidence of impact effects. It’s great to have this unexpected tool on board as we delve further into Mars’ deep past.

Dear Robot: How Travel Is (and Isn’t) Talking to AI

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Travel brands invest deeply in AI visibility, but according to Skift's audit of 50 major travel websites' crawler policies, few tell bots where to go — and the directives don't always match who actually gets in. That looks like a shaky starting point, as the rules of AI engagement change.

Travel brands say they want AI to find them. Their websites often tell AI something else, or nothing at all.

That’s the finding of Skift’s review of robots.txt files for 50 major travel sites. These files tell automated programs, or crawlers, whether they’re welcome, and only 10 of the 50 explicitly addressed AI crawlers. The rest lumped them in with every other bot or left them to default settings.

The gap matters more as AI becomes a way travelers shop. In July, Adobe reported that AI visits to travel sites surged 119% year over year. But there’s a “major bottleneck,” according to Adobe analyst Vivek Pandya. “Significant portions of U.S. travel and retail sites remain unreadable to machines, restricting their presence across AI search results,” he wrote in a blog post.

“When an LLM cannot easily read brand content (such as amenities, pricing, or availability),” Pandya added, “potential revenue is being left on the table.”

By submitting this form, you agree to receive email communication from Skift.

See more essential travel news in your search results.

Tags: artificial intelligence, online travel, skift feature, skift intelligence, the prompt

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Skift reviewed robots.txt files — the file that tells automated crawlers whether they're welcome — for 50 major travel sites and found that only 10 explicitly addressed AI crawlers. The other 40 left AI access to catch-all rules or default settings written for other purposes. That gap gained weight on Oct. 30, when Anthropic, Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI backed a proposal that would let a site opt out of AI training by all four with a single robots.txt entry — but only if the site adds it explicitly, since catch-all rules won't carry it. Among the sites that did set AI rules, the pattern favored visibility over training: Expedia and Vrbo give AI search crawlers broad access, Tripadvisor blocks training crawlers while allowing some search access, and Best Western and ITC Hotels explicitly welcome OpenAI's training crawler. Airbnb made the only major change in the sample, moving from allowing GPTBot and ClaudeBot in August to disallowing access across the board by October. Posted policies also diverge from enforcement: eight sites, mainly hotels, returned 403 Forbidden errors to Skift's automated requests, and CraftedStays found Marriott, Hilton, IHG, Hyatt, and Four Seasons name no AI crawler on their banned lists while their bot protection refused fetches outright. Adobe reported in July that AI visits to travel sites surged 119% year over year, and Adobe analyst Vivek Pandya wrote that significant portions of U.S. travel and retail sites remain unreadable to machines, leaving potential revenue on the table.

A Chef’s Guide to Eating and Drinking in Santo Domingo

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On a Friday night in July, Nidia Evangelista walked with her arms crossed over the Puente Flotante toward East Santo Domingo. Six lanes of traffic jockeyed inches from her left shoulder, while the black waters of the Río Ozama coursed below. “Oh my God, Oli, what are we doing?” she said. “Nobody does this.” Evangelista is the communications manager for Santo Domingo’s most intriguing restaurant, Casarré. Her boss, Swiss Dominican chef Olivier Bur, was leading Evangelista, photographer Clay Williams, and me beyond the fortified walls of the Zona Colonial, where few travel on foot.

On the other side, we turned a corner and found hundreds gathered around a dance floor in the undercroft of a bigger bridge. Live bachata drowned out the traffic overhead. Bur procured a big bottle of Presidente and plastic cups from a vendor, poured, and raised a “Salud!”

Bur normally walks (or bikes) everywhere. Moving slowly, he’s learned the textures of the city. Still, for the next six days, he took Williams and me to his most beloved restaurants by car. We visited a string of chimi stands where hawkers compete for clientele through the night; a chicharrón spot where couples dance shoeless on the cool tile floor; and a cafeteria where boys shuffle stewed eggplant, beets, and goat to bus drivers en route. Places like these are the “infrastructure for life,” Bur said. “Thirty years ago, there was nothing else.”

Over the last couple of decades, however, another dining scene has sprouted. Dominican chefs have returned from kitchens in Mexico, Japan, or Switzerland to explore the forgotten and sometimes shunned ingredients of their (or their parents’) homes. They served us conch, cassava, breadfruit, and djon-djon mushrooms in sashimis, foams, tostones, and surprising dessert creams. The dishes were fresh and exciting, but local and global traditions have always mingled—as such, the cuisine is not “New Dominican,” it’s simply “Dominican,” fully expressed.

Calle Andrés Julio Aybar 13+1 829 545 7503

The Piantini neighborhood was once verdant and sleepy, according to Bur. Now it’s a bustling business district. Most of the remaining vegetation decorates the façades of restaurants, like Ajualä’s. Brushed palm thatch and a bushy barba de Fidel (Fidel’s beard) tree greet you at the tall aged-steel door.

Venezuelan chef Saverio Stassi is a leader among the culinary new guard. His treatment of local ingredients is virtuosic. He confits guinea hen in cocoa butter and tops breadfruit tostones with local yellowfin and Cambita avocado; and over two days, his team bathes the succulent goat from the northwest—where they famously graze on wild oregano, self-infusing their meat—in Seville orange juice before roasting it in a barbacoa pit.

Calle Max Henríquez Ureña 14+1 809 547 3592

Chef Erik Malmsten has cooked at his parents’ cabaret in Gothenburg, Sweden, at Aquavit in New York, and at Bocuse d’Or competitions around the world. But to take his career to the next level, he returned to his mother’s homeland to open a Nordic-Japanese restaurant.

“You’ll see, it’s really good,” Bur told us. The meal starts with “lobster chicharrón,” with a reduced crustacean bisque, fried into airy orange sheets of bubbly crackling and served with tōgarashi Kewpie. Then, a parade of sashimi, like king fish, served with brown butter soy emulsion, ikura, and lemon zest. It ends with Dominican chocolates, molded, painted, and plated to look like koi fish on a synchronized swim.

If, like me, you come from a pizza region, you might not be inclined to use one of your Santo Domingo meal slots on a pie. But I urge you: Make time for Casa Gazcue. Spanish Dominican chef Francis Pena’s 48-hour-fermented crust comes blistered and sometimes covered in honeyed sesame seeds. Iterations are variously laden with spicy Dominican oxtail, chimi sandwich sauce, pistachio, and jamón ibérico, but you don’t have to choose: For groups, he serves a rosca (a round with an open middle) of six sections arranged like floral pizza petals.

In 1954, the Bonarelli family opened the legendary Italian restaurant Vesuvio. For decades, it was the place—the only place—to go out for a formal sit-down meal around here. Vesuvio closed over 20 years ago, but the founder’s grandson, Giancarlo Bonarelli, now runs Bottega Fratelli in a sleek modern building in Naco (Piantini’s equally upscale neighbor).

You’ll notice a great variety of beet dishes in the Dominican Republic. Farmers harvest them year-round in the cool, pine-forested Costanza Valley. Try them here, roasted and served with blue cheese, chèvre, candied nuts, and honey. Also try a carpaccio of king mackerel, caught off the eastern coast, or any of the risottos, an emblem of the family’s enduring Italian culinary influence.

Hideyoshi Tateyama’s parents opened Samurai in 1990. The restaurant joined Vesuvio, along with one or two others in that era’s small restaurant scene. It still stands out today among the many dozens.

Be sure to order the guatapaná con coco. A tried-and-true combo of shrimp and coconut imbued with native guatapaná leaf, tobiko, sesame seed, and scallion—it’s been on the menu for two decades. Also, don’t miss the chance to sample a sashimi of the meter-long “calamar diamante” from the Samaná region. It’s simply brightened with lemon zest. Meals are capped with a green tea amaro. Once a month, Tateyama personally runs a chef’s-choice omakase service.

On the day we visited Villar Hermanos, lines formed around the block for each of two buffets. Women balanced towers of takeout containers tightly wrapped in plastic. It was the World Cup Final, and people were rushing to get some of the city’s best comfort food to their coffee tables. “This is the place to come for all the Dominican classics,” Bur told us.

We found a large table under the sprawling canopy of a banyan tree and ordered mofongo. It was moist, punctuated with crisp chicharrón crackling. The sancocho, the classic Domincan stew thick with potato and yuca starch, assumed extra body when alternating spoonfuls of broth and rice. While I didn’t have my coveted mangú and tres golpes (salami, queso frito, and eggs), I would trade my house and car to live next door and eat breakfast there every morning under the filtered sun.

Avenida San Vicente de Paúl, Santo Domingo Este 11504+1 809 244 8906

On Avenida San Vicente de Paúl in East Santo Domingo, scooters zip down the busy street all through the night, many popping over a curb into the Plaza Del Chimi. The “plaza” is a stretch of  10 to 15 “chimi” sandwich stalls outfitted with griddles and prep stations heaped with roasted pork shoulders. Dealers quickly slice the meat, lay it on the griddle, flip it onto a squishy pan de agua roll, and top it with cabbage, tomato, and tangy pink sauce before sliding it into your mitts. The stalls are open till 6 a.m. When they run out of beer, a man runs bottles from a cooler at his house and serves them in paper bags.

Avenida Independencia KM 8+1 809 532 2580

Across from a shopping mall on Avenida Indepencia, about a dozen cars cram into five parking spaces for the lunch or late-night staple pica pollo. Behind a crowded serving station, a woman slides eight pounds of chicken into a hotel pan, and the picking begins. Six sets of tongs compete for room, like bird beaks, snapping the chicken into styrofoam containers before barely holding them shut with strained rubber bands. Another eight pounds comes out less than two minutes later.

Pica pollo is one of the lasting influences of Chinese immigrants in Santo Domingo. Usually cooks marinate it in bitter orange, garlic, and oregano before coating it in flour and spice (in this case we detected Chinese five-spice) and deep-frying it to golden brown.

At Parque Mirador Sur, teens and whole families perch along concrete walls and hilltops eating fried yuca and succulent mojo-dipped chicharrón with long toothpicks. It comes from the crowded, steel-barred serving window across the street: Chicharrones Light de la Cayetano.

Among all the chicharrón we ate in the city (there was a lot), theirs stood out. There’s a small covered seating area where you can order beer to share. Bachata plays loud over a hanging speaker. Women take turns kicking off their heels to do the four-step shimmy with their jevos (boyfriends).

The Colmado, Bur says, “is essential to Dominican life. Everything happens there.” For 70 years, Colmado La Venganza has anchored a prominent corner in the Don Bosco neighborhood. It’s hosted thousands of late-night domino games and sports viewing parties. We were there during the World Cup.

At the entrance, a man with a crisp-edged haircut, big sunglasses, and a pressed floral shirt parked his shoe on a milk crate while a boy polished it with a rag. Inside, groups spanning all ages gathered at high-tops and around the bar. Folks shared triple-stacked ham, pickle, and cheese sandwiches, pressed, cubed, and spiked with toothpicks: the “sándwich especial.” Leaving the bar, chef Raniel Cruz (part of our party) texted his mom in Montreal. She responded: “Wait, you’re at Venganza? I used to drink there.”

One couldn’t understand the Dominican Republic’s vast culinary splendor in a lifetime, but if you had just one night to try, you’d want to dine at Casarré. Every Tuesday, the team takes excursions. We accompanied them to meet the countryside cooks, who wrap guáyiga root masa in leaves from the almond trees on Najayo beach and bake it in coal-covered pots. We visited a produce market, where vendors passed fruit samples, like little red “frutas milagrosas” that, when chewed beforehand, make bitter limes taste like sweet limeade.

At night, in a candlelit, contemplative room in the Zona Colonial, Bur and his team showcase their findings in a series of sublime, thoughtful dishes. On our visit, they presented djon-djon mushrooms from the Haitian borderlands in a sweet, guáyiga-thickened foam and in the broth of a citrusy okra and fish soup. Chef Isabella Sánchez explained: “The broth is murky, like the current state of the Río Ozama. It represents the negative aspects of our water—contamination and conflict—and the beautiful: the fish, the beaches, and our grandmother’s tea.”

Smoke from an open Uruguayan-style parrilla floats in the soft light over two stories. Our waiter said, “We want this to feel like a Sunday asado.” Indeed, the smoke, the char, and the Malbec are there. This asado, though, also has Negronis smoked tableside and poured from a glass goose. As we awaited a perfect aged rib eye, we enjoyed paper-thin beef carpaccio with foie, and a paella-esque rice with soft leeks, cilantro, and pork trotters.

Recommendations on where to dine, shop, and stay, wherever you’re headed next.

Oliver Bur, the pioneering chef behind Casarré, offers an inside look at the Dominican capital’s restaurant scene.

On a Friday night in July, Nidia Evangelista walked with her arms crossed over the Puente Flotante toward East Santo Domingo. Six lanes of traffic jockeyed inches from her left shoulder, while the black waters of the Río Ozama coursed below. “Oh my God, Oli, what are we doing?” she said. “Nobody does this.” Evangelista is the communications manager for Santo Domingo’s most intriguing restaurant, Casarré. Her boss, Swiss Dominican chef Olivier Bur, was leading Evangelista, photographer Clay Williams, and me beyond the fortified walls of the Zona Colonial, where few travel on foot.

On the other side, we turned a corner and found hundreds gathered around a dance floor in the undercroft of a bigger bridge. Live bachata drowned out the traffic overhead. Bur procured a big bottle of Presidente and plastic cups from a vendor, poured, and raised a “Salud!”

Mortgage Rates Dip Ahead of a Big Week for Inflation Data

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In A Nutshell:  Mortgage rates are coming down as investors dial back expectations for rate hikes. But that could change: Key inflation data comes out this week.

Two highlights from a busy election week:

This week’s economics highlight is the CPI inflation data coming Tuesday, coupled with Friday’s PPI data. Together, the two will form a complete picture of the core PCE inflation measure the Fed uses. The consensus forecast has core CPI coming in at 0.2% month-over-month, down slightly from last month. Forecasters are expecting a small statistical distortion in the data due to last year’s government shutdown. A very low read for inflation might convince the Fed to hold off on hiking rates at the December meeting, but they could just as easily write it off as one data point. They would evaluate how broader financial conditions have changed since the last meeting, including any further improvement in rates.

Many Fed officials are holding speaking engagements this week. The ones to watch are Dallas Fed President Lorie Logan on Tuesday; Vice Chair Michelle Bowman on Monday, Tuesday, and Friday; Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack on Thursday; and New York Fed President John Williams on Thursday.

Chen Zhao is the head of economics research, where she produces research on the housing market for public and internal audiences. Previously, she was an executive director leading housing finance and financial markets research at the JPMorgan Chase Institute. Prior to joining JPMCI, Chen was an economics consultant at Analysis Group, Inc., where she worked on financial litigation cases and led teams conducting health economics and outcomes research on behalf of pharmaceutical companies. While in graduate school, Chen was with the Center for Economic Studies and the Social Economic and Housing Statistics Division at the US Census Bureau, where she conducted applied microeconomics research using large scale restricted-access linked survey-administrative data. She started her career at the White House Council of Economic Advisers, where she focused on labor and health economics.

Vitalik Buterin Backs an AI Slowdown as AI Tokens Slide 30%: Is the AI Crypto Bubble Popping?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Vitalik Buterin backs slowing the AI data center race. AI tokens fell 29.7% in 30 days, three times Bitcoin. Is the AI crypto bubble popping?

Vitalik Buterin said on Sunday that slowing the AI data center race is probably a good thing, as the crypto bubble around AI tokens loses air. The Ethereum co-founder made the case in a post on X.

He pushed back on what he called the “strong emotional reaction” against the anti-data-center movement.

I know this is a predictable opinion from me but I don't agree with the strong emotional reaction against the anti-data-center movements that I've seen this week.

As I see it, algorithmic progress means that whatever you would be able to do with more and bigger data centers in 2…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) November 8, 2026

That movement had a big week. On November 3, voters in 10 states decided 41 local ballot measures on data centers, Ballotpedia counted. A “yes” vote on 28 of them would limit building through bans or moratoriums. After the vote, Senator Bernie Sanders called for a “national data center moratorium now,” one of several Democrats pushing to rein in the AI buildout.

Crypto’s AI corner has felt the same chill. Seven of the largest AI tokens fell 29.7% on average over the past 30 days, almost three times Bitcoin’s 10.7% drop.

So is the slowdown Buterin wants already showing up as a popping bubble in AI tokens?

Discover: Bitcoin Slides to $73,510 Over the Weekend: Did Nvidia Scandal Trigger BTC’s $2,000 Flash Crash?

His case rests on three points, and he made it, in his words, “without any controversial ‘doomer arguments’.”

He concluded that “a slowdown in compute hyper-scaling seems quite net good.” Even if it proved a mistake, it would not be worse than “10+ other political things,” he added.

The view fits what Buterin has argued since 2023, when he set out “d/acc,” his name for speeding up defensive technology first, in an essay titled My techno-optimism.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best Low-Cap Crypto

AI tokens are the part of crypto that trades most directly on the AI boom. They include networks that sell computing power, run AI agents or pay people to train models. CoinGecko valued the sector at $18.9 billion on Monday morning.

We compared the 30-day moves of seven of the largest AI tokens on Binance with Bitcoin and Ether.

The selling lines up with doubts about AI spending in the stock market. Last Thursday’s sell-off in chip stocks pushed Bitcoin below its 200-day average, and AI tokens, which rose fastest on the AI story, have fallen fastest as it cools.

A wave of local data center bans adds a new worry for tokens that sell computing power. Buterin’s own argument cuts the other way, though: if better algorithms keep AI improving on today’s machines, a slower buildout does not end demand for AI.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best New Crypto to Hodl

It held on Sunday, but with little room to spare. Ether trades at $2,104 on Binance, down 16.1% in 30 days, after touching $2,060 late Sunday, its lowest price since August 19.

ETH sits below both of its daily exponential moving averages (EMAs), averages that weight recent prices more. They stand at $2,311 for 200 days and $2,370 for 50 days.

For traders asking whether the crypto bubble is bursting, AI tokens are the place to watch. They rose fastest on the AI story, and they are now falling fastest.

These Popular Bass Fishing Myths Were Debunked Decades Ago

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 9, 2026 5:04 PM EST

The hottest fishing news, tips, and tactics

“Missed him,” I said and groaned as I reeled in a slack line. Setting the hook after I had felt a gentle tap had produced nothing but a mangled plastic worm.

“Rerig and toss it back in there,” Ralph Manns directed me. “Another one might hit it.”

Manns was right. The next time I set the hook I was rewarded with a pleasantly bowed rod. After a short fight, a five-pound largemouth bass thrashed in the landing net.

“Now, you get one,” I said and grinned as my bass splashed in the live well.

Manns complied with a cast that dropped his worm beside the same rotted stump. The line twitched, the rod arched and he quickly landed a twin to the first bass. Not a bad start for our first look at Texas’ Lake Livingston—especially after my traumatic and almost sleepless night.

It had begun innocently enough with my arrival at Livingston’s Penwaugh Marina the previous evening for a meeting of the Texas Outdoor Writers Association. Luck of the draw had placed me in the same room with fisheries biologist Ralph Manns, a pleasant mild-mannered gentleman I was meeting for the first time. I had no idea that our meeting would alter the course of my fishing life.

An insatiable appetite for more facts about bass behavior had prompted Manns to become a full-time student at Southwest Texas State University after retiring from active duty in the Air Force where he had spent 20 years as an intelligence officer and a political scientist. At the time of his retirement he was a lieutenant colonel.

En route to a masters of science degree in aquatic biology, which he earned in 1981, Manns conducted a study of black bass in Lake Travis in 1977 and 1978. The study team used electronic transmitter tags and receivers to track 14 bass while divers recorded the behavior of another 935 bass. Manns’ team also used angling techniques to obtain detailed reports on an additional 289 bass. Data on more than 20 different types of behavior and the environmental conditions that might influence the fish were fed into a computer to compile and analyze the results.

Manns’ exhaustive studies of scientific literature on bass and his extensive two-year study have led to rather innovative and sometimes controversial conclusions. During my first few hours with him, he crumbled the foundation of my bass fishing knowledge with solid scientific evidence that contradicted many generally accepted fishing theories.

Those first two bass we caught from Lake Livingston, for example, were taken from classic bass structure—a stump on the upper edge of a submerged creek bed. The bass probably had not migrated from the depths, however, to feed in the shallower water as a popular myth would have us believe.

The hypothesis that bass live in deep water, migrate to shallow water on a daily basis to feed and then return to deep water along established migration routes is generally credited to Buck Perry, the gentleman rightfully recognized as the father of structure fishing. His methods of catching bass have been so successful that other anglers and writers have perpetuated his migration theories.

Ralph Manns agreed that such structures are good places in which to fish but the analytically minded biologist could find no scientific literature to substantiate a deep-water migration theory. Manns wanted a better explanation for the appearance of bass at certain underwater structure.

“Sure enough,” Manns related, “our tracking studies verified data available in at least 15 other studies that showed the predominant movements of black bass are parallel to the shorelines and contour lines and not perpendicular to them. The movement, moreover, usually is at a nearly constant depth.”

A national magazine recently published a story entitled “When the Sun is High, the Bass Go Deep”. The author surmised that because more bass are caught in deep water during midday hours, bass obviously migrate from shallow to deep water as the sun brightens. And that’s how myths get started.

Manns’ study of Lake Travis bass showed no such tendency.

“Our research showed some bass at shallow depths, some at medium depths and some in deep water at all times,” he revealed. “And they tended to remain at that particular depth whether active or inactive. Bass do move vertically but not routinely. A deep fish might pop to the surface to run through a school of shad, but it will grab two or three and almost immediately dive to its original depth because that’s where it has established its equilibrium and can suspend without effort.”

A bass can hover effortlessly at a particular depth because its air bladder is balanced at that depth and it gets out of balance if it varies much from that point. According to Manns, it takes hours for the air bladder to adjust to a major depth change. The more out of balance a bass gets, the harder it has to swim to maintain its stability.

Manns noted that black bass usually move along contour lines for only short distances and tend to stop at any bisecting lines of structure that obstruct their movement, such as a fence, stump or rock. By casting to a fence, an angler increases his odds simply because moving bass are likely to pause there.

Most anglers will catch more bass, Manns maintained, if they fish on the better bisecting structures for several minutes during activity periods. Additional schools often stop at the same structure long enough for the anglers to catch more fish.

Some fishermen consider schools of black bass a rarity. Limit catches might be rare but, according to Manns’ underwater observations, most bass are in either a school or an aggregation.

“There are almost no loner bass,” Manns says. “The few single fish we observed underwater were either very sick, very large or totally inactive. The sick ones obviously couldn’t keep up and the larger bass were probably unable to find compatible partners in their size range.

“Rule one,” he continued, “if you catch a bass, assume it is not alone. As a minimum, it is in a group of two to five fish. Actively striking bass are usually feeding and moving bass. They tighten up their aggregations and move in small schools.”

During activity periods, of course, almost anyone can catch bass by tossing an edible-looking lure in the path of a feeding school. It’s the inactive periods that separate expert from novice bass fishermen.

“Fishermen should remember that bass are inactive most of the time, even if their stomachs are empty,” Manns claimed. “Unlike humans who have a predictable three square meals a day, a bass may be forced to exist for days without eating. That little bit of minnow it ate three days ago must last the bass a long time and it does not waste it!”

The fact that a fish is inactive doesn’t mean that it won’t eat, Manns admitted. If something passes closely and in front of a bass’ mouth, it will usually eat because bass are opportunists. But an inactive bass probably won’t move more than a few inches to hit a lure. An angler who is fishing a spot that holds inactive bass may have to cast 15 or more times before the lure passes through that tiny strike zone in front of an inactive bass.

A fast-moving crankbait offers good odds of getting a lure to an active bass. Because active fish may chase lures for 10 feet or more, each cast effectively covers a wide path on each side of the bait’s track. The same lure retrieved past inactive bass, however, may pass by many fish without eliciting a strike.

The success of an angler’s technique, then, depends on how active the bass are. Anglers who cast and crank at wide intervals over inactive bass will usually get few strikes. Fishermen who flip worms or jigs to the base of cover objects where inactive bass may hold, however, get close enough to take a few fish. If the same anglers continue to flip slow lures while bass are in an active state, though, they cannot hope to hook as many as those with fast lures. Thus, an angler must match his lure and his technique to the activity level of the bass.

One widely accepted myth claims that bass avoid sunlight because they have no eyelids. Manns’ underwater observations indicate otherwise.

“It was clear to us that bass have no requirement to live in shade,” Manns declared. “Moving bass were as likely to stop at a sunny spot as a shaded one. Although bass have no eyelids, their eyes have pigment mechanisms that compensate for excessive light. Water is an excellent light filter, too. Unless a bass is in very shallow, calm and clear water, the water serves it well as a pair of sunglasses.”

Bass that anglers catch from beneath piers, boat houses and docks are probably there for cover or food rather than for shade. In fact, even slightly colored water refracts sunlight sufficiently to destroy any distinct shade line within a few feet. Even in moderately clear lakes, bass down more than 10 feet experience little or no shading from objects at the surface.

This means that bass a fisherman finds beneath floating structures will probably hold tightly to the bottom of the structure—unless, of course, there are other attractive structural elements on the bottom. Lures retrieved just beneath floating structures, then, will probably be more effective than lures retrieved along the bottom.

Although bass do not need to avoid sunlight, Manns’ records indicate that more bass are caught from shaded areas than from sunny ones. He stressed that this statistic is not contradictory because inactive bass may suspend in either shade or in sunny open water. But for an angler to cast his lure near enough to inactive bass to make them strike, he usually must find bass that are suspended close to an identifiable target. While the target creates shade, the shade itself is incidental and not the reason the bass are there.

Another concern of bass anglers is that bass will move when they hear noise coming from above them. Manns’ study, however, clearly showed that bass learn to ignore any sounds that don’t represent a threat. Bass frequently swam beneath docks where workers were pounding with hammers or sailors were stomping in boats. Bass also ignored outboard motor noises in Lake Travis, which is heavily used by recreational boaters. Surprisingly, however, some of the lake’s bass had learned to move away from boats with electric fishing motors and it was difficult to approach such bass unless they were pushed against cover objects.

Manns suggests that a great myth of angling literature is that bass seek out preferred temperatures, water clarity, structure and so on. He stressed that the part of a bass’ brain that processes every piece of visual or physical information is about the size of a thumbnail. A bass simply does not have the ability to be intuitive.

“A bass will not run across a lake to an area that has a better temperature level,” Manns claimed, “because it doesn’t know it’s there. Now if a bass is wandering around and finds a more comfortable area, it might think, ‘Wow! Isn’t this temperature nice?’ And if it can stay there without getting hungry, it may hang around long enough for the angler with a temperature meter to catch it.”

In Manns’ study, bass did not move to deeper water to avoid surface temperatures in the 84° to 88° range. In fact, when all other factors were equal, black bass (including smallmouths) preferred the water temperature to be between 78° and 86°, not the 72° that is popularized in most fishing literature. Manns’ findings are corroborated by at least 20 other scientific studies published during a 40-year period. Bass found in cooler water are there because of a better food supply or because they are unable to compete with larger predators present in warmer water.

Manns admits that there are optimum chemical environments for bass but in most lakes the fish must be satisfied with less. They merely move into someplace tolerable, adapt to it and once they’ve adapted to that condition, any change is a negative change to those fish.

“Most people don’t realize that a bass is constantly adapting to its environment,” Manns declared. “The chemistry of its body adapts to temperature and water conditions and, if any factors change, the fish is temporarily out of balance. When cold fronts go through, for example, sudden drops in temperature disturb the fish’s equilibrium and it becomes slightly sick until it adapts to the new condition. That helps explain why bass normally don’t bite as well after a cold front.”

A common dream of anglers is catching bass on almost every cast from a “hotspot” where hungry largemouths eagerly await the next cast. The nearest thing I ever found to that fabled “hotspot” was on Lake Sam Rayburn back in 1968. My partner and I found a timbered flat near a river channel where every cast with our crankbaits hooked a chunky bass. We tied the boat line to a limb and promptly caught 60 largemouth bass from an area no larger than the average living room. In that era of 15-bass limits it was the first chance I had to cull bass to improve a stringer.

Our hopes for a repeat performance the next day were short lived. Our first few casts hooked bass, but the action ceased and we never relocated the bass. Nor did any of our friends ever find fish there on many subsequent trips. Manns offered an explanation for the phenomenon.

“They were probably transient bass, which is a major cause of confusion to anglers. Bass are generally thought of as having one type of behavior pattern. Actually, though, there are two types of black bass: transient bass and home-range bass. The concept is an old one that shows up in scientific literature as far back as 30 years ago.”

Manns theorized that the fish’s health or its ability to catch prey determines whether it is a home-range or transient bass. Healthy fish establish territories and hold them, moving as little as possible. They conserve energy by feeding quickly and remain in the same general location unless there is a major change in water conditions or the food supply.

Large bass populations or shortages of prey create competition for food, however. Those bass that feed less efficiently must feed longer and move often to find forage. A transient is one because it is a loser.

There are many transient bass in our better bass lakes and they almost always school. An angler who finds such a concentration can catch great numbers of them at one time but he should not necessarily label the area as a great fishing hole. Such catches can be the result of chance meetings with transients.

Manns says that an angler should not spend much time returning to the site of a big catch unless he has reason to believe, such as repeated success, that the place is a home-range area. An angler can more consistently catch bass by returning to locations where he has taken one or two fish over and over again. Those are probably spots that have enough food to hold home-range fish day after day.

Manns stressed that bass reactions vary according to how hungry they are. A very hungry fish will probably strike at any lure it can get in its mouth. A bass that has been feeding on shad for 20 minutes, however, will probably only take something that is similar to the shad.

Although bass can see colors, anglers should remember that bass do not perceive them as a human would. Manns recalled a day when he and his partner followed a large school of bass feeding heavily on shad. The two cast all of the traditional shad-color lures without a strike. As soon as Manns put on a gold lure, though, he caught fish on almost every cast.

The bass obviously responded to some flash of gold from the shad caused, perhaps, by the angle of sunlight. The bass had enough color discrimination that they triggered to the tiny variation. Manns, therefore, suggests that anglers who are unsuccessful in catching obviously active bass should change lure colors, sizes and actions until they find one that triggers the fish. Bass are not ambush predators as are halibut, angler fish and sculpin; nor are they chase predators such as tuna or striped bass. A black bass is a compromise fish designed to do a lot of things—none of them perfectly but all of them well. The bass is a seeker with a short sprint. It enters cover for protection and to rest, not to ambush prey. Cover actually limits and hinders a bass’ ability to strike and that is why prey fish hide in such areas.

The standard hunting pattern of a bass is to move around structure objects to see if anything strays far enough from cover to become vulnerable. It assesses its ability to catch individuals of prey and strikes only at those it thinks can be caught. The bass ignores prey that are likely to dodge and thus escape. A bass will ignore a lure that looks like a healthy bluegill just as it would ignore a real bluegill under the same circumstances.

“Bass fishing is very mathematical, very predictable,” Manns claimed. “It is a problem to be solved. Most of the false concepts arise because writers intellectualize about bass. The reaction mechanism of bass is actually very very basic: a reaction to a stimulus and a response, nothing more.

Read Next: Old-School Tips for Catching Big Bass Right Now

“A bass can’t outsmart you. All you have to do is find something that will stimulate it, put it where it will stimulate it and you will catch a bass. There’s really nothing more to fishing than that.”

Manns makes it sound simple and it looks simple enough when you watch him in action. He merely works the odds, covering as much favorable water as possible with a wide variety of lures. But he seems to catch many more bass than the average fisherman. The years he spent in their living quarters are paying off.

This story was originally published in the November 1983 issue of Outdoor Life.

Police Say BMW M5 Street Racer Hid His Face and Plates, But Couldn't Hide His Identity Forever

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Authorities identified an allegedly masked BMW driver and arrested him at a Brampton, Ontario home after a three-month investigation.

It's no big secret that some folks treat driving laws like they're optional. To avoid getting busted for such behavior, some obscure their plates; others try to mask their face. One kid who allegedly did both around Toronto, Ontario is now facing serious charges because police still managed to track him down over the course of three months—and took his BMW away, to top things off.

Police in the Ontario region of Peel say they started tracking the suspect in August. On the 30th of that month, officers say they saw the driver of a red BMW M5 participating in a street race at 1:06 in the morning. It's unclear if they gave chase, but either way, they didn't catch the driver that day. Around a month later, police say they saw the car again; on September 26, it popped up at two different street racing locations as a participant, and the driver allegedly ran a red light in view of police. While they didn't snag the driver on that occasion, it appears authorities leveraged traffic cameras to start making a case. The driver was allegedly wearing a ski mask for much of his antics, and reportedly drove without a license plate.

A red BMW M sedan isn't exactly subtle or super-common. So, after a few months of investigation, police obtained a warrant and arrested Tarman Toor, a 24-year-old living in Brampton, and charged him with three counts each of dangerous operation, disguise with intent, and racing a motor vehicle. It's unclear if he's still in jail as of this story’s publication, but police say he has a meeting with a judge on the schedule in the near future.

Peel Regional Police say they they've arrested 119 people for traffic violations this year, and charged 214 with stunt driving in their area alone. Each one of those comes with a 14-day impound of the car in question and a 30-day license suspension for the driver. If convicted, Toor could face worse: several years in jail over just one of these charges, much less all nine of them.

2 Personality Traits Are Linked to Lower Inflammation

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Lower inflammation is associated with less fatigue, a lower risk of depression and a slower decline in reasoning as people age.

Lower inflammation is associated with less fatigue, a lower risk of depression and a slower decline in reasoning as people age.

People who score higher in conscientiousness or extraversion tend to have lower levels of certain markers of inflammation.

Among the qualities that make up conscientiousness, being hardworking and organised stood out.

For extraversion, which includes both sociability and energy, the clearest result was for people who described themselves as active.

These patterns matter because inflammation that stays elevated over time is linked to both physical and psychological health.

Lower levels of long-term inflammation are linked to a lower risk of heart disease, stroke and premature death.

They have also been associated with less fatigue, a lower risk of depression and a slower decline in reasoning as people age.

The researchers pooled data from six studies involving more than 19,000 adults in the United States, England and Japan.

Participants rated how well words such as ‘organised’, ‘hardworking’ and ‘active’ described them.

The researchers then looked at how those answers related to two markers of inflammation in blood samples, C-reactive protein and interleukin-6.

Earlier research had already linked higher conscientiousness with lower levels of both inflammatory markers (Luchetti et al., 2014).

In this study, people who described themselves as organised or hardworking tended to have lower levels of both markers.

Being hardworking showed the strongest association within conscientiousness, even stronger than the association for the overall trait score.

People who scored higher on extraversion tended to have lower C-reactive protein, and much of that association came from how active they said they were.

In fact, being active was more strongly associated with lower C-reactive protein than any other characteristic the researchers examined.

Being talkative showed a small association in the opposite direction, while being lively was associated with lower levels of both inflammatory markers.

In several of the studies, the association with being active held up even after the researchers took other aspects of extraversion into account.

Being active and hardworking may both reflect how much energy people have for everyday tasks.

In earlier research, people who scored higher on the activity aspect of extraversion or the achievement-striving aspect of conscientiousness tended to have better aerobic capacity during a brisk walking test (Terracciano et al., 2013).

Regular physical activity could help explain part of the association with inflammation.

The relationship could run in the other direction, too, if people with higher inflammation feel less energetic and come to see themselves as less active or lively.

The study was published in the journal Psychoneuroendocrinology (Stephan et al., 2026).

Psychologist, Jeremy Dean, PhD is the founder and author of PsyBlog. He holds a doctorate in psychology from University College London and two other advanced degrees in psychology. He has been writing about scientific research on PsyBlog since 2004. View all posts by Dr Jeremy Dean

Human sacrifices were rare in ancient Peru, except when weather got violent

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 9, 2026 5:00 PM EST

Today’s cataclysmic climate change-fueled disasters are scary enough even when we understand the root causes. Prehistoric cultures unfortunately didn’t have this luxury, and often responded dramatically to such changes. In northern Peru, archaeological evidence indicates local societies sometimes even turned to mass human sacrifices in an attempt to stop destructive flooding from events like El Niño.

That said, researchers are now confident these periods of intense violence weren’t the norm—nor were they necessarily linked to climate issues. According to their study recently published in Frontiers in Environmental Archaeology, such brutal moments were actually pretty rare occurrences in Peru’s Lambayeque Valley.

“The main thing that jumped out at us was just how few cases of interpersonal violence we observed outside ritualized contexts,” said New York University archaeologist and study co-author Thomas Snyder.

The Lambayeque Valley is located inside northern Peru’s coastal desert, but the region has hosted residents for  6,000 years. Although the valley generally receives little rainfall, its rivers and proximity to the sea allowed communities to flourish there for generations, including societies like the Moche from roughly 100–750 CE, and the Sicán from about 750–1375 CE.

“Archaeological evidence shows that in so many ways they were mirrors of us—in how they lived their daily lives, interacted with their world, ran their economies, and confronted (and sometimes contributed to) environmental crises,” explained George Mason University archaeologist and study co-author Haagen Klaus.

Previous excavations have indicated that mass human sacrifices possibly preceded the collapse of the Moche and Sicán’s political systems. Researchers have since hypothesized that these acts likely coincided with periods of mass flooding,probably due to El Niño weather oscillations. Just like today, these weather events destroyed food stocks and disrupted agricultural production. Since large human sacrificial rituals may have been attempts by the ruling class to restore balance to nature, the team to wonder if violence was simply a more common facet of everyday life for the Moche and Sicán.

“If these big ritual sacrifices were indicative of some underlying crisis, we hypothesized that these events might have been extending into people’s everyday experience of the world,” said Snyder.

Klaus, Snyder, and their collaborators turned to the Lambayeque Valley Biohistory Project’s skeletal archive for answers. The collection spans about 3,000 years of history across 30 different archaeological sites. The team established relatively precise death estimates for over 1,600 individuals, while also examining them for evidence of intentional cranial trauma associated with human sacrifice. Contrary to their theory, only 25 remains (1.6 percent of the sample pool) showed signs of violence, while just 77 displayed indicators of accidental trauma. Of those, none were directly linked to known periods of past ecological crises.

“In Lambayeque, there’s 6,000 years of creative adaptations to El Niño and longer-term changes in climate, and widespread violence wasn’t one of them,” concluded Klaus.

The study’s authors noted that because they were notable to radiocarbon date all of the remains, their findings remain broader estimates of wide time periods. At the same time, they stressed the evidence underscores humanity’s history of facing intense climate challenges not with violence, but adaptation.

“This really highlights that widespread violence isn’t a predetermined response to climate change,” said Snyder. “As a society, we both get to and have to choose how we respond to our own climate crisis.”

“Violence and social unraveling are not destined outcomes, ever,” added Klaus.

The thing that I really love about this automatic feeder is that I have been able to adapt it to my specific needs. The design of most automatic cat feeders means that food is dispensed into a bowl, with little flexibility for serving multiple cats.

Over time, my cats have learned that I am not their source of breakfast, and instead of harassing me from 4 am, they simply gather around the Automatic Cat Feeder until the food drops at half past five. They enjoy a predictable feeding schedule, and I enjoy a couple more hours of uninterrupted sleep.

The impact of this device really appreciates over time.

This air purifier is so quiet that I occasionally have to check itâs actually on! The amount of fur and dust it collects every week is incredible. With 9 furry creatures to contend with, I wasnât expecting a fur-free home, but it has definitely reduced the amount of fur in and around the cat room. What isnât drawn into the unit itself gathers as little furry tumbleweeds nearby, making cleaning much easier.

In terms of odor reduction, the Oneisall Air Purifier really makes a difference as part of my 3-pronged attack against stinky litter boxes:

Premium diet and good hydration to reduce odors at their source.

Optimal air purifier and litter box juxtaposition.

Maximized litter box hygiene and odor-trapping with an automatic litter box.

The only thing I hate about having a self-cleaning litter box is thinking about all the years I didnât have a self-cleaning litter box. So much time spent scooping and sifting. Resenting my catsâ every urination and bowel movement.

These days, instead of five litter boxes that needed daily attention, I have two Ease S1 Self-Cleaning Litter Boxes. Once or twice a week, I empty the waste drawer, replace the waste bag, top up the litter, and give everything a brush or wipe. Once a month, I do a more thorough cleaning, which takes no longer than cleaning a regular litter box.

Before upgrading, my cats had several large, top-entry litter boxes. Like many cat parents, I had (selfishly) opted for enclosed litter boxes because they meant reduced odors and less litter spillage. Why selfish? Because the uncomfortable truth is that enclosed litter boxes are designed for human comfort, not feline preference.

It was immediately apparent when I introduced the open-top self-cleaning litter box from Oneisall that all my cats preferred to âpoo with a viewâ.

Cats are at their most vulnerable when toileting, and, unlike us, prefer to have a clear view of the world when they do their business. In multi-cat households, itâs not uncommon for cats to ambush each other as they exit a closed-in litter box. Sometimes itâs playful, sometimes itâs opportunistic bullying. Either way, it can make skittish felines even more reluctant to use the provided privy and potentially opt for an alternative location of their choosing.

The other issue is that while we enjoy the benefits of any overpowering odors being trapped inside an enclosed box, our cats are forced to breathe in all the aerosolized fecal matter and toxic ammonia gas every time they go to the toilet. Just because they produce it doesnât mean they want to inhale it.

With the Ease S1 Self-Cleaning Litter Box, waste is locked away, along with the smell. I did empty the waste drawer more frequently over summer as the hot weather naturally increases the amount of ammonia in the air, which is to be expected.

Have I noticed an increase in the amount of cat litter on my floor? Yes, a bit. Although the litter trapping mats provided do help reduce litter tracking, I am definitely vacuuming more litter than I did in the past. And do I care? Heck no.

A bit of extra vacuum cleaning is a small price to pay for happier cats that are guaranteed to be met with a fresh litter tray for every visit.

For those who really hate the idea of a bit more litter on the ground, you can purchase an alternative litter guard from the Oneisall website for using pellet-type substrates, like tofu litter, which tends to track less.

The trouble with most product reviews is that they are rarely performed over a long enough period of time for any major issues to appear. After spending almost a year with my cat room kitted out with Oneisall products, I can honestly say that there are just three things I would change if I could:

Itâs petty and unimportant, but it still bothers me that the control unit is a creamy off-white, while almost everything else in the product range is either black, grey, or white. Just, why?

A fantastic and super important feature of the Oneisall Ease S1 Self-Cleaning Litter Box is the various motion sensors, which prevent the cleaning cycle from starting if there is a cat in or near the box, and pause the cycle if the sensors detect motion nearby. I have found that, compared to some others, the sensors on this unit are particularly sensitive. They react when I walk past the door, and I have had the occasional tantrum when one of the sensors was detecting debris or imbalance or maybe a ghost. Overall, an overabundance of caution is always better than not enough, particularly when it comes to the safety of our pets, and though I am loathe to accuse anyone or anything of being too sensitive, I think they could probably benefit from a little bit of tweaking.

The waste drawer under the self-cleaning litter box comes out the side of the unit, something I just thought of as mildly irritating until I recently tried out a similar (but much smaller) unit with a front-opening waste drawer. This might seem like a minor problem, but depending on the location and positioning of your litter box, it does mean needing to shuffle and shimmy things around when it comes time to empty the drawer.

It did take me an embarrassingly long time to realize that the drawer can be inserted on either side, which makes it a bit easier, but if youâre short on space, a front-opening drawer would be better. A minor complaint but worth mentioning.

For the best product range and customer service experience, head to the Oneisall website. You can also find Oneisall products online and in-store from reputable retailers like Amazon, Chewy, PetSmart, Target, and Walmart.

Iâm sure some super-skittish scaredy-cats might be horrified the first time they witness their litter box rotating, and mine were certainly curious at first. Within 24 hours, these technological wonders had been accepted as just âpart of the furnitureâ. When I first introduced the automatic litter box, as recommended, I kept their old box available for the first week to give them time to adjust. Suffice it to say that with five cats, I only had to scoop the old litter box twice in that week.

Just the other day I watched Clutch walk up to the litter box whilst it was in the middle of a cleaning cycle, causing it to pause. He jumped in to relieve himself, apparently unconcerned that the tray was sitting at about a 30o angle! Not only did this demonstrate the importance of the Ease S1 Self-Cleaning Litter Boxâs safety features, but it also showed how unbothered cats can be once something ânewâ becomes part of their everyday life.

All the products I have tried and tested from Oneisall are cleverly designed and have, so far, stood the test of time. Theyâre not the cheapest on the market, but they are most certainly not the most expensive, and considering the attention to detail and quality I have experienced firsthand, I think Oneisall is excellent value for money.

Absolutely! In fact, I recently bought myself a second Oneisall Air Purifier so I could have one upstairs and downstairs.

Itâs always difficult to cut through the noise of ads, reels, product reviews, and sponsored content to work out what to buy. Even with a Trustpilot score as impressive as Oneisallâs, you can never be sure how long people have been using a product before they post a review, which is why I wanted to take the opportunity to revisit this wonderful brand.

When it comes to kitting out my cat room, I donât just want the cheapest stuff, but I also donât like spending a fortune on designer brands or unnecessary bells and whistles. What I like about Oneisall is that their designs put the needs and comfort of pets first, with user-friendly products that are practical and affordable.

One year on, Iâm glad I discovered Oneisall, and more importantly, so are my cats.

The Unexpected Gift That Everyone Will Be Fighting Over At Your Holiday White Elephant

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Turns out, the most steal-worthy gift might also be the most practical.

White Elephant gifts fall into two categories. There’s the novelty item that gets a huge laugh, takes one triumphant lap around the room, and disappears into the black hole at the back of your closet. Then there’s the unexpected G.O.A.T everyone clamors for. It’s useful. It’s clever. It leaves everyone thinking “Why didn’t I think of that?”

This year, we’re making a case for a sleeper hit: a pack of Energizer® Ultimate Lithium® AA batteries. Hear us out.

Sure, batteries might not spark the immediate razzle-dazzle of a mini karaoke machine or a candle shaped like a plate of spaghetti. But they do have something arguably more powerful: the ability to solve a future problem. These pocket-sized powerhouses keep your life going. Especially when we’re talking about the holidays.

Picture it. It’s December. The house is lit up like a regional airport runway. Your kid has acquired approximately 11 new objects that beep, flash, sing, or inexplicably do all three. Somewhere in your home, a tiny screwdriver is about to become the most important object you own.

In the midst of all this, the question hits you. Did you remember to buy batteries? Exactly. Why should you stock up on Energizer® batteries for this year’s white elephant? Let us count the ways.

There’s something deeply satisfying about having an aggressively stocked battery drawer. It’s the domestic equivalent of having a full tank of gas and remembering to defrost the chicken before 5 p.m.

Energizer® Ultimate Lithium® AA batteries have a 25 year shelf life, which means your White Elephant contribution could conceivably still be hanging around when today’s toddlers are complaining about their own toddlers.

That also makes them the rare holiday gift that comes with absolutely no urgency. Nobody needs to exchange them for another size. Nobody has to smile politely while wondering whether the gift giver simply regifted the item. Nobody needs to dig it out of the back of the dresser to wear the one time the gifter comes by a year.

They call it the battery drawer for a reason. And one day, perhaps years from now, you open it in a moment of need and discover them sitting there. Cause you planned ahead.

December has a way of revealing just how much of your home is quietly powered by AA batteries.

The flameless candles? Batteries. The light-up garland? Batteries. The singing stuffed reindeer Grandma bought your child despite your explicit request for “nothing with sounds”? You better believe that thing needs batteries.

Just when you thought you were out of the woods, Christmas morning arrives and ushers in a fresh crop of tiny battery-powered overlords. Race cars. Remote controls. Games. Noise-making toys that leave you wondering whether the toy’s product designer has ever met a parent.

Energizer® Ultimate Lithium® is the #1 longest-lasting AA battery, designed to keep the devices your family relies on going and going.

Is it slightly ironic to give someone batteries that could keep their kid’s noisiest toy going and going? Yes. That’s the risk you take.

So batteries? Batteries are getting forgotten.

They are perhaps the quintessential “Oh, shoot!” holiday purchase. The thing you realize you need precisely when every store is closed, and a child is staring at you with the devastated expression of someone whose brand-new toy is apparently just a decorative object now.

Which is why Energizer® Ultimate Lithium® AA batteries make such a sneaky-good White Elephant gift. You’re not really giving someone batteries. You’re giving them the ability to skip one future panic trip to the store.

So yes, wrap up the batteries. Everyone can laugh when they’re opened. Until the snatching starts.

California voters reject measure to shred environmental red tape

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

California voters voted down Proposition 45, which sought to speed CEQA environmental reviews through standardized deadlines and faster court timelines. The measure lost 56% to 44% in unofficial returns, and the streamlining fight is expected to return to the Legislature.  AI Summary

California voters have rejected a ballot measure to speed environmental review of housing and other projects.

Proposition 45 would have consolidated a patchwork of legislative fixes to the California Environmental Quality Act, aimed largely at reducing a costly hurdle to building housing. The Associated Press called the race over the weekend, though the state is still counting votes. Unofficial returns showed about 56% of voters opposed and 44% in favor, a spread too large to overcome with the remaining votes.

It failed decisively across populous coastal counties, where housing affordability is a pressing concern. Voters in Santa Cruz, Marin and Sonoma counties rejected the measure by the widest margins.

Opponents tied the measure to data center development, an influential argument in midterm races nationwide.

“Californians have sent a clear message to corporate developers, utilities and data center builders that they will not stand for a broad assault at the ballot box on state law protecting our air, water and natural resources,” the Vote No on Prop 45 campaign released in a statement.

Proponents dubbed that an inaccurate scare tactic.

“The opposition relentlessly lied, falsely claiming that Prop 45 covered data centers when non-partisan experts agreed it did not – and used smokestack images and scare tactics about air and water pollution when the ‘essential projects’ included in Prop 45 did not include smokestack industries and also excluded mining, port, and other controversial projects,” Jennifer Hernandez, who leads Holland & Knight’s West Coast land-use and environmental group, told HousingWire TBD.

Opponents also countered the claim that the measure would help create affordable housing. They cited housing groups that urged a no vote or declined to endorse. Abundant Housing LA and California YIMBY, both of which endorsed L.A. Mayor-elect Nithya Raman, did not endorse the measure.

Opponents included the California Democratic Party, the League of Women Voters and the American Lung Association. Sierra Club California and the California Federation of Labor Unions also opposed it.

The California Chamber of Commerce sponsored the measure, with backing from home-builder groups and some affordable housing advocates.

“We were outspent but not outgunned, thanks to a broad bipartisan coalition that understood that Californians know we can have progress and safeguard our environment at the same time,” the campaign noted in its statement.

Had it passed, the measure would have capped full environmental impact reports at 365 business days. Court challenges would have faced a 270-day limit.

Covered “essential” projects included housing, water, energy, schools, health facilities and transportation. Applicants could have chosen the new procedures or the existing ones.

The Legislative Analyst’s Office said the measure could cost local governments up to $100 million a year at first. Long-term financial effects were unclear. A Chamber-commissioned analysis projected $1.5 billion in savings and added revenue over 10 years.

Prop. 45 followed a run of legislative changes that limited CEQA reviews of housing proposals. Gov. Gavin Newsom signed Assembly Bill 130 and Senate Bill 131 in 2025. They exempted qualifying infill housing from CEQA and narrowed review for some projects falling short of an exemption.

A newer round of bills signed this fall targets more permitting delays. Some shield specific developments from further CEQA challenges. One is a major mixed-use development in San Diego.

Backers argued those fixes helped only some projects. They said project-specific exemptions left other developments exposed to years of delay. Prop. 45 would have applied common deadlines to all eligible projects.

Supporters said predictable timelines would help California build homes and the infrastructure that serves them. They said CEQA lawsuits add tens of millions of dollars in costs, and thwart efforts to increase housing supply by escalating entitlement risk. They also argued that the measure could cut delays by two to 10 years.

“Not-in-my-backyard” and environmental groups have long sued local governments under CEQA. Those suits typically argue agencies did not properly handle environmental impact reviews.

A study by Hernandez and other researchers reviewed 498 CEQA appellate decisions from 2013 through 2023. Agencies prevailed in 75%. But the study said prolonged litigation still stalls financing and raises costs. It covers appellate decisions only and predates the 2025 reforms.

Opponents said the 2025 reforms already addressed much of CEQA’s housing problem. They said Prop. 45 would cap public-comment periods, narrow tribal consultation and limit judicial review. They also argued that affordability was being used as cover for a developer advantage.

Critics said “essential” was defined so broadly that it could fast-track freeways, power plants and pipelines.

The fight to streamline CEQA may now shift back to the Legislature and a new governor.

“We know this fight is not over,” the No on Prop 45 campaign wrote in its statement.

“The good news is that many millions of Californians voted for Prop 45, and do want timely, accountable, and predictable outcomes in CEQA,” Hernandez said. “Our legislative supporters saw the dirty trick campaign tactics unfold, and now have the opportunity to adopt these important CEQA reforms for the ‘essential projects’ that California needs to restore affordability to our state.”

There's an Advent Calendar for (Almost) Everything

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Souped-up Advent calendars are here to stay this holiday season, with just about every big player in food and beverage getting in on the trend.

Experts say there’s a mix of factors at play as for their rise in popularity: little treats and micro indulgences in an expensive world, the idea of “saving big” on high-value bundles, and the sensory satisfaction of unboxing 25(ish) little boxes leading up to Christmas.

“There’s something so special about having a small treat to look forward to each day,” Sockerbit Scandinavian candy founders Florence Baras and Stefan Ernberg said in a statement. Sockerbit is one of several newbies to the food Advent calendar space this year (and one of our new faves).

Advent Center, a hub for data and reviews for thousands of Advent calendars globally, says there are around 250 food and drink calendars on the market this year. The category makes up about a third of the 2026 calendars it lists.

Max Zeshut, Advent Center’s founder, said food and drink calendars work well because they’re a cheaper, noncommittal entry point into the ritual, compared to, for instance, a beauty box. And for all the new and zany iterations out there, chocolate calendars remain an anchor.

Broadly, the global Advent calendar market is anticipated to grow from $1.32 billion in 2024 to $2.47 billion by 2033, according to research compiled by Accio, a B2B platform. Food and drink calendars hold a huge share of that, with the help of mainstays like Bonne Maman, whose annual calendars have inspired “Jamvent” fan groups for a decade, alongside newcomers that announced inaugural calendars this season.

“As a small team, we try to be very conscious of the operational demands of every product launch,” Fishwife CEO and co-founder Becca Millstein told Bon Appétit. “Still, the demand [for calendars] grew too loud to ignore, so we decided to go for it.”

Millstein cited Fishwife’s success in the online gifting space as motivation to launch a calendar this year. Fishwife’s includes 12 doors of tinned fish and extras, retailing for $150. The company said the calendar’s “elements of surprise and delight” drives chatter and user-generated content on social media.

“In the end, we simply had to do it,” Millstein said.

When the Sichuan condiment company Fly By Jing’s Advent calendar launched in 2024, it sold out in about a month. That’s when founder Jing Gao knew the brand was onto something.

Gao said that 65% of its Advent calendars were purchased by new customers this year and that historically, 20% of customers who purchase a calendar go on to make another Fly By Jing purchase by the end of the year.

“To me, that really speaks to the power of discovery and giving people a chance to explore our flavors in a fun, unexpected way,” Gao said. “We never want that sense of discovery to go away, even for people who have been with us from the beginning. It's become such a fun creative outlet for our team. I think that joy really comes through in the experience.”

The stewards at Onyx Coffee Lab in Arkansas describe a similar phenomenon. “There's so much storytelling, education, and human connection surrounding coffee, and Advent gives us a unique opportunity to explore those things in ways that might not make sense within our everyday offerings,” Onyx’s co-founder Andrea Allen said. Their box features 24 different coffees, each a 56-gram package of whole beans that are roasted on a schedule for peak brewing by their respective reveal day.

At Bonne Maman, Advent calendar planning starts two years in advance. The development team pays close attention to seasonal ingredients and market trends to produce 24 limited-edition jams and spreads made just for the calendar. The result sells out every year, with production growing by 400% since 2017, according to a spokesperson. This year will mark 10 years of the Bonne Maman Advent calendar.

“We’re grateful to the fans who have made it part of their holidays,” a company spokesperson said. “On social media, we see everything from matcha pairings and coffee creations to new takes on toast, along with plenty of flavor-by-flavor reviews. Many fans also write to us to tell us which flavor they loved most. It helps us decide which flavors should return or come in larger sizes.”

Have we stretched Advent calendar hype to its absolute limits? Advent Center’s Zeshut says no.

“The clearest sign of demand is that people search for calendars that don't exist,” he said. “There's no official Monster Energy calendar this year, and 'monster Advent calendar' is still one of the most searched terms that brings people to our site,” he said. “I don't think we've reached peak calendar, but we may have reached peak chocolate.”

Can Venezuela Build a Different Kind of High-End Tourism?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: Los Roques, an archipelago in Venezuela. Wikimedia Commons / Tucanrecords

Adriana Cisneros sees an opportunity for Venezuela to build a distinctive, conservation-minded tourism industry before the international hotel brands arrive. Whether that vision becomes reality will depend on political stability, security, and crucially, the choices made by the country's tourism leadership.

Venezuela is hardly an obvious candidate for the next great luxury travel destination. After decades of political and economic turmoil, the country faces deteriorating infrastructure, significant security risks, and an uncertain political future. The U.S. State Department advises Americans to reconsider travel, with stronger warnings against visiting several regions, including parts of the Amazon.

But Adriana Cisneros, the Venezuelan businesswoman whose family group has interests spanning media, real estate, and hospitality, believes the country has an unusual opportunity to build something different.

She estimates it could be eight years before Venezuela sees a Four Seasons, Rosewood, or Capella. Rather than waiting for the conventional luxury hotel industry to arrive, she imagines a network of four or five base camps connected by small aircraft and boats, offering access to the country's remarkable natural environments.

Wanderlust follows the latest stories in tourism and experiences from across the globe.

By submitting this form, you agree to receive email communication from Skift.

Tags: On Experience, south america, venezuela

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Adriana Cisneros, whose family group has interests in media, real estate, and hospitality, told Skift that Venezuela could skip the conventional luxury hotel playbook and build a network of four or five ultra-high-end tented camps linked by small aircraft and boats. She estimates it could be eight years before a Four Seasons, Rosewood, or Capella opens in the country, and she sees aircraft such as Cessna Caravans and Pilatus PC-12s serving remote areas rather than waiting for roads and airports to be rebuilt. This is not an announced development program. The obstacles are substantial: Venezuela's political transition is unresolved with no confirmed election timetable, the U.S. State Department advises Americans to reconsider travel and applies its highest warning to some of the remote regions central to the concept, and reliable electricity, water, healthcare, and emergency response cannot be taken for granted. Cisneros anticipates more risk-averse wealthy travelers could take another two or three years to feel comfortable visiting. The country is not starting from zero: independent operators, family-owned posadas, and lodges including Waku Lodge and Campamento Ucaima have kept tourism alive through decades of underinvestment. Cisneros points to her Tropicalia project in the Dominican Republic, where a forthcoming Four Seasons sits within a conservation-focused development, as her training ground.
