Do you want a garden that looks better every single year? Plant these native perennials, then, stat!

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

All hail native perennials! They are, after all, the easiest way to create a garden that comes back year after year, growing fuller, more colorful and more beautiful with each passing season, right?

No gardener worth their salt wants to start all their hard work from scratch every spring, and why should they? If you can build a garden that gradually finds its rhythm, with established plants returning reliably, filling out their spaces and creating a landscape that feels more natural and abundant over time, it's a no-brainer to make native plants your mode of attack!

Perennials are especially wonderful plants to build that kind of garden around; if you go native, they can deal with local conditions, making them relatively easy to grow once established. They also offer something beyond good looks, providing food and habitat for bees, butterflies, birds and other wildlife.

From cheerful summer blooms to delicate grasses, birth month perennials to lush woodland foliage, these seven natives bring a lovely mix of color, texture and seasonal interest.

Plant them in the right conditions, give them time to establish, and they'll reward you with a garden that gets better with every growing season. You can't say better than that, right?

Coneflowers are one of those plants that make a garden feel instantly more welcoming. Their daisy-like flowers are drought-tolerant and able to withstand an awful lot of neglect, which shifts them firmly into the almost too-good-to-be-true category of native perennials.

For a particularly beautiful display, go for the Magnus Purple Coneflower from Nature Hills; it promises big bold blooms that never droop, and its flowers attract useful pollinators, while the seedheads provide a valuable food source for birds later in the season, not to mention lots of structure and interest through the colder months.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

If you love a garden filled with sunny color, coreopsis is a wonderful native perennial to consider. Its cheery flowers look delicate, but these little bloomers are tough and incredibly drought-tolerant... even if you're garden gets to that point in the summer when it resembles a frying pan.

I especially love the Moonbeam (available at Nature Hills), which produces masses of buttery-yellow flowers over a long summer flowering season. Its understated colour makes it particularly useful for combining with stronger shades of purple, pink and orange.

Not every plant in a beautiful native perennial garden needs to produce traditional flowers; sometimes, it's the texture, movement and changing seasonal appearance of a plant that make it special. And pink muhly grass? Well, this pretty cloud-like ornamental brings all three to the table, and then some.

Bag yourself some from Nature Hills and plant it in a sunny position with well-drained soil. Once established, you'll find that it is relatively drought-tolerant and doesn't require much fuss (so long as you avoid overly wet or poorly drained locations, particularly during winter).

A beautiful garden needs more than flowers, and an ostrich fern is an especially lovely choice if you dream of adding a lush woodland feel to your yard thanks to its feather-like fronds.

Unlike the sun-loving flowers elsewhere on this list, ostrich fern (which you can buy at Nature Hills) prefers a cooler, shadier position with consistently moist soil. In fact, like most ferns, it is usually at its happiest beneath trees or along the edges of shaded garden beds, so long as it has enough moisture.

Bee balm is always an excellent addition to a garden, as its shaggy flowers are packed full of color and nectar, making them very popular with hummingbirds (and therefore a great way to try and lure your birth month hummingbird into play).

For a native planting, Monarda fistulosa, commonly known as wild bergamot, promises lavender-pink flowers with a fantastic aroma. If you prefer a more dramatic splash of color, the Jacob Cline variety is all about those bright red blooms, while the Spotted Bee Balm does exsctly what it says on the tin.

Few perennials bring as much cheerful late-summer color as black-eyed Susan. Its golden-yellow petals are basically sunshine in floral form, and they are brilliant for filling out borders in hotter, drier conditions... not to mention blooming for months on end.

It grows best in full sun, tolerates a range of soil conditions and is all but catnip to pollinators. Well worth splashing out on something like the Glitters Like Gold Black - Eyed Susan from Nature Hills so you can sit back and watch it do its thing.

Anise hyssop is one of those gorgeous lavender dupes that offers something lovely in almost every respect. It has attractive aromatic foliage, tall flower spikes, a wonderful ability to attract pollinators, and some USDA Zones may find that this one keeps on going until the end of October (especially if you deadhead spent flowers).

Anise hyssop is a brilliant wildlife-friendly plant that keeps blooming all fall.

Try planting anise hyssop alongside coneflowers, coreopsis and black-eyed Susans (all of which appear on this list, handily!) for a colorful and super pollinator-friendly border with plenty of height and texture.

One of the greatest pleasures of growing native perennials is watching a garden develop over time. Just take care to choose plants that suit your garden's conditions rather than trying to make every plant grow in the same environment if you go down this route.

It's also worth combining plants with different flowering seasons, not to mention leaving some seedheads standing for winter interest. Over time, you'll find that your garden doesn't just look better for it; you'll also find that it needs less intervention and begins to take on a character of its own as familiar flowers return each spring, pollinators visit throughout summer, and new combinations of color and texture emerge.

Are native perennials basically a miracle plant, then? Well, no... but they're as close to one as you're ever likely to get! Anything with a 'plant, sit back, relax' vibe to it is always going to prove a winner over time.

Kayleigh is an enthusiastic (sometimes too enthusiastic!) gardener and has worked in media for over a decade. She previously served as digital editor at Stylist magazine, and has written extensively for Ideal Home, Woman & Home, Homes & Gardens, and a handful of other titles. Kayleigh is passionate about wildlife-friendly gardening, and recently cancelled her weekend plans to build a mini pond when her toddler found a frog living in their water barrel. As such, her garden – designed around the stunning magnolia tree at its centre – is filled to the brim with pollinator-friendly blooms, homemade bird feeders, and old logs for insects to nest in.

Google Ads Tests Asset Activity Page With Weekly AI Insights via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Guide: AI Max Drove 68% Of Retail Invalid Clicks

Some Google Ads accounts show an Asset activity page that uses AI to deliver weekly insight cards on ad creative, including headline ideas to test.

Some Google Ads accounts are showing a beta page called Asset activity, which gives advertisers weekly insight cards about their ad creative.

Google Ads practitioner Anthony Higman posted a screenshot on LinkedIn on Nov. 6, calling it an AI feature he likes, and asked whether everyone had it. In his screenshot, the cards suggest new headline themes, point to top-performing headlines and report an increase in searches for legal consultation guidance. The only reply to his post, as of Nov. 9, said no.

When I checked on Nov. 9, I couldn’t find any mention of Asset activity on either Google Ads Help or the Google Ads announcements page.

Asset activity is located in the Assets section of the Google Ads menu (between the Assets report and Videos) and has a Beta label.

The insight cards on the page carry a weekly update label. Each card includes a category, date range, headline and View details link, plus feedback options.

In Higman’s screenshot, the cards can be filtered by four different categories, named Featured, Asset performance, Asset diversity and Industry trends.

His Asset diversity card is titled “Add financial recovery headlines to attract injured riders” and is marked Oct. 21-27. His Asset performance card is titled “Higher clickthrough rate for top-rated headlines” and contains a section entitled Top-performing headlines.

His Industry trends card reports that the number of search queries related to seeking legal consultation guidance has increased. It recommends creating headlines with simple consultation details.

Between his cards and the Weekly activity panel is a message saying that this tool uses artificial intelligence and could be providing users with inaccurate information. The panel shows that the most current week is Oct. 19-25. Because the screenshot ends there, it doesn’t show what data is reported by the panel.

Google Ads already tracks asset performance and updates in many areas.

Google has a help section (Performance Max asset reporting), which says that the Assets report will allow you to compare performance by asset, asset group or one or more campaigns. The Last updated field provides the date for when an asset was last changed (added, modified, paused or removed).

Search trends on the Insights page provide categories related to your business that have shown large increases in searches from one period to the next.

Ask Advisor (Gemini-based advisor within Google Ads) is also in beta; it takes asset audit requests and provides examples of what you can ask about like “How am I performing against my peers?”

Asset activity collects insights about specific headlines on one page and refreshes them each week.

The first metric listed on the Asset performance card in Higman’s screenshot is click-through rate (CTR). Google lists CTR, CPC, CPA and ROAS at the asset level as “directional indicators only” because assets are shown together. This guidance applies to Google’s existing asset reports, and the screenshot does not show how these new cards determine their findings.

You should use a CTR-based card as a starting point to test a headline and then review the results at the asset group or campaign level, which are the levels where Google suggests you evaluate performance. Before keeping a headline suggested by the Asset diversity or Industry trends cards, check how it converts in your own account.

As of Nov. 9, there was no help page or announcement about which accounts or campaign types receive the Asset activity page in Google Ads. Google has yet to explain how these cards are created.

Look for a Beta-labeled Asset activity entry in the Assets section of your accounts. Watch for a page on Google Ads Help that explains who receives this new page and how the cards are generated.

AI Agents Are Testing the Limits of Zero-Trust Security

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

As autonomous AI agents work together, verifying their actions, enforcing security policies, and maintaining accountability becomes increasingly difficult

Vishal Sharma is a Reader in the School of Electronics, Electrical Engineering, and Computer Science at Queen's University Belfast, Northern Ireland, United Kingdom.

In a de-authentication attack on an aerial vehicle using a human- and agent-in-the-loop approach, an AI agent can quickly scan the active channel and autonomously intercept traffic, thus reducing the time to react and regain control.

This article is brought to you by Queen’s University Belfast.

Several teams around the globe are leveraging AI by creating a task force of agents managed by an orchestrator and creating applications to support risk assessment, client profiling, match making, and information yield with a slight overview of decision making. One of the important components for these agents is to ensure that the logs are managed and historic relevance is not being used to avoid skewing towards same decision making. Thus, the system is expected to keep checkpoints in place where agents are prompted to delete such historic relevance.

However, despite such methodology in place, a future prompt could make the agents bring forward the information that was supposed to be deleted and can be tricked into revealing more information, which is a classic case of context recovery attack. So, the question raised here is: How can we trust that our instructions have been followed? And where is the proof?

There is an element of utilizing zero-trust architectures (ZTA) for orchestrating and managing agentic activity, which simply means all agentic interactions use the core principle of “never trust,” “always verify.” ZTA components include a policy enforcement point (PEP) that has to be trusted to maintain trust across the system, so the question that remains is where is the component of zero-trust, and shall we trust the policies enforced by PEP?

Having a pro-agentic approach and utilizing agents could be helpful with the right guardrails in place. Opening our systems to random agents that are being trained on off-the-shelf models and blindly relying on them can pose significant cyber risks. For example, vibe coding by using an AI model to help develop software, giving away the entire codebase to an untrusted entity outside the organization can lead to vulnerability sweeping across multiple surfaces.

Vibe coding by using an AI model to help develop software, giving away the entire codebase to an untrusted entity outside the organization can lead to vulnerability sweeping across multiple surfaces.

This opens critical discussions towards how vibe coding could interfere with the positioning and implementation of trusted execution of the agentic queries. Consider an autonomous web of agents that relies on agents’ inputs to drive the operations. When the system is operating under low tolerance of failure and with non-tracking based agentic workflows, it could be computationally expensive to trust and verify each query in real-time that leads to the common trade-offs of memory vs execution.

Another risk to consider is the zero-day attack. When it happens, how will the system react? Would such an event be manageable without a total infrastructure breakdown? The scale of impact can be observed from over reliance on agentic ecosystems with only one trusted layer. This could mean that when failure occurs, the entire ecosystem could be exposed, just like there will be no autonomy if the backbone network is not available, which essentially means no layer to place trust.

An in-house experiment done at Queen’s University Belfast (QUB) provided an initial understanding of how the scale of agentic attacks could mean a lower window of vulnerability. The team focused on very popular de-authentication attacks and with agentic orchestration, the swift rate to do de-authentication attacks could go as low as 2 to 3 seconds. This gives little to no time to react and decide on patching the system.

There is another aspect of the large-scale agentic system, which establishes trust under asynchronous autonomy. Trust serves as a critical foundation for traceability. How would you ensure that all the agents operating towards a common goal are aligned and understand the delays associated with their goals?

Our team worked around this space, which at first involved demonstrating that the problem exists, and its impact and scale can be huge when you must prove trust under boundaries of asynchronous autonomy. Solutions towards such problems can be simplified by keeping things like AI-bill of material or leveraging distributed ledger technology that can provide underlying infrastructure of trust via traceability and immutability and keep the agentic activity and associated trust vectors agnostic to the platform.

Within trust, another factor that is not well explored but needs a thorough investigation is the concept of horizontal anomalies. It defines how agents interact differently with various sources under different settings. As agents behave correctly and align themselves with the architectural expectations for one specific task or application, that does not necessarily mean these can be trusted for their operations across all associated components. How to identify such anomalies and what methods can be employed without complying with such agents remain open questions and topics for further exploration.

The critical aspect is that utility of agents with semi to low trust governance offer short-term high benefits, but the cost of recovery when the system fails is yet to be explored, and this will not be a contained issue. We are looking into the system where agents are already sweeping into different entities and may not allow complete recovery. For example, a mere supply chain issue because an agent going rogue is now not a containable small task but needs sweeping across all the touch points of those affected agents. This essentially means despite the number of agents and their ability to trigger proxy-agents, which may not be directly linked to the wider ecosystem, there is a need to ensure that computationally expensive trackers are in place for all the agents.

Other relevant PhD works carried out in our research group are towards sovereignty of AI, exploring aspects of trust and accountability. Our hypothesis is that sovereignty and trust need to be governed together as onus of provenance is high on the host of agents. Thus, it needs understanding of laws governing trust-based executions of AI. The important challenge here is that there is a vital difference between missed information and missed instructions, and both have different impacts when it comes to trusted accountability.

The real-world issue with these systems lies in the failure to adopt approaches that historically have relied on state-based modeling when governing trust. In stateless systems where agentic autonomy is high, the convergence and reliability towards trust-provenance decreases significantly. Thus, it brings us back to the original question – does a true zero-trust even exist?

Can policies be trusted? If so, how should these be managed without falling into the trap of policy-based accessibility and control issues? Despite the argument that PEP can help manage access between the agents, placing a single entity and expecting it to scale towards all agentic interactions is underestimating the problem space.

Formal verification of trust is another part of a trust-based agentic ecosystem. When dealing with AI models, understanding the robustness helps to put trust in place for these models.

To build our case and settle around the challenges of trust embeddings in Agentic AI, within Cyber AI hub, we are exploring opportunities to help companies scale up as well as build their pipeline of agentic applications for commercial viability. However, the element that governs our regular work and decision making are scale of problem and number of agentic deployments, anomaly detection across the agentic workflows, continuous evaluation of the attack surfaces and vulnerability assessment, exploring differences in the pure agentic AI and task-driven multi-agent system.

We are working with industries where trust is a pre-requisite, and these organizations put trust as a guardrail managed via internal policies with a pure focus on securing the systems through policies rather than trusting the third-party agentic service providers. Additionally, trust in services is presented by ensuring constraints around their service models where agentic activities are tracked and managed via logs including management of their token revocation list to avoid replay attacks depending on the type of application. Formal verification of trust is another part of a trust-based agentic ecosystem. When dealing with AI models, understanding the robustness helps to put trust in place for these models. Things like balancing the adversarial accuracy along with certified adversarial robustness can help to determine how much the model can be trusted.

But the question is, can we settle for anything less than foolproof when it comes to deployment of agents in the real world? And if so, how much impact can be controlled, and who is responsible for the remaining component?

Sony is launching a new version of its PlayStation Portal exclusively in Japan next month. The updated model will launch in the midnight black color option we’ve seen before, and will include “additional” internal storage for future-looking features.

It’s the additional internal storage that’s the surprise change to this particular model, which will be locked to Japanese language options and can only be used with a PlayStation account registered in Japan. Sony says the extra storage is for “future functionality” that it will announce once these features are finalized.

Sony is also renaming this particular Portal from PlayStation Portal Remote Player to PlayStation Portal Player. The name change, combined with extra internal storage, seems to suggest that this particular model might get access to streaming apps.

The existing PlayStation Portal doesn’t support streaming apps like YouTube, Disney Plus, or Netflix, and is primarily designed to stream PS5 games from a console or the cloud. The PlayStation Portal isn’t powerful enough to play PlayStation games locally, but extra storage could also let you sync media for offline playback.

This Japan-only PlayStation Portal is launching on December 10th, priced at 39,980 yen (around $253).

Tips for Hunting Mature Bucks Over Bait (Plus the Ethics of If You Should)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 9, 2026 4:16 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

During the 2023 deer season, I shot my biggest buck ever: a Kentucky main-frame 12-pointer with a couple extra points that scored 168 6/8 inches. And I shot that big sum-buck over a yellow pile of ear and persimmon-flavored shelled corn. I even dropped a protein block in the middle and seasoned it with scent for good measure.

And as I write this, I can hear blood boiling and I can see the angry comments coming. Hunting whitetails over bait is still a controversial topic across much of deer country. It is, of course, illegal in many states in the Midwest, Northeast, and West. And even in places where baiting is legal, some argue that hunters should avoid using it.

The National Deer Association published an article explaining how baiting deer can spread disease: “By design, baiting and supplemental feeding concentrate deer and other wildlife species for the purposes of hunting, trapping, viewing, or simply increasing nutrition available to the animals. Some argue baiting and feeding have positive attributes, but no one can disagree they have numerous negative implications to a deer herd, including altering animal behavior, degrading the habitat near bait and feed sites, increasing the susceptibility to predation, and greatly enhancing the opportunity for disease transmission.”

The NDA’s formal position on baiting deer is this: “The NDA opposes the expansion of baiting where not currently legal. The NDA will not work to repeal baiting where currently legal, except where CWD (or other known diseases) is present. The NDA supports the use of baiting by wildlife professionals conducting scientific research. The NDA supports continued research on the effects on baiting in deer management programs.”

Baiting is legal and very popular in many Southern states. All hell broke loose when Kansas regulators began discussing the potential of banning deer baiting. Hunting deer in Texas is synonymous with hunting over a feeder. But still, many folks don’t understand how big bucks relate to bait piles.

Two comments I always hear when the bait debate pops up go something like: “Oh, you bait, bro? Must be easy killing those big bucks.” Or, “Bait won’t kill big, mature bucks, Jack.”

I scoff at both of those statements. Neither are true, and it’s far easier to screw up on a mature buck with a poor baiting strategy than it is to fill a tag with one. Like other tactics, baiting is just another tool in the arsenal. It isn’t a cheat code.

I know because I’ve been baiting Kentucky whitetails most of my hunting career, and it took a long time before I started experiencing success on mature deer. Here is how to bait deer and, more specifically, how to bait a monster buck—plus some considerations if you’re on the fence about legal baiting.

The early season is my favorite time to bait a monster buck. Hunting pressure is low. There’s plenty of cover for concealment. Deer are on predictable bed-to-feed lines of movement. There’s no better time to catch a buck off-guard over a bait pile.

Target warm-season bedding areas, such as north-facing slopes or low-lying areas near water that offer a high stem count. Then, determine what destination food source deer are targeting, such as green soybeans, fresh-cut corn, or dropping white oak acorns. Place the bait between the bedroom and dinner table.

Don’t overlook the pre-rut. This is a great time to bait a monster buck. Bucks are moving more but aren’t running wild just yet. So, capitalize on that buck’s rising testosterone levels. Often that leads to increased daylight travel.

As with the early season, it’s still all about bed-to-feed patterns. But now, deer are likely settling into their fall bedding areas and food sources. So, find these locations (which vary greatly by region and habitat type) and plant bait stations along lines of travel.

Once the rut hits, you might get a buck to hit a bait pile. But most of the time, a rutting buck is more interested in scent-checking does that have been through the buffet line. Because of this, bait stations are still effective during the rut, but you must hunt them differently.

Usually, I prefer to place bait piles on the downwind sides of doe bedding areas. Or locate the bait between two doe bedding areas. Bucks like to travel these locations anyway, so it’s a no-brainer game plan.

Once the rut is over, and the late season arrives, it’s all about the food again. But still, focus on late-season bedding areas. Think solar bedding (south-facing slopes) and thermal cover (dense stands of coniferous trees). Also, consider what deer are eating now, including remaining waste grain, green plots, remaining acorns, but most importantly, woody browse.

Once again, the best bet is placing bait piles between bedding areas and destination food sources. Use these as staging areas to intercept bucks during daylight. At this point, they aren’t likely to reach destination food sources before dark. But if your bait site is located close enough to bedding, they might hit that corn pile during legal shooting hours.

Read Next: 35 Deer Hunting Tips to Read Before This Season

No matter the time of year, there are certain tactics that apply to virtually every baiting scenario. Of course, you have to locate a big buck before bait will help you. No volume of bait will bring in a monster buck if he doesn’t already live there. But once you’ve got a big buck located, follow these tips.

Some people say deer hunting over bait isn’t ethical. But here’s the thing: Some hunting ethics are situational. I’m not talking about taking poor shots or breaking hunting laws. Those ethics are clearly black and white.

For me, baiting deer is more of a gray area. First off, if it’s legal, it’s legal. When analyzing the differences of state-to-state guidelines on baiting, you’ll see regional discrepancies just like you will with crossbow regulations, the use of deer urine lures, and season structures. So if your area allows baiting, then it’s likely that agency wildlife managers for that area have decided baiting is not detrimental to the deer and deer hunting.

Second, it’s important to realize that it’s not like you can just throw out bait at any random spot in the woods and start shooting trophy bucks. It’s far from a canned hunt. If you live in an area that permits baiting, understand that bait has several different applications. Where legal, it can be used to help you attract deer, hold deer, feed supplemental nutrition, take inventory of deer, and more.

As for effectiveness, people commonly say bait stations are either fool-proof or that they turn bucks nocturnal. In my experience, neither are true.

Whitetails are smart, especially around bait piles. They learn to approach bait piles downwind and they pick up on hunters’ scent in the area. Yes, they will often hit bait at night. But no buck is 100 percent nocturnal—that’s a myth.

If you suddenly start seeing a target buck avoiding bait during daylight, it’s likely because you screwed up and disrupted that deer. Or, he started bedding elsewhere. Or, he started targeting a different destination food source. Or, maybe he’s just being a wild, unpredictable deer.

The point is, hunting over bait is still deer hunting. Anything can happen, and most of the time the big buck wins. But if you understand how to bait deer and you execute a well-crafted game plan, then baiting just might lead to the biggest whitetail of your life.

United Unveils 14-Gate Concourse at Dulles

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

United and the Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority unveiled Washington Dulles International Airport’s first new concourse in nearly two decades on Monday. Concourse E opens to passengers Nov. 10, giving United 14 new gates at a hub where its departures have risen 30% since 2022.

The 435,000-square-foot concourse connects directly to the airport’s AeroTrain and has jet bridges at every gate. It can handle both domestic and international flights. The airports authority said existing gates were already fully in use several times a day, with passengers crowding waiting areas and concessions during peak periods.

The concourse also houses United’s second-largest United Club. The nearly 40,000-square-foot lounge seats more than 600 people and increases the airline’s total lounge space at Dulles by about 70%. New concessions include local businesses such as Good Company Doughnuts & Café and Honor Brewing.

“Washington Dulles is a critical part of United’s global network and growth strategy,” United CEO Scott Kirby said in a statement. The carrier currently offers more than 250 daily flights from Dulles to 135 destinations.

United has added six international destinations from the airport this year. It plans to begin international Airbus A321XLR service from Dulles in December with flights to Amsterdam and Dublin, followed by new service to Toulouse, France, and Milan in 2027.

Concourse E is the first phase of the broader Dulles Global Gateway program, which United and the airports authority estimate will involve more than $20 billion in investment. Later work is expected to add gates, expand the main terminal, and build a new U.S. Customs facility. The program also calls for extending the AeroTrain so the airport can eventually retire its mobile lounges.

Getty to help conserve art-filled 17th-century church in Peruvian Andes

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Members of the local community in the Church of Rondocan Photo: Alejandra Velez, © 2026 J. Paul Getty Trust

An art-filled 17th-century church in the Andes mountains will soon begin five years of conservation work. The project will strengthen the building and preserve its collection of 330 historical objects—including eight paintings by the artist Bernardo Bitti (1548-1610), an Italian Jesuit priest who introduced Mannerism to the Viceroyalty of Peru in the 16th century.

The Getty Conservation Institute (GCI) will work with the Peruvian Ministry of Culture to conserve the Church of Santo Tomás de Aquino, also known as the Church of Rondocan—named for the town in the Cusco region where it was built. The project will include seismic stabilisation using local materials; located 3,400m above sea level, the 340-sq.-m church is in an area prone to earthquakes.

“We provide all the advanced conservation techniques available, and we handle the structural design of the project,” Claudia Cancino, a principal project specialist at the GCI, tells The Art Newspaper. The Peruvian government will finance the work, which will cost $5m and start in 2027. Like other churches in the Andean highlands, Rondocan has adobe walls (1.2m thick), buttresses and a wooden truss roof.

“The project is not only about the temple and its walls. It’s about the cultural and spiritual heritage of the church,” says César Ccorimanya Mayta, the parish priest at Rondocan for the past 17 years.

The Church of Rondocan and its surrounding landscape Photo: Luigi Barazzetti © 2026 J. Paul Getty Trust

Involving the community as caretakers of the temple is part of the conservation process. Cancino and her team completed a similar project at the Church of Santiago Apóstol in the nearby town of Kuñotambo in 2019. As part of that initiative, they showed locals how best to take care of the building—including deceptively simple tasks, like cleaning the floors.

“You have to understand how to sweep the church and with what, because if you stir up too much dust it sticks to the murals,” Cancino says. “All this training was done with the community of Kuñotambo. We even have a manual in Quechua,” the local Indigenous language.

Through the work in Kuñotambo, the GCI became familiar with the Church of Rondocan. “The community asked for our support so that they could request the Archdiocese of Cusco and the Ministry of Culture to work on their church,” Cancino says.

Technical examination and assessment of a painting from the series Principales Misterios de la Vida y Muerte de Cristo Nuestro Salvador y la Virgen Santísima, attributed to Bernardo Bitti © 2024 J. Paul Getty Trust

“This church is much more than a historic building. It is part of the community’s memory, identity and everyday life,” Fernando López Sánchez, Peru’s vice minister of cultural heritage, said in a statement.

As part of the conservation project, a Bitti painting will travel to Los Angeles for scientific analysis and conservation on the Getty campus.

Bitti arrived in Peru in 1575, creating paintings for churches in Lima and Cusco. When the Jesuits were expelled from the Americas by the Spanish Crown in 1767, their works were sold. It is believed that the parish priest at Rondocan at that time bought Bitti’s paintings in Cusco and had them transferred to the church. The discovery, less than two decades ago, of the only complete series of eight Bitti canvases has sparked renewed interest in the study of his work.

In addition to the paintings, the church’s collection includes sculptures from the viceroyalty era, manuscripts and silver liturgical objects. “The art collection in this small town in the middle of nowhere is breathtaking,” Cancino says.

As the Getty winds down its decade-long Mosaikon project, it leaves behind a legacy of expertise in a region threatened by looting and political unrest

The preservation project could serve as a prototype for saving other earthen buildings

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/trump-administration-announces-10b-awards-rural-health-transformation-programs-second" hreflang="en">Trump administration announces $10B in awards for Rural Health Transformation Program’s second year</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →
Trump administration announces $10B in awards for Rural Health Transformation Program’s second year" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration announced Monday funding for the second year of the Rural Health Transformation Program (RHTP), with $10 billion in awards slated for all 50 states.

The U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) announced awards through the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services.

“President Trump made a historic investment in rural America, and HHS is putting those resources to work in every state,” said Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., HHS secretary, in a statement. “This next $10 billion will expand access to care, strengthen the rural health workforce, improve prevention, and help states build solutions that fit the needs of their own communities. We are giving rural America the resources and flexibility to build stronger health systems that deliver better care and better outcomes.”

Under the statute, half of the $10 billion-per-year distribution is split between the states evenly. The remaining half is determined by the CMS, with a portion of second-year funding being determined by states’ progress in implementing approved RHTP plans.

Federal officials say the annual review “strengthens accountability” for the investment.

Every state submitted applications to the federal government last November outlining plans to improve rural healthcare through their share of $50 billion in funds from the program, with CMS announcing first year awards in December.

Similar to fiscal year 2026, Texas and Alaska will receive the largest amount of funding as part of the program. Texas is set to receive $281,976,026, while Alaska will receive $272,653,149.

Other top recipients were Montana ($239,740,669), California ($231,105,823), Oklahoma ($227,097,543), Kansas ($225,896,117) and Georgia ($221,731,766).

New Jersey will receive the lowest amount of funding at $142,338,224, followed by Connecticut ($153,652,078), Rhode Island ($158,171,967), Massachusetts ($158,392,692) and Delaware ($160,294,521).

“Rural Americans have life expectancies three years shorter than their urban peers, but President Trump’s Rural Health Transformation Program is funding the historic investments necessary to close that gap,” said Mehmet Oz, M.D., CMS administrator, in a statement. “States have already put these resources to work bringing better care closer to home for their rural communities, and CMS looks forward to helping them build on that progress in the coming year.”

8 Things I Learned About Gardening From an Amish Farmer – and Why They're Worth Trying in Your Own Yard

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

My Husband Is a Professional Gardener – and He Always Does These 3 Things to Roses in November

This Clever Chopstick Trick Shows When Houseplants Need Water – No More Guessing or Soggy Soil

7 Household Trash Items You Should Save in Fall to Protect Plants From Frost

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Some of this Amish farmer's gardening wisdom might sound old-fashioned, but his practical advice has plenty to teach modern gardeners.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Some of the best gardening tips never make it onto a seed packet. They come from people who've worked the same ground for years and notice what the rest of us walk past – like how a tomato leaf changes color before trouble starts.

I recently stumbled across a fascinating piece of gardening history that feels just as relevant today as it did when it was written. Back in 2013, Amish farmer Joni Miller sent Modern Farmer a 45-page handwritten essay on organic gardening and farming, packed with diagrams and advice on everything from compost to certification paperwork. All he asked in return was a two-year subscription.

Much of what Miller wrote will sound familiar to organic growers. Some of it surprised me, though, and it's fascinating to see how well his ideas line up with newer research. These are the eight lessons I found most interesting, and how to use each one at home.

Miller suggests making a little chart of leaf colors, and I love the idea of reading a vegetable bed that way. For tomatoes and eggplant, he says lower leaves that yellow once the plants start bearing give up their potassium to the fruit. A black, rotting spot on a tomato's bottom – aka blossom-end rot – in his experience, means poor calcium uptake.

Newer research largely agrees, though each symptom has other causes, too. Lower leaves also yellow from a nitrogen shortage, plain old age, or early blight (Alternaria linariae). Blossom-end rot does come from calcium not reaching the fruit, but the soil usually has plenty, and uneven watering tends to be the real trouble. I'd sort that out before buying fertilizer.

The weed section grabbed me most. Miller noticed foxtail and other grassy weeds sprouting better in tight, high-magnesium soils, and lambsquarters surging after heavy turkey manure. Velvetleaf liked rich ground, Canada thistle faded once soil was loose and cultivated, and pastures full of dandelions never grew lush. As he puts it, "Reading soils by their weeds is an art!"

Researchers read weeds the same way today, as clues with wiggle room. Weeds tolerate a range of conditions, so dandelions might mean compacted soil and chickweed might mean wet ground, or a neighbor's seed heads might just be blowing your way. Then confirm with a soil test, using a home kit that you send to a lab, like the MySoil Soil Test Kit, available on Amazon.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Miller's pest advice starts with life cycles, using squash bugs (Anasa tristis) as his example. The nymphs that hatch look like little spiders and start out soft. Each molt hardens them, and by the later ones, he found few organic sprays could touch them. If oil can't smother the eggs, his trick is to "target for those young spiderlings while they're easier prey."

That means turning squash leaves over. Look at the undersides, often in the crook between two veins, and you'll spot copper-brown egg clusters. Duct tape pulls them off easily. If a few nymphs get past you, insecticidal soap or neem oil, like Bonide Captain Jack's Neem Oil Spray, will knock them back, sprayed early or late, when bees are quieter. Adults, by then, are another story, and hand-picking works better on them.

Straw mulch is nothing new, so Miller's note about greenhouse tomato growers using alfalfa hay caught my attention. It keeps weeds down, but the bigger benefit, he says, is that "the decomposing alfalfa feeds the vegetables with high-quality nutrients."

Modern studies hold that up. Alfalfa is a legume, so it has more nitrogen than straw and breaks down faster. It holds moisture, too. Once the soil warms, spread 2–3 inches (5–8 cm) of weed-free hay around tomatoes and peppers, leaving a gap at the stems.

Ask if the hay field was sprayed, as some hay herbicides linger, and tomatoes are very sensitive. Can't find bales? Try using alfalfa meal instead, like Down to Earth organic alfalfa meal from Walmart.

I was surprised to learn you can time weeding with a thermometer. Miller counts heat units. You average each day's high and low, but a high over 90°F (32°C) only counts as 90, a low under 50°F (10°C) counts as 50, and you subtract 50. Weed seeds need about 60 units to germinate, he says, so he harrows right before that and starts back at zero with each new flush.

Weed researchers still study the same idea today, under the label stale seedbed, which is a funny name for it, honestly. Simply prepare the bed a few weeks early, water it, and wait for the first weeds, usually a week or two in warm soil. Slice them off in the top inch (2.5 cm) – digging deeper brings buried seeds up to sprout. WaLensee's stirrup-style action hoe from Home Depot suits that shallow work. Then sow without stirring the soil again.

This one sounded simple to me, and it is, just about. Miller waters his greenhouse plants in the forenoon, not the evening, and opens the vents on sunny days so plants don't stay damp. Fungal diseases, he warns, "explode in stale, damp environments."

Current advice backs him up. Picture tomatoes watered at dusk, still wet at midnight. That's where early blight and gray mold (Botrytis cinerea) get going. Water the soil in the morning instead, space and stake plants for airflow, and stay out of wet rows. Greenhouses need open vents or a fan, and patio pots need elbow room.

For run-down soil, Miller says green manure crops really help, along with soil tests. Bare soil takes a beating from winter rain. A cover crop holds it with roots and shades out weeds, too. Turned in or left on top, it adds organic matter. Legumes like crimson clover and field peas also pull nitrogen from the air, with help from root bacteria.

This late in fall, I'd go with cereal rye. Oats and field peas want a late-summer start, and a cold winter usually finishes them off. Crimson clover, available from Outsidepride on Amazon, needs a few weeks of growth before hard frost – unless your winters are mild, that one's an early-fall job. In spring, cut the crop around flowering, before seed sets, then wait two to three weeks to plant.

Farmers in Miller's community save back their own soybean seed, which he says "helps that variety to somewhat adapt to your farm's soil type." You don't need a farm for it. Save seed from several of your healthiest, most productive open-pollinated plants, not one favorite, and do it again the year after, too. Little by little, your seed comes from plants that thrive in your garden.

Beans, peas, lettuce, and tomatoes are the easy ones, because their flowers mostly pollinate themselves. Squash, cucumbers, and corn need isolation or hand-pollination, since bees and wind can cross them with nearby varieties. I'd skip F1 hybrids, though, since their seed can come up quite different. Dry your seeds well, then store them somewhere cool in labeled kraft paper seed envelopes.

Much of Miller's letter covers certification forms, buffer strips, and ice houses. But tucked in between are gardening ideas that today's research still backs up. Next season, I'm taking a slower look at my weeds and tomato leaves. You might notice things you've been walking past for years.

Tyler’s passion began with indoor gardening and deepened as he studied plant-fungi interactions in controlled settings. With a microbiology background focused on fungi, he’s spent over a decade solving tough and intricate gardening problems. After spinal injuries and brain surgery, Tyler’s approach to gardening changed. It became less about the hobby and more about recovery and adapting to physical limits. His growing success shows that disability doesn’t have to stop you from your goals.

A major Venmo update could change how you pay for dinner with friends

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Splitting the bill after a group dinner can sometimes feel like an Olympic sport. One friend ordered the expensive steak, their girlfriend stuck to a salad, and your friend Greg has been drinking expensive bourbon all night but thinks you should all just split the bill evenly.

Venmo is finally taking some of the mental gymnastics out of the equation.

The payment app is rolling out a new receipt-scanning feature this week that lets users take a photo of a receipt, automatically itemize everything on it, and assign individual charges to the people who ordered them.

How Venmo’s new receipt-scanning feature works

The new tool is built directly into the Venmo app, so users won’t need to download anything extra or switch between apps to split a bill.

To use it, open Venmo, tap the V on the home screen to start a payment, and select “Scan receipt.” From there, take a photo of the receipt or upload an existing image.

Venmo automatically identifies and itemizes the purchases. Users can then assign individual items to the appropriate people and make adjustments as needed. Once everything is assigned, Venmo calculates each person’s portion of the tax and tip and generates payment requests. The feature works regardless of which mobile device someone in the group uses.

“Splitting the bill is one of the most common things people do on Venmo, but it can also be one of the most tedious,” said Alexis Sowa, Venmo’s general manager. “With receipt scanning, a pile of math goes away with a single photo. Venmo does the heavy lifting so customers can get back to what matters: the meal, the trip, or the night out with friends.”

Gen Z is often stuck figuring out who owes what

The launch comes as new research from Venmo suggests younger adults often cover group expenses upfront and collect payments afterward.

In a survey conducted by Morning Consult on behalf of Venmo, 40% of adults aged 18 to 22 said they’re typically the person who pays first and then collects everyone else’s share.

The research also found that people have different approaches to dividing shared expenses.

About 20% of respondents said they split bills based on exactly what each person ordered, while 15% divide the total evenly. Another 12% said they take turns paying or have different people cover different portions of the bill. Meanwhile, 11% said one person typically pays the entire bill and requests everyone’s share afterward.

The findings suggest there’s no universally accepted way to split a group bill, which may be part of what makes the process so frustrating.

It’s not just for restaurant bills

Although splitting restaurant checks is an obvious use for the feature, Venmo says receipt scanning can also help people manage other shared expenses.

Roommates, for instance, could use it to divide a grocery receipt based on who purchased which items, rather than splitting the total evenly. Friends traveling together could scan receipts from meals or shared purchases to settle expenses after a trip.

The tool also works alongside Venmo Groups, an existing feature that allows users to track shared expenses over time.

Groups is designed for situations where people regularly split costs, whether roommates manage household purchases, couples divide monthly bills, or friends track expenses during a vacation. Instead of handling every expense as a separate transaction, users can see shared spending in one place and settle up when they’re ready.

When will Venmo’s receipt-scanning feature be available?

Venmo says the feature is rolling out now, with wider availability expected over the next several weeks. That means some users may see the option before others.

The launch is part of a broader Venmo app update designed to make its payment and financial features easier to find and use.

Other features highlighted in the updated app include scheduling recurring payments, such as monthly rent, and earning rewards through Venmo Stash.

For anyone who’s ever spent 15 minutes after dinner trying to figure out who owes $23.47, the new receipt scanner could be a welcome addition. The only thing it can’t do is persuade your friend to actually pay you back.

A celebrated Berlin designer turns her eye to kids’ furniture

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

If you walk through the kids’ furniture section of a Target or Walmart, you’ll get a glimpse into what adults think children like: pink for girls, blue for boys, a parade of cartoon bunnies and Disney princesses emblazoned on bed frames and step stools.

But do kids actually want all this? Or have we simply stereotyped them? Hanne Willmann suspects we underestimate children’s ability to appreciate clean, minimalist design. The Berlin-based industrial designer and founder of Studio Hanne Willmann has spent a decade making products for grown-ups: chairs and sofas for Freifrau, tables for Favius, lighting for Hartô Designs. Today, in an interesting twist, Willmann takes on the role of chief design officer for Piccalio, a six-year-old Los Angeles furniture startup.

Willmann’s new collection for Piccalio—a table and chair set made of carved wood—reflects her approach to designing for children. The pieces, which are inspired by a 1970s Swiss chair, blend the simplicity of wood with interesting curves. Willmann believes they will appeal to both parents and children, who will cultivate a taste for good design.

A European Approach

There’s a long history of European designers bringing their skills to children’s furniture. Alma Siedhoff-Buscher designed a suite of children’s furniture for a model house in a 1923 Bauhaus exhibition in Weimar, Germany, where every piece was multipurpose: a dresser cabinet became a puppet theater, a changing table became a writing desk.

In the 1930s, the Finnish designer Alvar Aalto designed a children’s version of his famous three-legged Artek stool, and it has been part of Scandinavian schools and nurseries for generations. And in Norway, Peter Opsvik’s Tripp Trapp chair for Stokke has become a global best-seller.

The founders of Piccalio—Ali Tavsanoglu and Aneta Kostic, a married couple who met in the U.K. and now live in L.A.—are inspired by this history of design. They launched the startup in 2020, after a frustrating afternoon shopping for furniture for their niece and nephews. A lot of products they found on the U.S. market were low quality, covered in graphics, and inconsistent with the aesthetics of a modern home.

For their first product, they wanted to create a safe step stool for children to help their parents in the kitchen. They developed a sturdy wooden “toddler tower” with a railing that lifts small children, so they can reach kitchen counters and sinks. They began modestly, selling the $249 product on Etsy, but it quickly became a breakout. So they decided to launch their own website, where they also sold a $499 bed that won awards from Domino and Good Housekeeping. The brand is sold as well at Babylist, Maisonette, and Janie and Jack. Since 2020, Piccalio has more than doubled its sales each year, and now generates upwards of $10 million in revenue.

Bringing on an Industrial Designer

The success of the toddler tower inspired Piccalio’s founders to have broader ambitions, and reimagine other children’s furniture.

In 2022, before bringing her on as their chief design officer, they partnered with Willmann to design other furniture. Willmann’s focus is on bringing out the natural beauty in the lines and materials of the material she’s using. She wants her objects to inspire feelings, like comfort, safety, or creativity. “My work sits somewhere between sensual and functional,” she says.

When it comes to children’s furniture, Willmann takes cues from Montessori education, where child-sized furniture inspires kids to move and work independently. She wants to create pieces that accomplish a task, but that are simple enough that children can find other uses for them. “If you give a child paper, they will draw something, or make a paper plane, or create a little basket to hold paper balls,” she says. “They are crazily creative if you don’t limit them.”

But Willmann finds many Montessori-based products to be clinical or overtly pedagogical. She believes it is possible to create furniture that supports kids’ learning and creativity that is also attractive and fun. This is evident in her first project for Piccalio, which was designing a toddler table and chair set that comes in a range of calm colors, like ochre yellow and sage green. While Piccalio doesn’t share revenue figures, Tavsanoglu says that sales of this table set exceeded expectations.

The furniture is easy for a child to manipulate, and the chair can be flipped upside down or on its side to create different ways of sitting. There is a little semicircle on the backrest that evokes a rainbow, but also serves as a handle to pick the chair up. With some imagination, toddlers can transform the table into a fort or turn it upside down to pretend it is a boat; the chair can become a little bed for a doll.

“They may not even sit on it,” says Willmann. “That’s the beautiful thing about kids. I would never want to limit what kids do with products like these.”

Curved Wood Table

In her role as chief design officer of Piccalio, Willmann has created a new table and chair set, called Arcc, designed for older children of preschool age. The idea is that children will graduate from the toddler set to this set as they spend more time doing crafts, drawing, and learning to write.

Its reference point is the wooden Rey chair, designed in 1971 by the Swiss designer Bruno Rey, which has a curved backrest, a circle seat, and rounded edges. (The Danish brand Hay now sells the chair, starting at $645.) Willmann’s version is similarly minimal, and aims to bring curved lines into the wooden furniture. There is an interesting visual contrast between the hardness of the wood and the gentle movement you sense from the arched back and legs.

The set is made from bentwood, thin layers of wood with alternating grain that are glued and pressed together with heat. This approach requires developing a complex and expensive mold. Piccalio partners with a Romanian factory that has specialized in this technique for 70 years. The proportions are scaled to a child’s body, rather than shrunk from an adult chair. And the rounded shapes are designed to look engaging, friendly and safe, without tipping into cartoonish. It comes in several wood finishes, as well as red and light blue.

At upwards of $650 for a table and two chairs, the Arcc set is pricey. But Tavsanoglu believes there is a growing market in the U.S. for aesthetically pleasing, durable children’s furniture that pushes back against the wave of “fast furniture” that is widely available on Amazon and Wayfair.

Piccalio is part of a small but growing group of design-oriented children’s brands, like New York-based Lalo, which makes toys and furniture, and Belgium’s EcoBirdy, which makes speckled chairs and tables from recycled plastic toys. Bauen sells an award-winning table-and-chair set engineered to resist tipping.

These brands charge more than mass market, and have proven that some parents are willing to pay for pieces that fit within their home’s aesthetic. Tavsanoglu says there’s now a booming secondhand market for high quality children’s furniture, and Piccalio expects its pieces to last years of play. “Often, our pieces show up on Facebook Marketplace for 80% of what they cost new,” he says.

Many of Piccalio’s customers live in big cities where they don’t have space to shove their children’s furniture into a playroom. Families want them to match the broader aesthetic of their living room or kitchen. For Willmann, small spaces are not a disadvantage; she believes they encourage families to spend more time together.

“It can be sad if kids are off in a playroom,” she says. “My mother always told me to put the drawing table close to where the adults spend their time. Ours is next to our kitchen island, so our kids can draw while we’re cooking.”

A grand new start of it: Exploring Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s effect on business—in New York City and beyond

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A democratic socialist running the financial capital of the universe? For a magazine obsessed with how business can change—and change the world—it’s hard to imagine a more intriguing experiment.

As Zohran Mamdani wraps up his first year as New York City’s mayor, we decided to explore a possibility that got lost in the reductive shouting that surrounded his campaign: What if his democratic socialism-fueled agenda is actually good for business? And what will happen as other cities and states elect leaders with similar ideas?

Making New York more affordable is a goal that most of us can get behind. So is making city government faster, more responsive, and more innovative. The question—a thorny one—is whether Mamdani can deliver a more equitable city without throwing a wet blanket over the investment and entrepreneurship that help it thrive.

That tension animates this issue’s cover story, by Morgan Clendaniel, our executive editor, digital. Clendaniel, who has long covered the intersection of business and progressive politics, spoke to Tony Shorris, president and CEO of the NYC Economic Development Corporation, and Julie Su, deputy mayor for economic justice, about the sweeping role that they believe government can play in the economy—speeding up procurement, cutting red tape, making city services function.

The revolution may include city-owned grocery stores. It will definitely include high-tech public bathrooms.

Capitalism + democratic socialism

There’s something invigorating about that combination of grand ambition and attention to the daily details. At Fast Company, we spend a lot of time talking to visionaries who see a broken system and decide to build something better. It’s exciting to see that startup energy coursing through City Hall.

Clendaniel interviewed chief executives, too, such as Warby Parker’s Neil Blumenthal, and he spoke with key opposition figures, including Steven Fulop, president and CEO of the pro-business Partnership for New York City, and the venture capitalist Bradley Tusk. (Bill Ackman, the hedge fund manager, had his own words for the mayor at our Innovation Festival in September.)

This unfettered-capitalism crowd worries that the S-word alone is enough to discourage investment in New York and prompt companies to relocate or move jobs elsewhere. There’s some fearmongering here, no doubt, but also some merit. Business hates uncertainty.

These are complex questions worth exploring with an open mind and a willingness to scrutinize the trade-offs. Clendaniel does just that, with a big assist from his editor, Jill Bernstein.

A visual caffeine jolt

Meanwhile, our creative director, Mike Schnaidt, came up with the idea for the coffee cup on the cover, brought to life by illustrator Justin Metz. Along with Clendaniel and Bernstein’s journalism, this graphic effort reminds me why I love making this magazine. Even in the fully digitized, AI-ified year of 2026, a great magazine cover can turn a complicated argument into something immediate and surprising.

Here, an everyday New York object carries an extraordinary question about the future of the city—and the ever-evolving relationship between the private sector and the public good.

AI’s new business model: Break the internet, then sell the fix

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

People who work in cybersecurity or work on the fundamentals of digital infrastructure do not want to live in “exciting times,” but right now, they don’t have a choice.

Binance was just hacked in what is, so far, the largest cryptocurrency heist in history. Anthropic, the company behind Claude, narrowly averted a major attack through a third-party extension that would have allowed hackers to upload worms to the affected computers. A number of major airlines were affected when the Spanish travel-tech company Amadeus was hacked, leaking customer information on the web (mercifully without payment info, this time). At the center of this are generative AI companies like Anthropic and OpenAI, which all at once are empowering hackers while billing themselves as the only line of defense. They’ve found a new niche, and it’s to become a protection racket. 

The threat is quantifiable: Severe bugs are classified as CVEs (common vulnerabilities and exposures), and around 400,000 have been officially logged since 1988. Of those, 13,000 happened in the last month. PHP2Shell is the perfect example of this. If you spent any time on social media in the last few weeks, you probably heard some chatter about PHP2Shell and just how dire the threat was. Roughly 70% of websites rely on code written using PHP, and the announcement of a fundamental bug in the language—akin to discovering that millions of buildings share the same faulty lock, potentially giving intruders easy access—had people panicking. Websites such as Wikimedia preemptively went offline until a fix was pushed through.

These bugs were rediscovered separately by two cybersecurity firms: Calif and Wiz. The bugs are very difficult to spot. They had to do with how the host computer accesses memory while an application is running: If a malicious actor forced an out of memory condition by whatever means, it would be possible to interrupt the runtime, leave a partially initialized object behind, and have the application treat it as valid. It would be a stepping stone for a hostile takeover of the host server, while a separate bug could leave instructions from a request that could be read again subsequently instead of being reset. This can be an apocalyptic threat if somebody can actually do those things. They would gain remote control over the host computer, and at that point they can do whatever they want with that machine and what’s on it.

Notably, developers at the PHP Foundation knew about this bug in 2024, but couldn’t agree on a solution for it. Every proposed fix had associated costs, and in any event, nobody actually knew how to act against these bugs. The threat was completely theoretical. It sat there, which wasn’t the optimal workflow, but it also wasn’t an omega-level threat until generative AI came into the picture. Forcing the condition while also creating the takeover wasn’t easy to do. Once the vulnerability was publicly announced, what had been a trickle of attacks became a flood. Fixes were rapidly pushed (in some cases by volunteers) and anybody running a website on PHP was urged to quickly upgrade to protect themselves.

This was just one bug, but pretty much everybody expects that more will be found. The people talking loudest about the danger are, surprisingly, generative artificial intelligence companies. AI companies are all too happy to make clear how incredibly dangerous all of this is and how many more bugs and vulnerabilities they expect to find as they continue to look around. Sam Altman doesn’t think we’ll be able to patch all the bugs, while Jason Clinton of Anthropic terms it a “vulnerability tsunami.” They’re promising years of instability following this as they struggle to discover and patch all of these bugs—an ongoing process, because software development is itself a process of continual updates. 

Why are they talking this way? They’re emphasizing how dangerous their product is, which seems like terrible salesmanship. But they’re also peddling the solution to the problem they helped to introduce.

And to force the issue, they’re going to look for the bugs themselves. That’s the point of OpenAI’s Patch the Planet initiative, which is all about looking for defects and vulnerabilities in open-source software. Nor are they just running around bug-hunting and letting chaos ensue. They’re trying to step into the cybersecurity space and set themselves up as the best solution to the problem.

OpenAI unveiled a new security-focused model in September, GPT-6 Cyber. OpenAI’s Daybreak program is intended to be available only to eligible organizations so that only “good actors” can take advantage of this, though in the long term as models become open access it’s not clear how that distinction will matter. Anthropic has its own model, Claude Mythos Preview. Google has Gemini Carbon 4 as its cybersecurity model. As with all models, you pay per token. Access to Daybreak is subsidized, although if you’re a believer in Cory Doctorow’s theory of enshittification, the long-term business model will be to make this more expensive over time as customers grow to depend on it. These companies are desperate for new sources of revenue even as overall revenue growth slows.

They’re not being subtle about this. Sam Altman told a group of business students at Stanford that “I think we’re going to have to completely switch the defense model to using the same technology for a new kind of defense shield.” In other words: Gotta use my products to keep yourself safe. What they’re doing verges on extortion; they’re trying to monetize the chaos their products are introducing. But in addition to that, they’re taking aim at a fundamentally vulnerable digital space.

A threat to the open-source commons

Let’s take a step back and consider how the internet, much of which runs on open-source software, is really maintained. PHP is supported by the PHP Foundation, which has just over a dozen employees and relies on donations. It is in effect a passion project for a few developers. There’s an Xkcd comic that sheds a great deal of light on this: Random critical pieces of digital infrastructure are being maintained by people entirely on their own, thanklessly and often without compensation. The modern open internet has been built up in ad hoc ways that often deprioritized security, but that mattered less when only human beings were doing the building or the hacking. To take advantage of those vulnerabilities would require not just expertise, but a great deal of time, effort, and computational power in many cases. PHP2Shell’s exploits were hard to spot, even if you really knew what you were doing and understood PHP down to the source code.

Yes, these potential threats are dire, but this isn’t a tsunami or a natural disaster that simply could not have been prevented. This is entirely manmade, and the people shrieking about what a dire emergency it is are also the ones who have helped to inaugurate it. This exploit sat virtually unknown by anybody for more than a decade, and even when it was recognized for what it was, the threat was purely theoretical. Now because of generative AI, cybersecurity is starting to favor the attack rather than the defender. People like Sam Altman helped to create the problem, first by developing the models, and now by going around and actively looking for weak points in our ramshackle internet. They broke it, and now they’re here to offer a solution: “Nice open internet you’ve got right there; it would be a shame if something happened to it.”

And what are the costs associated with this? For individual consumers of course, there’s the threat of hacks, theft, and general instability on the internet. For businesses, outages, lost revenue, and theft. That’s severe. But I think that there’s a more fundamental risk to the whole of the internet, at least as we know it. Groups like the PHP Foundation or the individual developers who sustain free and open-source software (FOSS) are going to be under even greater strain in the years to come, especially when compared to companies like Anthropic that, for the moment at least, have very deep pockets. Those 13,000 CVEs I mentioned at the beginning of the piece? Those largely fall on volunteers and overworked organizations to remediate, which will burn them out and in the long run likely push people away from the whole FOSS project. 

Turning to generative AI is probably what Anthropic and every other company wants, as another use case for their products, but it poses its own severe risks. Large language models (LLMs) are already being used on open-source projects that call into question the whole license-free structure and system of attribution that has made FOSS possible. That’s one leg of our stool that’s being kicked out from under us.

On top of that though, as AI-generated code winds up in more FOSS projects, is it going to introduce more vulnerabilities that will then have to be hunted down and eliminated? The answer seems to be yes. Whether it’s from vibe coding or simply the fact that LLMs trained on code with security vulnerabilities will tend to reproduce those vulnerabilities, we’re likely going to be running in place for years on end. And even if it can be introduced without undermining the security of the code itself, it’s another avenue to eat away at the licensure and copyright-free status that makes FOSS possible.

So this protection racket that’s forming is more than just a tax. It’s going to massively destabilize the ecosystem of the internet as we know it. I don’t know what comes next if the delicate FOSS sandbox falls apart. Maintainers could abandon their projects, which in turn creates more security vulnerabilities as things fall out of date and become obsolete. Even stably running pieces of infrastructure will eventually be unsupported by the languages that they run on; people can wake up one morning and find that some odd dependency powering their website stopped working. Who’s responsible at that point for fixing it? 

Nor do I want to see it entrusted to for-profit entities. Maintaining this work is basically thankless as it stands; it’s unprofitable to take on because the whole point is that everybody can freely use it. The only reason for them to do so in the long run would be to monetize it, which costs the rest of us. And as they make these into their own proprietary systems, will they wall off what is theirs from everything else? Why shouldn’t they; Microsoft spent years fighting the FOSS movement because it saw open-source software as a threat to its copyrighted systems. What has been up until now a true commons, maintained by people and organizations largely doing it out of passion, would become something else entirely: less able to talk to each other, and more expensive to use.

What’s the solution? This is probably a whole other essay, but in the long run it’s going to have to be government. Whether by directly assuming the burden or offering cash and resources to private groups, governments need to take steps to ensure that an open and free internet continues to exist. Could something like the United Nations do it, ensuring that it’s a truly global movement instead of under one national or regional umbrella? Failing that, could the European Union take it on—a prudent move, especially as Europe tries to get out from under the thumb of U.S. big tech. Food for thought, because under the shadow of all of these scary cyberattacks, that’s where I think the real threat lies.

→ Baca artikel asli

Warren Buffett says really successful people say no to almost everything. Most leaders miss the point of his famous quote

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Most leaders are pretty good at adding things. There’s always room for another initiative, another “important” meeting, or some new process or tech that’s supposed to make everyone’s life easier.

Before long, the organization is carrying far more than anyone intended, and very little ever gets taken off the table. Sound familiar?

Leaders aren’t nearly as good at subtraction. That’s why one of Warren Buffett’s most famous observations still deserves attention, although perhaps for a reason different from what we’ve been giving it.

The difference between successful people and really successful people is that really successful people say no to almost everything.

I’ve written about this Buffett quote before, usually in the context of protecting your time and focusing on what matters. But there’s another leadership lesson buried inside it that becomes more important the higher you climb.

Every “yes” has a cost

When a CEO says yes to another priority, that decision doesn’t stay in the executive suite. It travels downstream.

Someone has to attend the meetings, create the deck, track the metrics, and somehow find room for the new initiative alongside everything else leadership approved last quarter and never officially killed.

Eventually, an organization can have 12 “top priorities,” which is corporate code for having no priorities at all.

Saying no is an allocation decision

This is where Buffett’s advice spells leadership discipline. Leadership is partly the job of deciding where those resources will not go.

Research on “initiative overload” found that companies struggle to kill projects even after they no longer support the strategy. Instead, executives keep adding initiatives, creating overload farther down the organization.

More recently, research found essentially the same problem. Many organizations know they have too many projects, yet still struggle to eliminate them, slowing execution and muddying strategic priorities.

I’ve seen a version of this in leadership work for years. Executives talk about focus while rewarding addition. Every new idea gets oxygen because nobody wants to be the person who kills something that might work. That’s not strategy. To me, that looks more like organizational hoarding.

Give your people permission to stop

Here’s a simple little leadership habit that can change the conversation. Every time your leadership team approves a significant new priority, ask: “What are we going to stop doing to make room for this?”

Don’t accept “we’ll figure it out” as an answer. If nothing comes off the table, you haven’t prioritized anything. You’ve simply increased the workload.

This becomes even more important when resources are tight. Research shows that when the workload keeps growing but resources don’t, leaders can’t keep asking people to do more with less. At some point, something has to come off the table.

The better question isn’t always, “How can we get more done?” Sometimes it’s, “Why are we still doing this?”

Have the leadership courage to disappoint people

There’s another reason saying no gets harder as leaders become more successful: More people want something from them. Customers, employees, investors, and partners believe they deserve attention. But you still can’t satisfy them all.

That means a CEO who refuses to disappoint anyone will eventually disappoint almost everyone, because the organization becomes stretched too thin to execute its best ideas exceptionally well.

Buffett’s wisdom, then, isn’t really about becoming someone who walks around saying no all day. It’s really about becoming clear enough about what matters that the answer to everything else becomes easier.

And sometimes the most strategic thing a leader can say is two letters long. No.

—Marcel Schwantes
