As AI Videos Terrify Hollywood, Is It Time To Hand IP Management To The Masses?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Saro McKenna is the CEO of Dacoco GmbH and cofounder of Alien Worlds.

​A viral clip of Brad Pitt and Tom Cruise fighting on a rooftop showed us where the hands have come on the clock with AI-generated content.

The hyperrealistic 15-second clip, created by Irish director Ruairi Robinson using a two-line prompt punched into Seedance 2.0, perfectly illustrated how easy it has become to produce Hollywood-tier content using artificial intelligence. And it raises a pertinent question: Is it time to hand IP management to the community?

Pitt vs. Cruise sparked the latest AI cause célèbre, with Deadpool scriptwriter Rhett Reese predicting (paywall) the tool would “cost jobs all over the place.” Inevitably, users were soon spinning up alternate endings for Game of Thrones and pitting Jackie Chan against Bruce Lee in a dispute over ketchup.

Predictably, Disney sent ByteDance a cease-and-desist letter claiming the Chinese company had hijacked its iconic creations “by reproducing, distributing, and creating derivative works featuring those characters.”

That sound? Pandora’s Box snapping open, and film studios and other stewards of intellectual property (IP) scrambling to preserve their cherished copyrighted works. The press release announcing the launch of Seedance vowed to let users “control their creations like a true director.” But what about other people’s creations?

ByteDance, which owns TikTok and Seedance, promised to strengthen safeguards to “prevent the unauthorized use of intellectual property,” but this reassurance rings hollow.

If ordinary users can access powerful AI tools to produce slick scenes that previously existed only in their imagination, they’ll do it. And they’ll go to great lengths to bring their favorite actors, characters and worlds to life—guardrails be damned. The AI content onslaught will cause headaches for IP stewards everywhere. Seedance 2.0 is one of many powerful tools that can deliver epic visuals at scale, fast. These tools won’t regress, they’ll improve.

As Anthropic co-founder Dario Amodei suggested, “Humanity is about to be handed almost unimaginable power, and it is deeply unclear whether our social, political, and technological systems possess the maturity to wield it.” As access to quality generative AI tools increases, a tsunami of content will be unleashed, and IPs could quickly spiral out of control. Which is why we should think hard about how best to manage and control IP in the AI age.

Communities ought to be empowered to take the IP management reins, not central actors who cannot possibly scale enforcement fast enough to manage, much less contain, the threat.

The fact that Hollywood’s big players were blindsided by Seedance proves just how good these tools are and how ill-equipped the old guard are to defend against them. So why would the community fare better? Well, if literally everyone has the power to create convincing cinematic productions featuring characters from the annals of film, gaming and literature, why wouldn’t you leverage the crowd to manage what’s considered “official”?

It’s analogous to trusting the community to enforce Bitcoin’s consensus rules. There is no executive branch upholding the blockchain’s trustworthiness. Rather, nodes (users) reject blocks that do not meet consensus rules and, in so doing, keep the network healthy. If a collective dissents, they can fork the network (see Bitcoin Cash).

We have come a long way from glitchy prompt outputs to Hollywood-level choreography, lighting and audio, so the idea that studio execs will bury the GenAI genie through cease-and-desist letters and copyright finger-wagging is unrealistic. It’s easier to envisage legal and ethical questions piling up as IP-busting videos roll off the production line.

If we assume that IP can no longer be centrally controlled given the sheer volume of AI content, how would a community-driven system work? Imagine we enter a phase in which the canon of, say, LOTR is contested and the average fan can’t discern real from fake. How do a community of IP stewards deal with that?

As mentioned, Bitcoin provides one possible template: If a fan wished to discern what was official, they could open a block explorer and cross-check the repository, just as users can probe the entire Bitcoin blockchain, locate transaction IDs, etc. Thus, a community could be tasked with enforcing established consensus rules determining what constitutes approved canon.

A community-led IP management model featuring built-in arbitration and on-chain voting could enforce rules without involving a central authority. In this model, contributors would be incentivized to vet work and decide whether it meets the canonicity threshold. Connecting community management to legal IP enforcement, in other words, while entailing a “social rejection” of non-canon activity.

In short, if the community owns the decision-making process, non-canon IP activity isn’t a threat: It’s either absorbed to be exploited legally or rejected, thereby creating a clear process to absorb quality content.

Of course, this just deals with official canon. Arguably, the ability to produce high-quality, copyright-breaking clips detracts from or cheapens an IP, even if viewers acknowledge the new works aren’t “official.”

Beyond the question of official canon lies a thornier issue: outright commercial exploitation. While communities may agree on what constitutes official lore, there’s nothing stopping individuals from wielding AI to generate output featuring famous characters that they later monetize.

When high-quality AI content can be produced cheaply and at scale, the line between fan expression and outright infringement blurs. The challenge isn’t just protecting canon; it’s preventing people from profiteering off supposedly protected IP. But what recourse do IP owners have? Are they relegated to being impotent spectators as an AI-powered imposter mass-produces an endless stream of simulacra, the very existence of which prevents their work from gaining traction?

There are no easy answers. AI is no longer feeding the creative process—it’s attempting a root-and-branch takeover. If we want to maintain some semblance of control and stave off content chaos, we must look to leverage the wisdom of the crowd—humans, not AI agents—and decide our own destiny.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Why Most Enterprise AI Spending Automates The Wrong Part

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Co-Founder of Superlinked, building enterprise-grade open-source inference for production-scale Agentic AI search and document processing.

​After Superlinked narrowed its focus to a single open-source product, I had to rebuild our go-to-market engine with far fewer people. My first move was an obvious one these days: I pointed Claude at our existing outbound process and asked it to write more emails to more people.

It worked, in a way. We did send many more messages, but the total number of replies barely moved. We made the easy part of the process faster. The hard part—knowing who to contact and when—hadn’t changed at all.

Organizational inertia is real, and it’s hard to let go of a well-established and familiar process, but we had to. What follows is how we redesigned the process after the volume experiment failed.​​​

​Companies expect to roughly double AI spending this year to about 1.7% of revenue, according to BCG’s January 2026 survey of 2,360 executives. Yet, in PwC’s “2026 Global CEO” survey, only 12% of CEOs said that “AI has delivered both cost and revenue benefits,” and 56% said they’d “seen no significant financial benefit” so far. And McKinsey analysts reported in April 2026 (registration required) that 60% of respondents to its global survey “still have not seen enterprise-wide EBIT impact from their AI programs.​”​

From both sides of the table (as an operator today and a management consultant previously), I think most of this money buys speed on individual tasks. However, speed on one step rarely changes the outputs of the whole process. The bottleneck just moves somewhere else, and the faster step just piles work up in front of it.​

​When redesigning our outbound process, I focused on the desired outcome rather than which tasks AI could automate. The goal wasn’t to increase email volume but to reach engineers experiencing the specific problems we address, at the right moment.

That reframing exposed a constraint that a small team couldn’t remove. Many of the best buying signals were public: engineers describing their problems in GitHub issues on open-source projects adjacent to ours. Reading thousands of those threads every week wasn’t realistic for a human team, but for an AI agent, it’s routine.

The new process looks nothing like the old one. Agents watch for specific pain signals, match each person to a professional profile and reach out to them with a message tailored to their pain point. An ML engineer fighting memory crashes receives something different from a CTO worried about infrastructure risk. The old process started from a purchased list. The new one starts from evidence of a problem.

The redesign was more successful than I expected. Each response and even each ignore helps us identify which signals indicate genuine interest, improving targeting over time. For example, we initially targeted everyone who starred a relevant repository, but learned that stars indicated curiosity rather than usage. Those who filed issues were more responsive, so we shifted our focus accordingly.

​Process redesign doesn’t mean removing people altogether. It means being deliberate about where they bring most value. My first test is simple: How costly is a mistake, and can it be undone?

Scanning and scoring signals is cheap to get wrong and easy to reverse, so agents run it unsupervised. A first message to a senior leader at a target account is expensive to get wrong, so a person signs off on each one.

My second test often gets less attention than it should. How much effort does it take to check the AI’s work? If verifying an output costs more than producing it by hand, automating that step is a net loss.

Before a workflow goes live, I’m looking to answer four questions. How can the AI agent know it’s unsure? Who does it hand off to? How can we stop or reverse an action? How does a correction feed back into the process so the same mistake doesn’t repeat?

Early on, our pipeline reached out to the people most active in those issue threads. Several turned out to be the project’s own maintainers (often, direct competitors), answering questions rather than asking them. They were the last people we wanted to pitch to.

That incident taught us more about the design than weeks of smooth runs: The system couldn’t tell someone asking for help from the person offering it.

​If your AI investment isn’t (yet) showing in your P&L, avoid purchasing additional tools. Instead, select a critical process and clearly define its intended outcome. Then, redesign the process based on current AI capabilities, rather than sticking to the workflows that you inherited.

Expect the first redesign to be wrong, and keep human supervision where the consequences are significant. Importantly, treat their feedback as a design input rather than a bug to solve. Track how long it takes a person to check the agent’s work. If this time is trending down, the redesign is working.​

The biggest gains I’ve seen came from teams willing to throw the old process away. A faster version of last year’s workflow often produces last year’s results.​

When To Build Your Own AI Model (And When To Stop Kidding Yourself)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Eilon Reshef, cofounder and CPO of Gong, is a seasoned entrepreneur, executive and investor in the internet and software spaces.

We were running our own models at Gong long before ChatGPT became a household name. This was the era of transformer-based language models, the state of the art for natural language understanding before LLMs. We’d take these prebuilt foundation models, train them further on our domain and data and then deploy them for very targeted jobs.​

We called it fine-tuning. Academics called it transfer learning. Most people just called it a lot of work.​

I tell you this because it’s important context for what comes next: the hype around post-trained small language models (SLMs) and their very real practical potential.​

First, an important distinction: This isn’t about training a foundation model from scratch. For most product companies, that would be economic malpractice. It’s about taking an existing model and making it much better at a specific job through supervised fine-tuning, preference optimization, reinforcement learning, distillation or some combination of them.​

Fine-tuning is one form of post-training. I’ll use the broader term when that distinction matters.​

Most major AI providers offer a lineup of models that sits roughly on a diagonal: cheap and fast at one end, more capable and expensive at the other. The names differ, but the trade-offs don’t.​

The promise of post-training is that it lets you move above this line for a specific task. You can get higher quality than an off-the-shelf model at the same inference cost or comparable task quality at a lower cost.​

That doesn’t mean the smaller model is smarter overall, but that specialization can make it better at the specific job you need it to do. The real question is when that advantage holds and what it costs.​

Before we go further, I want to address something I keep hearing in investor conversations and startup pitches: “We fine-tune our own models, so we have a proprietary moat.”​ The reality is, no, you don’t.​

Fine-tuning is an engineering decision. It’s neither magic nor a competitive advantage on its own. Saying “we do post-training” to investors is about as distinctive as saying “we use a database.”​

What’s defensible is what compounds around the model: proprietary data, feedback loops, rigorous evaluation and the application layer, or harness, that connects the model to context, tools, workflows and systems of record.​

The learning system that fine-tuning or a generic agent framework creates is the true competitive moat, refined and improved by real-world use. It captures how work gets done, raises switching costs and makes the underlying model replaceable. Pitch the model work itself as the advantage, and the story eventually falls apart with investors or customers who look closely.​

Knowing when to fine-tune is hard. The decision comes down to four things: scale, task specificity, prompting as the baseline and negotiation leverage.​

Scale is the biggest factor. Post-training carries meaningful upfront costs. At millions of inference calls a month, the economics can work. At thousands, they usually don’t—especially once ongoing maintenance is included.

Task specificity is the next filter. Fine-tuning works best on narrow, repeatable tasks with a high quality bar. Think tagging customer conversations, emails and calls against a defined taxonomy, where the model must understand the domain and return the exact format the system expects.​

Prompting as the baseline means pushing prompt engineering as far as possible before committing to post-training. Strong system prompts, few-shot examples, the right context and strict output specs will go a long way.​ It’s also no longer entirely manual. Automatic optimizers can generate and test variations, raising the baseline before you change a single model weight.​

Too many teams skip this work and go straight to fine-tuning because it feels more “real.” Don’t do that. Exhaust the prompting path first.

Negotiation leverage comes with scale. If you’re big enough to consider fine-tuning, you’re probably big enough to push vendors on price, volume discounts or dedicated capacity. “Should we fine-tune?” may not be the right question. You should probably be asking, “Should we renegotiate our API contract?”

One prerequisite underlies all of this: evaluation. Build a representative gold set with human-approved answers, hold back some for testing and run every prompt revision, model upgrade and fine-tune through the same pipeline. Keep adding production failures and edge cases. Without strong evals, you can’t tell real product improvement from an overfit demo.​

Once the math works, scale is achieved and prompting is exhausted, be sure to understand the operational cost of post-training before committing:

1. The model has to run somewhere. Self-hosted or managed, it’s still more work than calling a frontier API. You now own uptime, scaling, monitoring, hardware, utilization and capacity planning.

2. Models age. A fine-tuned model reflects a snapshot in time. Months later, the base model has improved, data has shifted and new edge cases have appeared.

Post-training isn’t a one-time project. You need to collect new data, rerun evaluations, track drift and retrain as new failure modes emerge. That takes ongoing staffing and investment.

The overlooked cost of SLMs is keeping them competitive.

Cost and quality aren’t everything. Latency, control and privacy can also justify fine-tuning. Know what you’re optimizing for before you make the investment.

The decision shouldn’t come down to cost alone. Be clear about what you’re optimizing for: speed, control, privacy, quality or some combination of them.

Post-training and SLMs are real, powerful tools, but they work best as engineering and business decisions, not marketing stories. The winners usually have scale, strong evaluations, good data and the discipline to keep maintaining the system.

We use both approaches. Some products depend on fine-tuned models, while others are better served by riding frontier improvements. The point is to let the trade-offs decide.

The practical rule is simple: Start broad, measure everything and specialize only when the task is stable and the economics justify it.​

How Much Of Last Quarter’s Code Can Your Team Explain?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Soumendra Mohanty is the Chief Strategy and Innovation Officer at Tredence Inc.

It’s easy enough for large organizations to add agent tooling for their developers and mark the productivity gains. You’re invoiced for generative output; you make the business case based on hours saved. But there’s a second part to that transaction: all the review time that those developers will consume from colleagues whose calendars were already full.

Over the past two years, in particular, I’ve watched some versions of this cycle play out across enterprise delivery organizations. Thanks to the generative power of AI coding capabilities, the volume needing review outstrips the capacity available to provide it. This gets resolved by senior engineers approving changes faster than they can follow them.

As a result, code merges with an assumption that someone understood those changes. Then the cost surfaces months later, when an incident review asks why, to which nobody can provide a good answer. Too often, this is because the decision was made by a model and approved without enough scrutiny.

Agile is designed for a world where the scarce input is engineering hours. Standups, story points and the sprint itself all exist to extract more shipped software from a fixed supply of human time. Agent tooling makes generation hours abundant.

What remains is the number of hours in a week during which an experienced engineer can trace the logic of a change rather than skim it for red flags. Today, that number governs how fast an organization can safely ship. Few planning systems record it anywhere.

• In its 2025 report, Google’s DORA program put AI use among tech professionals near 90%, with about 30% of respondents saying they trust the output little or not at all. The same research found higher AI adoption moving throughput and delivery instability in the same direction.

• Stack Overflow’s 2025 developer survey says two-thirds of developers named “almost right, but not quite” as their leading frustration with AI output; 45% reported that debugging AI-generated code takes them longer than writing it themselves.

• A randomized trial by METR, in which 16 experienced open-source developers worked through 246 real issues, found that it took 19% longer when using AI tools (yet after the experiment, the developers had estimated that AI made them 20% faster).

In a word, adding tooling expands the queue. It’s understandable that, when looking at a review backlog, an executive’s response is to add capacity. In fact, that instinct may compound the problem. Each additional seat raises the volume of change requiring review, while adding nothing to the queue’s ability to perform it.

And of course, the people who can clear that review queue are the ones that most organizations have the fewest of. Senior review time isn’t procured on a quarterly cycle; it doesn’t scale with the licensing budget. Doubling generation capabilities is not the same as doubling review-capable engineers.

So how can organizations build a verification layer that sits inside whatever delivery framework they already run?

Estimate the hours per week your senior engineers can give for careful code review. That is, the kind of review in which engineers follow a change through the system, rather than skimming it for what stands out. Set that figure against the volume of change your teams now produce. Organizations that run this exercise often find a much bigger gap than they expected.

More likely than not, delivery estimates are already part of your planning sessions. Verification capacity belongs right beside them. When the two figures diverge, it reveals a decision about headcount, scope or risk appetite that would otherwise arrive as a later incident.

Uniform review across every change will exhaust the people doing it. Teams under that kind of review tend to reduce review to formality. Instead, review effort should track what breaks when the change is wrong.

Authentication, payment paths, data deletion and anything that touches customer records should earn full manual review by a named owner. Internal tooling and prototypes less so, and they should be labeled clearly enough that they don’t migrate into production unexamined.

Tagging a codebase this way is architecture work. Have an architect assign the tag, store it with the code and treat permissive tagging as the expensive error that it is.

Whatever your current system is, every merged change already carries a name attached. The question is whether that person can explain the logic, side effects and dependencies. Best not to scramble for those answers during a security review, or at three in the morning after an incident. Regulatory exposure resolves to a human being, so your delivery system should be able to show which one and on what basis.

Engineering judgment used to accumulate slowly, through years of writing code and living with the consequences. In the era of agentic AI, that apprenticeship is compressing. Organizations handling it well are putting junior engineers on verification work deliberately, paired with someone who can explain what they’re looking at. Organizations leaving this to chance are drawing on a supply of senior judgment that’s not currently being replenished.

We call this AVID: Agentic Verification and Iterative Delegation. It is a working methodology built around four values:

2. Judgment budgets over unlimited delegation

4. Blast-radius awareness over uniform review

None of these four values requires new tooling or reorganization. Taken together, they take verification from something that happens when there is time to something that appears in the plan. This will be critical in an era where organizations will need to account for what they’ve built. That capacity has to be budgeted like any other.​

What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

If you are curious to learn more about Pathways, you can take a look at the following resources:

Cost per task: Using cheap classifiers to route across Gemini models and thinking levels

[$] Git considers voting to determine its LLM policy

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

One of the dominant themes of 2026 has been the debate within development projects about the terms under which LLM-written contributions should be accepted — if, indeed, they should be accepted at all. The Git project has not been immune to this trend; the topic was discussed at the recent Git Contributor's Summit. In this case, the path to a resolution may be somewhat circuitous; the project, which has never been governed as a democracy, is considering adopting a voting process as a way of bringing the discussion to an acceptable conclusion.

What are you doing this week?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

What are you doing this week? Feel free to share!

Keep in mind it’s OK to do nothing at all, too.

I'm organizing a programmer meetup, Chilean style, in Santiago. We are going to have a nice barbecue outdoors (it's summer here!) and I'm expecting about 30 attendees. Looking forward to it :)

I wrote up a list of all the projects I started this year and turns out I started almost exactly one every month. Didn't finish any of them. For the rest of the year, I am going to focus on polishing and shipping all the ones I still find interesting.

Starting with emmer (a Wayland notification daemon, inspired by Sonner).

How is WoW? I haven't played in a very long time (and I'm hesitant to try again, I need to protect myself lol).

So far I'm having a blast. I played from Classic to WotLK and then I dabbled in the other expansions. Forever feels like Classic but with a lot of QoL improvements. It's slow, large and a great excuse to be unproductive (after the last months have given me productivity anxiety).

Going back to tanking dead mines with randoms has been a great experience.

Dinner with a friend on Wednesday. Going to Cambridge for the Retro Computer Festival 2026 at the Centre for Computing History on Saturday & Sunday.

As my work has continued to push LLMs on me (and my promotion has not materialized nor disappeared yet) I am going to write an MCP server for Citrix. My replacement will surely make use of LLMs and it would be nice if I could simply tell them to point their agents at an MCP server to get good diagnostics / other information.

Celebrating another revolution of the sun completed. New toys include a new waterproof coat, (new old stock), Wayfarer mk2 sailing dinghy (secondhand but ready to go) and iPad mini (new, so thin! Nano-texture!)

Off to see a live show of the Smith n Sniff podcast, and hopefully getting the Z4 back from having an exhaust blow cut out and new pipes welded in, in time for a trackday at Donnington Park on Friday. Not done a trackday before (taking the Focus estate round the Nurburgring excepted) and quite looking forward to it, but also somewhat nervous.

I've been writing and editing documentation for the GoActivityPub library. If anyone has 10m to kill and read through it, I'd appreciate some feedback (and owe you one <3).

Got some miniPCIe LTE modems that my workplace was throwing out, so I'm waiting on a SIM card (on a cheap 19 DKK/month PAYG plan) and USB adapter to arrive. First task is to figure out which of them still work, and after that I'm not sure. I don't have anything that urgently needs cellular connectivity.

I'm also going to be moving my home network back to my own router (a N100 or N150—I can't remember—mini PC with 2 NICs and 4 ethernet ports running OpenBSD). After my recent move, it was easier to just use the ISP router until I had some time to clear the space I need under the stairs. I still don't have ethernet everywhere, but my wifi mesh is good enough for most things.

I have another project turning my Marantz PM6005 amplifier into a smart speaker, using an ESP32, a tinkerboard, raspotify, and home assistant. At the moment it communicates over IR, but I plan to hook the ESP32 up to the remote control RCA jack at the rear so it doesn't need line of sight any more (right now the biggest blocker is a literal one, a spanish galgo that likes to stand right between the ESP32 and the IR receiver). I'm not sure whether I'll bother with much of a write up for this, the tinkerboard, raspotify and home assistant stuff is fairly standard, and the ESP32 firmware is mostly vibecoded, so not that interesting to read about. I know many here are skeptical and highly critical of LLMs, but I've been finding them more and more useful. As someone who has struggled with executive function all my life, they've actually allowed me to get projects done that I would have in the past indefinitely postponed.

Found an index optimization few days ago that largely eliminates pointer chasing, so I've been updating the buffer pool with io_uring to make use of that. Getting io_uring to actually deliver the performance that Patterson, Gimlin et al. suggests is pretty hard in real world scenarios where IOPS go brr is not the only outcome you're looking for.

Next is actually adjusting the index to make use of the async fetching capabilities.

I moved one of my internal tools for my company to emacs/elisp. I live in emacs so it makes sense to replace some webapps only used by me with buffers and completing-read

Looking for engineering jobs in Amsterdam area, and maybe spend some time to polish my application launcher.

This week I'm polishing CabFuse, a small utility I built to bring tabs to Windows 10 File Explorer.

I originally started it to solve some everyday annoyances with File Explorer, and I've been using it myself ever since. It's been especially rewarding to see other people finding it useful too.

Pursuantly, I'll be generalising my implementation of fibers into one of effects after a lightbulb moment. Fibers share a storage location for the yield target, but if you make that a parameter, you get effects. So it's analogous to a labeled break. I think that's how effects are supposed to work? Seems to be similar to how OCaml does it anyway. Also simultaneously halfway through rewriting the parser (said Sisyphus).

Horrible public key tricks

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

So, I've always been somewhat annoyed at SSL/TLS. It requires everyone to go through a complex "ACME" dance just to put up a public website. Worse, certificates expire every few months, so it's not a one and done thing: even with automation, maintaining a certificate requires care to ensure the ACME client always has access to DNS records or the web server.

... and the whole system doesn't even work because certificates are issued over plaintext connections, and trusted certificate aurthorities use very secure keys like "4"

There is prior art of protest SSL implementations, but I do actually want to encrypt web traffic. Passive eavesdropping is a real risk... but preventing it doesn't require the whole certificate thing: therefore, the best choice is a protest certificate.

The only part of the certificate I directly control is the public key. Therefore, the simplest way to obtain a bad certificate is use a vanity private key such as 313333333337 (one of the most eleet primes). However, while the resulting public key would be week, no one would know unless they actually try to break it.

# openssl s_client -connect maurycyz.com:443 < /dev/null 2> /dev/null \ | sed -n /BEGIN/,/END/p | grep -zE '////.?*////' --color=yes -----BEGIN CERTIFICATE----- MIIFADCCA+igAwIBAgISBjst/cQvgUTu+O7siJYo8bAFMA0GCSqGSIb3DQEBCwUA MDMxCzAJBgNVBAYTAlVTMRYwFAYDVQQKEw1MZXQncyBFbmNyeXB0MQwwCgYDVQQD EwNZUjIwHhcNMjYxMTA4MjE0NzM5WhcNMjcwMjA2MjE0NzM4WjAXMRUwEwYDVQQD EwxtYXVyeWN5ei5jb20wggEiMA0GCSqGSIb3DQEBAQUAA4IBDwAwggEKAoIBAQCz lkaPgD2IZPPtC6fB////You+dont+actually+have+to+pick+random+privat e+keys//No+one+checks+these+things//A+certificate+aurthority+got +away+with+the+number+4+for+a+year////8HzhFiAzSv/zKHf7l4u6d6d3w6 nOg7N/C2ra5QvNpFnZVzrS5IcZmvRdi0dcptavcB8s4ThHNZLhY414Mvdo5t0kr0 n8WFrydnc6NEMKDPiZxehnF5D3n7z1piI5YCsNcS0NVFb45+8QvBZrv0O2BSIKNE 7M9hcE00KsaSzZ3eDBsRAgMBAAGjggIoMIICJDAOBgNVHQ8BAf8EBAMCBaAwEwYD VR0lBAwwCgYIKwYBBQUHAwEwDAYDVR0TAQH/BAIwADAdBgNVHQ4EFgQUZoonXxel KC563vN2kbvwXTLBQgMwHwYDVR0jBBgwFoAUQBUtJnntMiCe35pyHdYyH4EMgQww MwYIKwYBBQUHAQEEJzAlMCMGCCsGAQUFBzAChhdodHRwOi8veXIyLmkubGVuY3Iu b3JnLzAnBgNVHREEIDAegg4qLm1hdXJ5Y3l6LmNvbYIMbWF1cnljeXouY29tMBMG A1UdIAQMMAowCAYGZ4EMAQIBMC0GA1UdHwQmMCQwIqAgoB6GHGh0dHA6Ly95cjIu Yy5sZW5jci5vcmcvNi5jcmwwggELBgorBgEEAdZ5AgQCBIH8BIH5APcAdgAAGl0a HC2TdbZIVXj4L3Ghrm7vOX0pfIrjFXvK3uGgHgAAAaG9Vy0ZAAAEAwBHMEUCIEof iI14E0jzfOz9woGKiiYR4pUAm/Dv9FmFWEr5ma8uAiEAyl2EMpURJ65Oe+FIFE6v Uj4QomzFJ98qVqhQozQxcC4AfQAai51rjdeR0c0FSe22A1XWBrZPrTDbcf54jw/H yPvEsQAAAaG9Vy2AAAgAAAUAFVVRHQQDAEYwRAIgTBocYGR/XcTqNx58xJh/pAme q+w6fDl+NktKUuPBqZcCIFJU6/GE71s9OkORDBC4+XHiQWWxrHk8uuD6nTQzCtHS MA0GCSqGSIb3DQEBCwUAA4IBAQAUIK5MyB3bhSFc6NOkC1UYYi2BQZN4zDfZMTD1 gGtnK7FCCNKvsanv9FIeGgr7e3A50TJhqIWH42uz9mQt8rnofwKVu7fJ77l/HZIf oAqNol06AbtwM2I4vOEAZyfD3zAfL/3G70rGw4omDFpzX852Yi3Ou40wpHbdboYY Bn58bZDvBVaUrKVdTdN+S5Ea2usWKgUjqfouz/VjnlEgLfcxqPwF2IPfPCOPKJH2 uIo3tnjx267OWY3O8nyxuOAxrBuP5fc8g2bEcKI5RewRWh7CCVZB23Ov5dyFQVcb ICN41m494GUBgwqYpAjLgdIubvyoJzBpACGuIl2QlJza1p/y -----END CERTIFICATE-----

First, let's look at mathematical framework of RSA. To generate a key, pick two large prime numbers (p and q) and multiply them to yeild the modulus (n). Pick a small number, e as public exponent.

The RSA public key is composed of the modulus (n) and the public exponent:

# Encryption requires knowing e (public) cyphertext = plaintext^e mod n

The private key contains the modulus and the private exponent (d) which is chosen to be the multiplicate inverse of e modulo n. Exactly how d is found is not relevent, but the process requires knowing p and q.

# Decryption requires knowing d (pivate) = cyphertext^d mod n = plaintext^e^d mod n = plaintext^(e*d) mod n = plaintext^1 mod n = plaintext

Ok, back to vanity keys. The standard value for e is 65537 (0x10001), because the small number of set bits makes encryption faster. In fact, the CA I used will refuse to sign keys with a non-standard public exponent, so I can't do anything funny there.

The modulus, n, is either 2,048 or 4,096 "user controlled" bits, so it's the ideal place to hide a message. However, to use the certificate, I'll need to find two large primes that multiply out to the vanity modulus.

My method starts by picking two random primes and calculating the resulting n value. Then, the bytes of n are modified to introduce message into the base64 encoding. Of course, the modified n is not the product of p and q, but this is fixable by setting q to n/p.

The resulting q value is very unlikely to be a prime (or integer), so I round it and find the next prime after the ideal q: which usually only requires changing the value of q by a few hundred. Since p and q are similar in size, a small change q only affects the least-sigifiant half of the product:

58174124 * 918491824 = 53432457262362176 (incrementing until the next prime...) 58174124 * 918491843 = 53432458367670532

As long as the message is embedded into the upper half of n, it's likely to survive undamaged. The original p and newly selected q can now be used to construct a functional RSA private key in the usual manner.

From here, it's really just a matter of getting a certificate aurthority to sign the key:

# Single domain openssl req -new -key priv.pem -subj '/C=US/CN=example.com' > cert_request certbot certonly --manual --csr cert_request # Wildcard cat > config< cert_request certbot certonly --manual --csr cert_request ... although since base64 operates on groups of three byte, it is possible for the message to be misaligned and not readable. However, since the layout of the certificate is static, it is possible to adjust the message's placement until it works: this should take less than four attempts.

The result is a functional, trusted certificate with a longish message hidden in the public key. Renewal is done by resumbitting the CSR and repeating the ACME chalange, which can be automated.

Base64 is missing most punctuation, but it provides the full ASCII alphabet, and the ~150 (RSA-2048) or ~320 (RSA-4096) avalable charaters provides plenty of space for jokes. Another option would be to target the base16 displayed in most browser's certificate viewer: this would provide a lot more space (2 chars/byte instea of 0.75), but requires careful word choice.

As for security, this trick obviously reduces the entropy in the private key. I'm not aware of any factoring algorithms that could exploit key selection method, but I can't garantee they don't exist. The method may also produce a p and q that are close together, which makes it possible to easly factor n and recover the private key. A very small d value can also allow factorisation.

While I wouldn't trust these keys for anything important, they should be perfectly fine for serving a public website: modern TLS provides forward secrecy, so eavesdropping requires man-in-the-middle even if the private key is compromised.

How Much Energy Does Building Distro Packages Use

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

When I set up the infrastructure for Casuarina Linux I repurposed an AMD AM4 system with RyzenÂ 9Â 5950X 16-Core CPU (105W TDP) and 32Gb of DDR4 RAM as the build server. I set it up in our laundry, connected it via a smart plug, and reset its counters. Over the course of the project I would regularly pull package updates from Chimera Linux, build and install them locally, and then push them to the repo, which would trigger Buildbot to build and publish them.

You can select a different currency, which will use exchange rates from the time of writing.

The project ran from 30 March 2026 to 8 November 2026 (a bit over 7 months) the total energy used was 98.4 kWh. My electricity plan is a 100% renewable plan (meaning they offset the non-renewable parts) and costs AU$0.33 kWh. Ignoring the daily service fee the cost of that electricity is AU$32.47 .

Since it turned out I was the only one doing package updates I kept the builder off when idle. When there were packages to build Iâd hit the power button (on the machine, not the smart plug) and some time later when it was done it press it again to shut it down. This was pretty beneficial as the idle draw was about 55W.

The number of package builds was around 5000. SELECT COUNT(name) FROM logs WHERE name LIKE 'pkg:%'; from the Buildbot state.sqlite yields 5201, however thatâs probably slightly inflated due to rebuilds/packages that failed to build on the first try.

The actual usage is likely more than double the 98kWh figure as all packages were built on my desktop machine first, before pushing to the repository for the builder to pick up. That machine is an AM5 system with AMD Ryzen 9 9950X3D 16-Core CPU (170W TDP), 64Gb DDR5 RAM. Additionally our house has rooftop solar, and where possible I would kick off builds during the day so there was a better chance of using the solar. That would have reduced the actual cost, but I have no way to measure the specific amount.

The slowest package by far to build was Chromium, taking just shy of 4 hours to complete. Power draw measured by the smart plug when the builder was fully loaded is about 193W. So 4 hours at 193W is 0.77kWh, which means a Chromium build costs about 25 cents.

Overall it cost a bit under AU$5 a month (ignoring solar, and my workstation) to build all the packages for a Linux distribution. Thatâs not too bad I think.

I also publish new posts as emails in my newsletter.
